About this document

This independent guide describes the S.O. Multi-usuario Version 6.2 operating system for the Spanish-made Secoinsa Serie 20 computer. This operating system may be the earliest known surviving copy of a Spanish-language operating system: its command names, messages and help text date from 1981 and are of historical importance.

This document exists because, at the time of publication, no surviving set of technical manuals for this machine's operating system is known. The guide is the product of reverse engineering a surviving Secoinsa Serie 20 computer and examining the behaviour and implementation of its operating system. It is an independent reconstruction, not a reprint of a manufacturer manual.

Original product names, command names and messages retain their historical spelling. Their inclusion does not imply manufacturer authorship or endorsement of this edition. No rights in the operating-system software are granted by this document.

Preface

Secoinsa (Sociedad Española de Comunicaciones e Informática, S.A.) was founded in 1975 as part of Spain's effort to build a domestic computer industry. Its founding partners were the Instituto Nacional de Industria (INI), the state's industrial holding company, together with Telefónica and Fujitsu. The idea was to combine Spanish design and manufacturing with technology from abroad. Fujitsu gave the company international industrial ties and invested in its factory in Málaga.

Much of Secoinsa's technical background came from Telesincro, a Spanish electronics firm founded in 1963 that was already building business computers well before Secoinsa existed. Its Winner/Contafac billing machine was followed by the Factor Q and Factor R, and then in the early 1970s by the programmable Factor S. All of these were built for accounting, invoicing and everyday office work. Secoinsa negotiated majority control of Telesincro in 1975 and completed the purchase in 1976. Telesincro kept its own name and products within the group, bringing with it an established product range and long experience supplying business equipment.

Secoinsa's own range drew on both Telesincro and Fujitsu designs. One of its machines was the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer, a compact business system built around terminals introduced in the early 1980s. It was supplied with Secoinsa IS 9502 terminals, each with a keyboard and a green monochrome screen showing 24 lines of 80 characters. For the people using it, the terminal was effectively the computer: commands, programs and file contents all appeared there as text.

After a series of commercial struggles, including a failed move into personal and educational computers in 1984–85 with the Secoinsa-branded version of Fujitsu's FM-7, the company agreed in 1986 to merge with Fujitsu España, which took it over along with all its assets and obligations.

Very little of Secoinsa's work in computers seems to have survived. Its machines were built for offices, banks and public bodies, not for homes, and when they became obsolete they were usually scrapped rather than kept. Few examples of most models are known to survive, and the software and documentation is in an even worse state. Only a handful of brochures, technical descriptions and official records are known to exist.

For a company at the centre of Spain's attempt to build its own computer industry, that is a very small record, and every recovered machine, manual or listing is worth documenting before it too disappears.

The historical value of this Secoinsa Serie 20 computer preservation lies in the complete working environment that includes its rare operating system. The Z80 CPU-based system described here combines several terminal users, banked memory, structured business files and an integrated BASIC environment. Explaining how those facilities work together preserves a part of Spanish computing history that a company chronology or hardware specification alone cannot convey.

This document teaches and describes the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer's operating system "S.O. Multi-usuario", its Spanish command names and file notation, and everyday sequences such as connect to the system, access files, do basic tasks, and session disconnection. Later chapters turn the same operations into command files and introduce BASIC programming. Technical chapters explain how these visible operations reach the executive and devices.

How to use this document

Start with Chapters 1–5 if this is your first session. Chapter 6 introduces file operations. Chapter 7 covers text editing and running compiled BASIC; Chapter 8 introduces EXEC command files from a first greeting through variables and loops. Chapter 9 covers accounts and messages. Work through the examples in order; later examples use files created earlier. Ask for an ordinary practice account and enough privilege to use EDITAR and BASIC before working through those examples.

Administrators should continue with Chapters 10–14. Programmers can move from the file-operation examples to Chapters 15–19: a first BASIC program, values and control flow, file I/O, Z80 machine code, then system services and BASIC extensions. Chapters 20–30 explain the system at increasing depth. Chapters 31–35 provide command, service, error, device and limit references, followed by command spelling and the separate editor, EXEC and query vocabularies. Emulation in MAME, after the Index, provides instructions for emulating and booting the system in MAME. The appendices collect program formats, hardware maps, system-file explanations, BASIC vocabulary and binary structures.

Entering examples

A code block headed by an instruction to enter contains exactly the text to type. Press Return after each command. Do not type a prompt unless the instructions explicitly say it is part of the input. The command processor displays >, EDITAR displays *, and BASIC displays -. Check the prompt before entering a command: BASIC's RUN and the command processor's RUN are different operations.

Each introductory command is presented separately. Read the paragraph after it before continuing. Longer program listings are complete examples to enter one numbered line at a time; the accompanying explanation accounts for every instruction.

Names such as NOTE.TEXT:S, COPY.TEXT:S and HELLO.BASICOBJ:S are practice names. Check that a name is unused before creating it. S is a logical disk association, not a directory. The examples require an accessible, writable S association; ask the administrator which association to use if yours differs.

Syntax notation

Notation Meaning
LISDIR Literal command name
name.type:drive Replace these fields with your file specification
[item] Optional item; do not type square brackets
item... Repetition permitted by that command
(VERIFI) Literal parentheses and option keyword; type them
A or B in prose Choose the stated alternative; do not type the word “or”
0x1234 Hexadecimal number in the explanation
08h Hexadecimal constant in assembly notation
Ctrl-X Hold Control and press X
Escape, Q Press Escape, release it, then press Q

Quotation marks in BASIC are literal. General CSI (command processor aka system prompt) quoting and command continuation are not assumed. Use full command names while learning. Chapter 5 explains command names and the limitations of abbreviations.

A warning before a maintenance example identifies a real change or destructive operation. Read its prerequisites before typing. Reference examples involving device A require an administrator to establish what A denotes. They are not instructions to format an arbitrary connected disk.

Part I — The system

Chapter 1 — Meet the operating system

S.O. Multi-usuario Version 6.2 is a multiuser operating system for the Z80-based Secoinsa Serie 20 computer. You work by typing commands at a terminal. The system replies with text, performs the requested operation and normally returns to a prompt for the next command.

The command processor is called CSI (command processor aka system prompt). It understands command names such as AYUDA, LISDIR and COPIFI. Most commands are programs loaded when requested. Learning these names is more useful than translating commands from another operating system: the punctuation, options and file rules belong to this system.

Your terminal, account and partition

Your account identifies your files and privileges. Your partition holds a running program, its memory, device assignments and connection state. Several partitions can operate at once. The executive switches among them so that one user can work while another user's program runs.

These terms describe different things. Changing account changes identity; stopping a partition stops an execution context. Logging out ends your connection. None of those actions means that the whole machine has shut down.

What you can do

The system provides file listing, text editing, copying, printing, account maintenance and storage administration. EXEC command files automate sequences of commands. BASIC supplies an editor, interpreter, compiler and runtime. Machine-code programs can call executive services for console input, files, devices and scheduling.

Files use a two-part name, such as NOTE.TEXT, followed when necessary by a disk association: NOTE.TEXT:S. There is no hierarchical directory path to learn. Ownership and disk associations determine which files a command can find.

What the version means

The operating system identifies itself as Version 6.2. CSI (command processor aka system prompt) and EXEC have their own 5.5 version numbers, and BASIC S20 has a 1.0 version number. A component number need not match the operating-system number. This document retains the Spanish names and messages you will encounter at the terminal, while explaining their operation in English.

Begin with startup in Chapter 3. You do not need to understand memory banking or executable formats to list a file. The next chapter provides a map of the system so that those details have a place when you reach them.

Chapter 2 — Architecture at a glance

The terminal, command environment and storage work together through the operating system. A command such as LISDIR begins as text typed at a terminal. CSI selects the command program, that program asks the operating system for file information, and the result appears on the terminal. This chapter identifies the components along that path; their memory addresses and internal interfaces come later.

Figure 2.1. From startup to command execution. A device definition does not by itself establish that the corresponding hardware is fitted.

Starting the operating system

Firmware is the initial program held in the computer's read-only memory. It checks the hardware and reads a small disk program called the bootstrap. The bootstrap loads SISTEMA.NUCLEO, the operating system's executive. SISTEMA.STARTUP supplies further initialization code. It is an executable component, not a text file of commands for the operator to edit.

The executive remains responsible for coordinating execution and access to devices. Once initialization and account connection have completed, a user normally reaches CSI's command prompt. Chapter 3 explains that user-visible sequence; Chapter 21 describes the bootstrap and initialization in detail.

Accounts, partitions and shared work

An account identifies the user and the files that user can access. A partition provides a running environment, including memory and terminal assignments. The executive switches between partitions so that several users can make progress on the same computer.

Part of memory holds routines shared by the partitions. Other parts are selected as different programs run. This memory arrangement is called banking. It lets the system use more memory than the processor can address at one instant. You do not need to choose banks to list or edit a file; the memory arrangement is explained in Chapter 20.

Commands, languages and system services

CSI reads the command line and selects the requested program. Most utilities are separate files of type COMANDO. Thus EDITAR and COPIFI are programs that CSI starts, rather than editing and copying operations built into the prompt itself.

BASIC provides its own editor, interpreter, compiler and runtime for BASIC programs. EXEC reads command files that automate command-level work. Each has its own instructions and input context. The prompt tells you which component is currently accepting input; Chapter 4 teaches that distinction before you begin entering programs.

Programs request common operations through system services: displaying characters, opening files or obtaining information about the current account. The executive carries out those requests and returns the appropriate result. Chapter 19 introduces the programmer's calling interface; Chapter 24 explains its internal dispatch mechanism.

Files and devices

The filesystem connects a file's name to its ownership, organization and stored contents. Utilities use it to find the data they need. A name alone does not determine how those contents should be interpreted: ordinary text, BASIC source and compiled programs require different operations.

A logical device name gives a program a way to select a device without naming its hardware connections. For example, S selects the system disk association and IMPR1 selects a printer association. The administrator connects logical names to physical device definitions. A driver then supplies the hardware-specific operations. This separation lets a program use an assigned printer without knowing its controller ports.

Chapter 5 introduces the file notation seen by users. Chapter 11 and Chapter 12 explain storage and device administration; Chapter 23 develops the underlying storage and device model. Later chapters distinguish an operation accepted by software from data actually written or printed, which matters when diagnosing incomplete output.

Chapter 3 — Starting, connecting and leaving

Begin with the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer powered and configured by its administrator. During startup, ROM diagnostics precede Carga del S.O.—“loading the operating system”—and the system banner. Wait for the account prompt; do not type commands while loading is in progress.

Connect to your account

The system asks:

Nombre de cuenta a conectarse:

The administrative account used in this guide is SISTEMA. Type its name and press Return:

SISTEMA

SISTEMA is the real administrative account, not a practice account to create. For ordinary work, try to use an account with fewer privileges. Login masks password input. A successful connection normally reaches the CSI prompt, >. An account can instead start a command file automatically, so a menu after login can be normal.

Make your first enquiry

At >, enter:

AYUDA

AYUDA means “help.” With no topic it displays the command summary. If the display pauses, press Return to continue. You have asked a question of the system without changing a file.

Now ask about one command:

AYUDA LISDIR

This displays help for LISDIR, the directory-listing command. You will use that command in the following chapters. The distinction matters: AYUDA LISDIR describes LISDIR; LISDIR performs a listing.

AYUDA normally displays a matching topic. If lookup fails, it can invoke the named utility with AYUD as its argument. AYUDA BORRAR follows this fallback and reports Falta tipo del fichero instead of displaying help. Use the BORRAR reference for deletion instructions; do not assume that every topic request is a passive lookup.

Finally, check your identity:

ESTADO QUIEN

ESTADO reports a selected part of system state; QUIEN selects the current identity. Unlike AYUDA, ESTADO requires a subject. Entering ESTADO alone produces a syntax error.

Finish a session

Save your work and leave the application using its own completion command. At the CSI prompt, enter:

FIN

FIN disconnects the account and returns to the login path. It is a logout command. It does not promise to close another program's unfinished output or power down the whole machine.

Restart and interruption

Escape followed by Q requests interruption. The application's current handler determines when and how it responds; return to CSI need not be immediate. Prefer the application's own exit command and check which prompt returns. Forced interruption can leave incomplete output. Chapter 7 explains the pending-interruption behavior in EDITAR.

Escape followed by I asks Desea IPL (S/N) -. S confirms a restart; N declines the restart but terminates the interrupted command rather than resuming it. Neither response is a save operation. Restart affects other partitions as well. Chapter 14 discusses maintenance and the limits of the shutdown interface.

Part II — Using the system

Chapter 4 — Using the terminal

A terminal sends characters to the computer and displays characters returned by it. The current prompt tells you which program is reading those characters. Before typing, check whether you are at CSI (>), EDITAR (*) or BASIC (-).

The Secoinsa Serie 20 computer was paired with the Secoinsa IS 9502 computer terminal, with a separate keyboard and a green monochrome screen displaying 24 lines of 80 characters. The terminal sends keystrokes to the computer; CSI, the editor or the running program decides what those keystrokes mean. The keyboard and display do not form a separate computer running this operating system. The Preface gives the historical hardware context.

Enter and correct a line

At CSI, type the following, then press Return:

AYUDA

Return submits the line. The terminal connection uses carriage return, character 0x0D, for this purpose. If you mistype a character before submitting, Backspace or Delete removes the preceding character. Ctrl-X clears the whole input line so that you can start again. Escape followed by lowercase c also cancels the current CSI input line.

CSI normally folds lowercase letters to uppercase. Use uppercase letters and digits for practice filenames. Underscore is unsuitable here: the CSI input path can treat it as Delete. BASIC quoted strings have their own rules, so do not infer their behavior from CSI input editing.

Key CSI operation
Return Submit the current line; continue a paginated listing
Backspace or Ctrl-H Erase the preceding character
Delete Erase the preceding character
Ctrl-X Discard the current input line
Escape, lowercase c Cancel current input line

Ctrl-Z is not a CSI previous-command recall key. Physical BREAK has no sufficiently specified general operation in this guide. BASIC's BREAK command is a separate debugging facility.

Pause, resume and restore output

Escape followed by Q requests interruption through the application's current handler. It is not a universal immediate exit. EDITAR can retain the request until a later operation; use its completion command and check for the CSI prompt. See Chapter 7.

Escape followed by S pauses the requesting partition. Press the same pair again to resume. Do not type the rest of a command while paused: ordinary queued input can be discarded on resumption. Other partitions continue running.

Escape followed by O toggles ordinary output and keyboard echo. The program can keep running while its display is suppressed. If the terminal becomes silent after that control, repeat it to restore output. This differs from both a pause and an interrupt.

Recognize a terminal setup problem

If commands are accepted but cursor movement or screen layout is wrong, the system may be using the wrong terminal class, the component that translates screen controls for that terminal. If characters are garbled, the communication settings may differ. Report the symptom to the administrator rather than changing terminal settings while learning the command language.

If rapid pasted input loses characters, stop sending, clear the partial command and enter it again at a slower rate. The administrator should check flow control and the terminal's communication settings. A correct screen display does not by itself guarantee reliable sustained input.

The normal console dimensions are L79/P23. Leave them unchanged: reducing L can shorten the amount of input accepted on a command line, as well as affecting display behavior. Restore output with Escape–O if it was suppressed, and distinguish that condition from a communication fault.

Chapter 12 explains communication settings, terminal classes and their configuration. Chapter 28 explains how terminal controls reach the device.

Chapter 5 — Commands, filenames and disks

The command line tells CSI what to do and which object to use. Start by reading a concrete example from left to right:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

LISDIR is the operation. NOTE is the filename's first field. TEXT is its type field. The dot separates those fields. The suffix :S selects the system volume through its logical disk association, S. In these examples, read it as ‘on the system disk’: NOTE.TEXT:S means the file NOTE.TEXT on that volume. This command asks for the catalogue entry for one file; it does not display the file's contents.

S is a logical disk name, not the literal volume label SISTEMA. SISTEMA is also the administrative account name and the first field of many system filenames. A storage volume holds files; a partition in this operating system is a memory and execution environment, explained in Chapter 9. The :S suffix selects storage, not an execution partition.

Each filename field has at most eight characters. Use uncomplicated uppercase names such as NOTE.TEXT and HELLO.BASICOBJ while learning. A type is a name, not a guarantee of contents: naming a file BASICOBJ does not turn arbitrary text into a BASIC program.

List several files

Enter:

LISDIR *.*:S

The first asterisk matches names; the second matches types. This requests the files visible to your account on S. Ownership can hide another account's private files. An empty result therefore does not mean the entire volume is empty.

To narrow the selection, enter:

LISDIR SISTEMA.*:S

This selects the name SISTEMA with any type. The system uses that family for components such as SISTEMA.CSI and SISTEMA.AYUDA. Appendix C explains those components individually.

The question-mark wildcard can match a position in a listing pattern, but wildcard support is command-specific. Do not use a listing pattern in a copying or maintenance command unless that command accepts it.

Add an option

Enter:

LISDIR *.*:S (SORT)

Parentheses enclose options. SORT requests a sorted listing. These parentheses are literal characters, unlike the square brackets used to mark optional material in syntax descriptions.

Drive S is an association maintained by the system. A–G can designate other disks. They are not subdirectories. Omitting the drive can select a work unit that has not been mounted; spelling :S explicitly makes the destination explicit in these examples.

Read completion messages

A command may finish with CR = n, a time and TE = ... minutos.. CR is its completion result; TE is elapsed time. CR 0 reports command success. Read preceding messages too: a checker can finish successfully while reporting filesystem problems.

A syntax error usually calls for checking the command name, spaces, colon and parentheses before trying again. A file-not-found message calls for checking the name, disk association and current account. Chapter 33 separates command results from BASIC errors and system message numbers.

File organizations

Organization What it is for
S Sequential text or serialized records
SR Sequential storage with read protection
D Fixed-length records selected by number
I Keyed records with an ordered index
K Keyed records without the I-format ordered index
R Relocatable machine-code programs
A Programs placed at absolute addresses

You will normally choose a program appropriate to the file, rather than manipulate these letters directly. LISTAR displays sequential text; BASIC loads its own saved source; RUN executes compiled BASIC. Their internal formats are explained after the programming chapters.

Full names and abbreviations

This document uses full command names. With standard abbreviation enabled, most listed commands can also be shortened to their specified minimum prefix; intermediate prefixes are accepted. For example, ASOCIAR can be entered as ASO, ASOC, ASOCI, ASOCIA or ASOCIAR. They request the same association operation. ASIGNAR is a different command: its short forms include A and AS, which do not select ASOCIAR.

The minimum is defined separately for each command. It is not always three letters, and some command names must be written in full. There are two important exceptions to the intermediate-prefix rule: B selects BASIC but BA selects BACKUP, while BAS and BASI select BASIC; EXEC must always be written in full because EX and EXE can select the wrong command file. Use the full names until these distinctions are familiar.

Options have their own spelling rules. In ESTADO MEMORIA, ESTADO is the command and MEMORIA selects the information to display. MEM is a permitted abbreviation of that option even when command-name abbreviation is disabled. A full keyword is the system's accepted spelling, which may itself be a shortened Spanish word; do not extend it into an assumed longer word.

Chapter 35 lists the full names, permitted short forms and settings. It also explains exceptions caused by synonyms, filenames and option-name collisions.

Chapter 6 — Your first file

This chapter creates a small text file and takes it through the ordinary file operations. Use a practice account that may run EDITAR and write files on S. First make sure that NOTE.TEXT, COPY.TEXT and OTHER.TEXT are not names you already use.

1. Check the practice names

Enter:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

This asks whether NOTE.TEXT is visible on S. If it already exists, choose a different unused name and substitute it consistently below. A file you recognize as your own is not expendable merely because its name matches an example.

2. Start the editor

Enter:

EDITAR NOTE.TEXT:S

EDITAR opens a text-editing session for that name. Its prompt is *. You are now giving instructions to the editor rather than CSI.

Enter:

CASE U

This selects uppercase handling for the text you are about to enter.

Enter:

INPUT MY FIRST FILE

INPUT adds the words following it as a line of file text. INPUT itself is an editor instruction and is not part of that text.

Enter:

FILE

FILE saves the text and leaves EDITAR. Wait for the CSI > prompt.

3. Display what you saved

Enter:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:S

LISTAR displays the file. Its text includes MY FIRST FILE; headings depend on listing options. Compare this with LISDIR: LISTAR shows contents, whereas LISDIR shows the catalogue entry.

4. Copy the file

Check the proposed destination first:

LISDIR COPY.TEXT:S

If it already exists, choose another unused practice name. With the intended new name available, enter:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:S COPY.TEXT:S (VERIFI)

The first specification is the source. The second is the destination. COPIFI creates a separate copy; VERIFI requests verification. Do not reverse the two specifications.

Inspect the destination:

LISTAR COPY.TEXT:S

Its text should agree with NOTE.TEXT. The original is still available under its original name.

5. Rename the copy

Check the proposed new name:

LISDIR OTHER.TEXT:S

If it is unused, enter:

ATRIB COPY.TEXT:S OTHER.TEXT:S (NOCONSUL)

ATRIB changes the catalogue name. NOCONSUL suppresses the consultation prompt for this one-file example. The stored data is retained under OTHER.TEXT; this is not another copy.

Check the new name:

LISDIR OTHER.TEXT:S

You should now find the renamed entry. Use OTHER.TEXT for the next step, because COPY.TEXT is no longer its name.

6. Remove the practice copy

First inspect the exact name selected for deletion:

LISDIR OTHER.TEXT:S

Check that it is the practice copy you renamed. Keep NOTE.TEXT for later examples.

Caution — immediate deletion. The following exact-name command deletes the selected file without a confirmation stop, even when CONSUL is supplied. Check the filename before entering it.

Delete the practice copy:

BORRAR OTHER.TEXT:S

BORRAR removes the entry immediately and releases its allocation. Deleted space can be reused; deletion is not a dependable undoable operation. The confirmation behavior for wildcard selection is different; see the command reference.

7. Check free space

Enter:

ESTADO DISCO

Disponible reports total free capacity. Mayor zona reports the largest contiguous free region. The distinction becomes important for direct files, which can need one continuous allocation. You have now completed the basic create–inspect–copy–rename–delete cycle while keeping the original practice text.

Chapter 7 — Editing files and running programs

A text file and a compiled program serve different purposes. EDITAR lets you enter and save ordinary text; LISTAR displays that text afterward. RUN starts a compiled BASIC program. This chapter develops the editing steps introduced in Chapter 6, then explains how to recognize and run a program that has already been compiled.

Prepare a text file

Use DRAFT.TEXT for this exercise. At the CSI prompt, check the name first:

LISDIR DRAFT.TEXT:S

If it already exists, choose an unused name and substitute it throughout this editing example. The example creates a new practice file; it must not replace text you want to keep.

Start the editor:

EDITAR DRAFT.TEXT:S

EDITAR opens a text-editing session. Its command prompt is *, rather than CSI's >. Until you leave the editor, the instructions you enter are editor instructions.

Enter two lines

At the editor prompt, enter:

CASE U

This selects uppercase handling for the text you enter. Add the first line:

INPUT FIRST LINE

INPUT puts the words following it into the file. The saved text is FIRST LINE; INPUT itself is not saved as part of the line.

Add another line:

INPUT SECOND LINE

The file now has two lines of text. These words are file contents, not commands to be executed.

Save and return to CSI

Enter:

FILE

Normally FILE reports salvado y fin edicion, saves the text and returns to CSI. Wait for the > prompt before entering another system command.

If Escape–Q was entered at the editor prompt, the interruption request can remain pending. The next FILE can report salvado and leave EDITAR active at its * prompt. On that path, enter FILE again to save and finish. This is a response to the remaining editor prompt, not a requirement to enter FILE twice for every save. Escape–Q is not a dependable substitute for the editor's completion command.

Once CSI has returned, inspect the saved file:

LISTAR DRAFT.TEXT:S

The contents should be FIRST LINE followed by SECOND LINE. LISTAR reads the text for display; it does not reopen an editing session. Check the saved contents before relying on the file in later work.

Protect existing text before editing

Before valuable edits, make an explicit copy using the COPIFI procedure in Chapter 6. EDITAR's COPIA option requests a backup and is the documented default; NOCOPIA suppresses it. The complete backup naming and recovery rules are unspecified, so the automatic backup is not a substitute for a copy whose name and contents you have checked.

The dependable text-creation workflow here uses CASE U, INPUT and FILE. The full movement, search, replacement and recovery command set is not sufficiently specified for a step-by-step editing course. Do not borrow commands from another editor. TEDITAR is a small-file editor; CEDIT edits structured records. Their interfaces are separate.

Recognize a program that RUN can execute

RUN executes compiled BASIC files, normally of type BASICCOM. The source from which they are built is handled by BASIC and saved as BASICOBJ. These are different stored representations. Renaming DRAFT.TEXT to give it a BASICCOM type would not turn its text into a program.

A COMANDO utility is normally invoked by its command name, as EDITAR and LISTAR were above. RUN is the entry point for compiled BASIC; it is not a general way to open every file.

The next example uses HELLO.BASICCOM:S, produced by the complete programming example in Chapter 15. Follow these steps when that compiled file is available. If you have not built it yet, continue with Chapter 8 and return to this example after completing that program.

Run a compiled program

At CSI, check that the compiled file exists:

LISDIR HELLO.BASICCOM:S

If it is absent, complete the source, save and compilation steps in Chapter 15. A source file alone is not sufficient for this RUN command.

With the compiled greeting available, enter:

RUN HELLO.BASICCOM:S

RUN loads the compiled BASIC program and executes it using the BASIC runtime. For the unchanged greeting from Chapter 15, the program displays HELLO WORLD and finishes. When CSI returns, you can enter another command.

Editing or saving BASIC source does not update an older BASICCOM automatically; compile the changed source before expecting RUN to execute the new version. To end an application, use its own exit operation. BASIC QUIT leaves the BASIC editor/interpreter, whereas BASIC END ends a running BASIC program. Forced interruption can leave open files incomplete, even when CSI returns to the screen.

The next chapter uses ordinary text files for a different purpose: storing instructions that automate command-level work.

For the editor’s complete list of recognized internal names and the limits of their argument descriptions, see the EDITAR reference in Chapter 35.

Chapter 8 — Automating work with EXEC

EXEC is the command-file interpreter. It reads a saved text file and carries out the instructions in that file, so you do not have to type the same sequence at the system prompt each time. A command file can contain ordinary system commands together with EXEC's own instructions for displaying text, using variables, making decisions and repeating work. Its usual file type is EXEC.

You create this text with EDITAR, using the editing steps from Chapter 7. You then leave the editor and ask EXEC to read the saved file. No BASIC compilation is involved: RUN executes compiled BASIC, while EXEC interprets command-file text. Begin with a file that displays a greeting and stops, before adding variables or loops.

Your first command file: HELLO WORLD

Use the name HELLO.EXEC. At CSI, check that it is available:

LISDIR HELLO.EXEC:S

If it already exists, choose another unused name and substitute it throughout the example. This command file is separate from DRAFT.TEXT, the plain text file created in Chapter 7.

Start the editor:

EDITAR HELLO.EXEC:S

The editor's * prompt means it is ready for editing instructions. Select uppercase handling:

CASE U

Now enter the line that will display the greeting:

INPUT &TYPE HELLO WORLD

INPUT belongs to EDITAR: it puts the rest of the line into the file. The saved instruction is &TYPE HELLO WORLD. TYPE belongs to EXEC and displays the following fields, here HELLO and WORLD. The ampersand introduces the EXEC instruction. Nothing is executed merely by entering this text in the editor.

Add the instruction that ends the command file:

INPUT &QUIT

This saves &QUIT as the second line. QUIT tells EXEC to finish the command file and return to its caller. It does not exit EDITAR; you are still composing text.

Save and leave the editor:

FILE

Wait for the CSI > prompt. If FILE leaves the editor active after a pending interruption, follow the save-and-exit guidance in Chapter 7.

Inspect the saved instructions

At CSI, enter:

LISTAR HELLO.EXEC:S

The file should contain these two lines:

&TYPE HELLO WORLD
&QUIT

This block shows the saved contents; do not type it as commands at CSI. INPUT, CASE U and FILE were instructions to the editor and must not appear in this command file. Check the contents before execution. A successful save means the text was written, not that the instructions are valid.

Execute the greeting

At CSI, enter:

EXEC HELLO

EXEC finds HELLO.EXEC through its file lookup and reads it. TYPE displays HELLO WORLD; QUIT ends the command file. When the CSI prompt returns, the sequence is complete. Use the full command name EXEC in these examples.

You can enter the same EXEC command again to repeat the greeting without reopening EDITAR. This is the purpose of a command file: the saved instructions can be reused.

The remaining sections introduce EXEC statements that belong inside command files. To put a statement into a file, use EDITAR's INPUT instruction as above; INPUT itself is not part of the saved statement. A complete program block shows the intended file contents. Examples described as commands entered at CSI, such as EXEC COUNT, are entered after saving and leaving the editor.

Display longer text

TYPE prints fields and substituted values. Ordinary fields have an eight-character limit; HELLO and WORLD each fit separately. For a longer line that should be printed literally, use this three-line construct in the command file:

&BEGTYPE
THIS IS A LONGER LINE OF TEXT
&END

BEGTYPE starts literal output; END closes it. The middle line is printed as text without variable substitution. The two delimiters are required parts of one construct. Retain a final &QUIT after the construct to end this greeting file.

Store a variable

Write:

&I = 1

This creates or changes variable I to the value 1. The ampersand marks EXEC language elements. A frame has sixteen ordinary variable cells; names and values occupy eight characters each.

Next write:

&TYPE VALUE &I

TYPE substitutes the value of I. The line therefore prints the label VALUE followed by 1.

To change it, write:

&I = &I + 1

The expression reads the old value, adds one and stores the result. Integer arithmetic includes truncating division: 7 / 2 gives 3. Addition wraps at the 16-bit boundary: 65535 + 1 gives 0.

Repeat a group

Create COUNT.EXEC with this complete body:

&CONTROL OFF
&I = 1
&WHILE &I LE 3
&TYPE COUNT &I
&I = &I + 1
&REPEAT
&QUIT 0

CONTROL OFF suppresses command echo. Assignment sets I to 1. WHILE checks whether I is less than or equal to 3. TYPE displays it; the assignment increments it; REPEAT returns to the condition. When I becomes 4, the condition fails and QUIT finishes with result 0.

At CSI, run it:

EXEC COUNT

The loop displays COUNT with values 1, 2 and 3, then returns to CSI.

Make a decision

The branch form is:

&IF &I EQ 4 &GOTO -DONE

IF tests equality; GOTO transfers control only if that condition succeeds. The target must exist in the file. A labelled ending can be written:

-DONE &QUIT 0

The label marks the destination, while QUIT is the statement executed there. Labels are not variables.

Pass arguments

For a script ARGTEST.EXEC whose body contains &TYPE &1 and &QUIT 0, enter:

EXEC ARGTEST ALPHA

ALPHA becomes argument 1. &2 selects the second argument, and &INDEX gives the count. EXEC stores at most sixteen argument fields. If IDX contains 2, &&IDX selects argument 2 indirectly.

The special operands &* and &$ mean “any argument” and “every argument” in comparisons. For example:

&IF &* EQ ALPHA &GOTO -FOUND

This branches to a label named FOUND if at least one argument equals ALPHA. It does not expand into a string containing all arguments.

Work with short strings

Write:

&T = &SUB ABCDEFGH 3 5

SUB takes positions 3 through 5, inclusive, producing CDE. To join two fields, write:

&T = ABC | DEF

The vertical bar concatenates them into ABCDEF, subject to the ordinary value width.

Handle nested results

A script may call another using the full EXEC command. Ordinary variables belong to their own nesting level. After the child finishes, &RETCODE holds its result.

Register an error action before a child command:

&ERROR &GOTO -ERR

A qualifying nonzero result transfers to ERR. At that label, this statement propagates the result:

-ERR &QUIT &RETCODE

Without an error action, a nonzero child result need not stop the parent. Complete recursion and error-class rules remain unspecified.

EXEC keeps the state of nested command files in a work file for the current execution partition. The filename ends with the partition number: partition 1 uses SISTEMA.EXEC1, partition 2 uses SISTEMA.EXEC2, and so on. Even a script that only displays information can update this work file. Appendix C describes these files.

Optional implicit execution

At CSI enter:

PONER IMPEXEC ON

This permits an accessible script name to be entered without the EXEC prefix. To restore explicit-only operation, enter:

PONER IMPEXEC OFF

The full form remains the clearest form for instructions. Chapter 35 extends this introduction with queued program input, READ, SKIP, UNTIL, display controls and the complete directive index. It identifies the remaining interface limits separately from the examples.

Chapter 9 — Accounts, messages and everyday habits

An account gives your session an identity. A partition supplies the running context and terminal. Start sensitive work by checking both who you are and what you are allowed to do.

Check identity and privilege

Enter:

ESTADO QUIEN

QUIEN displays the current identity. Now enter:

ESTADO PRIVILEG

PRIVILEG displays the current privilege. A command can require a higher value than your account has; that is an authorization failure, not a spelling error.

Change account

To connect to the existing SISTEMA administrative account, when authorized, enter:

CONECTAR SISTEMA

CONECTAR asks for the account's password when required and changes the connection after successful authentication. When switching to an available account, you do not have to enter FIN first. An account number already connected in another partition cannot be connected a second time: the command reports Esta cuenta esta siendo utilizada por la particion followed by its partition number, with CR = 10. End the existing connection before trying that account again. This refusal precedes the password check. An incorrect password does not authorize the new identity. Check ESTADO QUIEN afterward if uncertain.

An account-named EXEC file can run automatically after connection. Its contents determine whether you reach a menu or the CSI prompt. This facility does not imply that every account has a startup file.

Send a short message

If the intended recipient is connected at partition 2, enter:

MENSAJE 2 HOLA

MENSAJE addresses the recipient and sends the short text HOLA. Use a known recipient; a partition number is not an account number.

For longer text, enter:

MENSAJE 2

The program prompts with ?. Enter one line at a time and finish with an empty line. Read the result message: CR 0 alone does not guarantee live delivery. When mailbox storage is offered, S accepts it. Address stored messages with the complete account name.

Read stored messages

Enter:

BUZON

BUZON displays messages for your account and removes the retrieved messages from the mailbox. Record anything you need before continuing; this is not a nondestructive repeated display.

To inhibit ordinary incoming utility messages, enter:

PONER MENSAJE OFF

To receive them again, enter:

PONER MENSAJE ON

Privileged broadcasts can still arrive while ordinary messages are inhibited.

A reliable daily sequence

Connect, check your account, inspect the file you plan to use and then start its application. Save before switching tasks. Use an explicit copy before a valuable edit. Read both command results and diagnostic text. Finish by closing the application and entering FIN at CSI.

For the basic file-work sequence, return to Chapter 6. The full alphabetical command reference is Chapter 31.

Part III — System administration

Chapter 10 — Administering accounts

An account identifies a user and determines the privilege used by commands. It also supplies the account number against which file ownership is checked. A partition is a separate matter: it provides memory and execution time. Creating an account does not create a partition, and stopping a partition does not remove its user's account.

SISTEMA is the administrative account used in the examples. Account maintenance requires privilege 5 and, at that privilege, the command requires the SISTEMA identity. Connect to SISTEMA as described in Chapter 9, then check the current privilege:

ESTADO PRIVILEG

The report must show the privilege required for maintenance. These are software checks; the machine does not provide a general protected-memory boundary between application and executive code.

List accounts and connected users

To see the registered accounts, enter:

CUENTA (PANT)

The listing contains columns headed Cuenta, Pal.paso, Priv and Id: account name, password, privilege and account number. This administrative listing can display stored passwords. Keep its screen or printed copy private. It is a list of account records, not a list of people currently using terminals.

To inspect connected users instead, enter:

ESTADO USUARIOS

Use that report before changing or removing an account. Arrange for the affected user to finish work and disconnect. Account names can be synonyms for the same number; deleting one name must not be mistaken for deleting every identity that shares its number.

Understand the maintenance dialogue

Start the interactive account editor:

CUENTA

The operation prompt is:

Modo (Nueva/Camb/Borrar/Fin)?

At this prompt the following letters select an operation. Each answer is followed by Return.

Answer Meaning What follows
N Nueva: new account Name, synonym, password and privilege
C Camb: change an existing account Name, password and privilege
B Borrar: delete one account name Name to remove
F Fin: finish maintenance Write the account list and return to CSI

Make one planned change at a time, finish with F, and inspect the account list again. F is the save-and-finish operation; leaving the machine or forcibly interrupting the program is not a substitute.

In the following examples, <new-account>, <account-to-remove> and <new-password> are placeholders. Type the chosen value without angle brackets. SISTEMA already exists: do not use it as the new-account or deletion example.

Add a user account

First use the account listing to choose an unused name. Use an uppercase name of no more than eight characters, beginning with a letter, and keep the remaining characters to letters and digits. Start CUENTA as above. At its operation prompt, enter:

N

At Nombre cuenta, enter the new name:

<new-account>

At Cta.sinonima?, press Return without typing anything. An empty answer requests a distinct account number. Supplying an existing name instead creates a synonym sharing that account's number; it is not the usual choice when adding a separate user.

At Pal.paso?, enter the password chosen for the account:

<new-password>

The maintenance dialogue echoes this input. Password storage is an eight-character field, and a comma is not accepted. Use no more than eight characters and avoid spaces, which this dialogue removes. An empty answer during creation leaves the new account without a password. Login masks input, but neither that masking nor a password makes the stored account file encrypted.

At Privilegio?, enter:

1

Privilege 1 permits ordinary file work but does not grant account maintenance. Some tools, including parts of the programming environment, require more privilege. Assign the privilege needed for the intended work rather than assuming every new account should match SISTEMA.

When the operation prompt returns, enter:

F

CUENTA writes the revised account list. Check it:

CUENTA (PANT)

Check the new name, privilege and assigned number. The program allocates a number; this example does not prescribe one. Test the new login from another terminal if available, keeping the administrative connection open. This is possible because the new account has a distinct account number; another name for an account already in use cannot be tested this way.

Change a password or privilege

Password changes are available through CUENTA's C operation. There is no separately established unprivileged password-change command. The procedure below changes SISTEMA's password; use another existing account name to maintain that account instead. Before changing the administrative password, ensure that a usable recovery arrangement is available. The new password cannot be tested while another partition remains connected under the same account number. An ordinary account must not be assumed able to restore administrative access; the backup and recovery limits are described in Chapter 13.

Enter:

CUENTA

At the operation prompt, enter:

C

At Nombre cuenta, enter:

SISTEMA

At Pal.paso?, supply the new password:

<new-password>

During a change, an empty answer keeps the existing password. It does not remove it. To remove a password deliberately, the special answer is a single hash character:

#

These are alternatives at the same prompt. Choose the new password, an empty answer to retain it, or # to clear it; do not type all three. Clearing the password permits connection without that password challenge.

CUENTA next asks Privilegio?, even if only the password was being changed. For SISTEMA's administrative privilege, enter:

5

When maintaining another account, enter its intended privilege. Do not copy 5 automatically. A blank privilege answer causes the prompt to repeat rather than preserving the previous value.

At the operation prompt, finish:

F

After F saves the change, end the existing SISTEMA connection at its terminal:

FIN

FIN disconnects that account. Then, at another available terminal, enter:

CONECTAR SISTEMA

Supply the new password when requested. If the command reports Esta cuenta esta siendo utilizada por la particion 1 with CR = 10, partition 1 still holds that account number; another partition number can appear in the message. That refusal does not check the password. End the existing connection before retrying.

After the new login succeeds, check its privilege:

ESTADO PRIVILEG

For SISTEMA, the expected value is 5. Account aliases have separate name records, but names sharing an account number do not permit simultaneous connections. Changing one record must not be assumed to change every synonym's password.

Remove an account

Before removing an account, have its user close applications and enter FIN. Record its account number and decide what should happen to its files. Account deletion removes the named account record; it does not perform a file deletion or ownership transfer. Transfer required files before withdrawing the user's login.

Caution — removes a login name. Do not delete SISTEMA as a practice operation. Check for synonyms and files owned by the account number before proceeding.

Enter:

CUENTA

At the operation prompt, enter:

B

At Nombre cuenta, supply the exact account selected for removal:

<account-to-remove>

The program removes the matching record from its working list and returns to the operation prompt. There is no additional per-account yes/no confirmation in this path. Finish and save:

F

Inspect the result:

CUENTA (PANT)

The removed name should be absent. Other names sharing its account number are separate records and may remain.

Do not substitute CUENTA (ELIMINAR) for this procedure. ELIMINAR removes the account system itself. CUENTA (VACIAR) clears account history; it also does not delete an individual account.

Ownership and sharing

The next operations require the current file owner's account. COMPART cannot be used by SISTEMA, and administrative privilege alone does not make CAMPRO select another owner's private files. Use a distinct non-SISTEMA account with privilege 3 or higher for these operations. Account creation and privilege maintenance are described above; do not assign privilege 5 merely to follow this example.

At CSI, connect to the chosen account, substituting its actual name:

CONECTAR <source-account>

The account number must not already be connected elsewhere. Supply its password when requested, then check the identity:

ESTADO QUIEN

Check that it has sufficient privilege:

ESTADO PRIVILEG

The following commands require at least 3. Create NOTE.TEXT under this account using the steps in Chapter 6. If that name already exists or resolves to a public file, choose an unused practice name and substitute it throughout this procedure. Do not reuse another account's file merely because it can be listed. Inspect the file and its ownership before proceeding:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

To display the accounts sharing this practice file, enter:

COMPART NOTE.TEXT:S (PANT)

PANT lists grants. With none present, an empty result and CR = 0 are expected. From SISTEMA, this operation instead reports La cuenta sistema no puede compartir ficheros, CR = 12. Ordinary COMPART enters a grant/revoke dialogue. Sharing creates an alias to the owner's file; it does not duplicate the allocation. The owner retains the data and its protection policy.

While still connected as the current owner, transfer the practice file to SISTEMA:

CAMPRO NOTE.TEXT:S (SISTEMA NOCONSUL)

This changes ownership to SISTEMA. NOCONSUL suppresses consultation. Running the same command from SISTEMA does not pull another account's private file into its ownership; it can report No se ha cambiado ningun fichero, CR = 12. Check the current owner when this occurs.

End the source account's session:

FIN

When no other partition is connected under SISTEMA's account number, connect to the recipient account:

CONECTAR SISTEMA

Supply its password if requested. Inspect the transferred entry:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

Read the practice text to check that its contents remain available:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:S

Transfer to SISTEMA does not guarantee loss of lookup access for the former owner: SISTEMA's account number 0 participates in public-file fallback. Use COMPART when the original owner is to retain ownership and another account needs shared access.

Protect the account service

SISTEMA.CUENTA stores account records and uses special ownership byte 0xFF. It is not an ordinary public text file to rename or rewrite casually. CONECTAR.COMANDO performs connection. If either becomes unreachable, CSI can appear without the normal authentication path. A visible command prompt alone is therefore not proof of a healthy account service.

CAMBIAR changes a command's minimum privilege field; it does not change the current account's privilege. Application checks can impose further restrictions. Chapter 31 explains that command and Appendix C describes the account components.

Chapter 11 — Managing storage

A disk association tells the system which volume a name such as S or A denotes. Before modifying storage, identify the association and inspect its capacity. Never infer the destination from a familiar letter alone.

Inspect capacity

Enter:

ASOCIAR

With no arguments, ASOCIAR lists the current associations. Find the disk letter you intend to use. Then enter:

ESTADO DISCO

Compare Disponible, the total free space, with Mayor zona, the largest continuous free region. A direct file can require one contiguous allocation. For example, 38 KiB split into separate runs of 1 KiB and 37 KiB cannot satisfy a 38 KiB contiguous request. A sequential file can use several runs.

ESTADO DISCO can correct a cached free-count field when it recomputes capacity. It is a status operation with a possible metadata update, rather than a byte-for-byte read-only inspection.

Associate and read a floppy

Distinguish the physical device name, the logical disk letter and the volume label. Each identifies a different part of the storage arrangement. FLOPPY1 and FLOPPY2 identify physical device definitions. The letters A through G designate logical disks that programs can access. The volume label is the name assigned to the medium, independent of its logical disk letter. Associating A with FLOPPY1 does not rename that floppy's volume, and A is not inherently a floppy drive.

The following procedure requires a working compatible floppy drive and an already initialized system-format floppy. FLOPPY1 and FLOPPY2 select different driver interfaces; use the definition matching the fitted controller. An association by itself does not prove that the drive is ready.

First display the associations:

ASOCIAR

Make sure A is available for this procedure. If an application is using its current association, close the application's files before changing it. Check the physical definitions:

LISTAR SISTEMA.NOMBDISP:S

For a fitted drive using FLOPPY1, associate it with A:

ASOCIAR A FLOPPY1

This selects the first floppy definition. Where the installed drive uses FLOPPY2 instead, substitute that physical name. Do not choose a definition merely because it appears in the file.

Insert the prepared floppy while the drive is idle and close its loading mechanism. Notify the operating system:

MONTAR A

MONTAR tells the system that the disk or floppy has changed. It refreshes the device's media state; it does not attach a filesystem beneath a directory path. It neither formats a blank floppy nor assigns its logical letter.

List the files visible to the current account:

LISDIR *.*:A

The suffix :A makes the device explicit. A missing file can reflect account ownership rather than an empty medium. To read the practice file when it is present, enter:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:A

This displays the file on the floppy. It does not read NOTE.TEXT on S.

Copy a file to and from a floppy

Use writable expendable media with enough free space and an unused destination name. To copy the closed practice text from S to A, enter:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:S NOTE.TEXT:A (VERIFI)

The first filename is the source; the second is the destination. VERIFI requests a readback check. Wait for completion, then inspect the result:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:A

To copy it back under a different, unused name, enter:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:A RETURN.TEXT:S (VERIFI)

The reversed drive suffixes reverse the direction. Using RETURN.TEXT avoids replacing the original practice file. Display the returned copy:

LISTAR RETURN.TEXT:S

Compare its text with the original. For a larger archive spanning removable media, use the ARCHIVAR and RECARGAR procedures in Chapter 13.

Change or release removable media

Before changing a floppy, finish transfers and close every file using it. Wait for drive activity to stop, replace the medium, then enter:

MONTAR A

List the new medium before using it. A no prep. diagnostic indicates that the device is not ready; check that the intended medium is inserted and available. A prot. escritura diagnostic indicates write protection. Restore the intended media or protection state before retrying; do not select Ignore to turn a failed write into an apparent success. Chapter 27 explains the distinct retry, ignore and abort choices. When several units have changed, the alternative form is:

MONTAR *

The asterisk asks the system to examine all units. It is not a wildcard filename.

To release the logical association after work has finished, enter:

ASOCIAR A

This removes A's association. It does not eject the floppy or close an application's files for it. No separate universal unmount-and-flush command is specified here; closing files and completing transfers come first. Do not remove active media or disassociate S while the operating system depends on it.

Format a floppy

Formatting prepares the physical tracks and sectors. INICIAR is the floppy utility; INICDISC is the disk utility. Both also offer operations that change labels, directory capacity or write protection. These are distinct requests, not synonyms for MONTAR.

Caution — formatting destroys existing contents. Check A's association and the physical medium before issuing FORMAT or VACIA. Use an expendable floppy of the correct size, sidedness and density for the fitted drive. The minimum command privilege is 3.

Read the floppy utility's help before selecting a format:

AYUDA INICIAR

For a drive and medium supporting the utility's double-sided, double-density format, its documented format example can be written in full as:

INICIAR A (FORMAT CARA 2)

FORMAT requests physical formatting; CARA 2 selects two sides. In the FLOPPY2 path, this form uses 77 cylinders, two heads and 26 sector IDs per track. Both tracks of cylinder 0 use FM recording with 128-byte sectors; cylinders 1–76 use MFM with 256-byte sectors. These are the utility's software-format characteristics, not identification of a drive manufacturer, model or interchangeable PC format. The fitted drive and medium must support them.

The following is a syntax template, not a command to type literally:

INICIAR A (FORMAT CARA <sides> PISTA <tracks> SECTOR <sectors>)

The help lists these geometry options, but it does not establish that every PISTA or SECTOR value changes every physical formatting loop. The FLOPPY2 loop described above reaches cylinder 76. Do not use the template to assume support for arbitrary geometries or derive values from a hard disk or a modern floppy format. The options are:

Option Operation
FORMAT Physically format the floppy; required for a virgin medium
CARA n Select the number of sides
PISTA n Request tracks per side; physical-loop behavior depends on the controller path
SECTOR n Request sectors per track; arbitrary physical-format values are not established
DIRE n Request directory capacity for n files
ETIQU Change the volume label
VACIA Erase the file contents described by the volume's directory
WP / NOPE Set or clear software write protection
BOOT=d Copy the bootstrap track from the specified disk

Do not add BOOT merely to make an ordinary data floppy. The source bootstrap and destination hardware must be compatible. The full initialization dialogue and its success messages are not specified here, so no sample completion output is supplied.

After the utility completes successfully, refresh the device:

MONTAR A

Check the format:

VERFORM A

Read the diagnostics as well as the completion result. This does not replace file-copy verification or certify compatibility with another drive. Then inspect its directory:

LISDIR *.*:A

Then use the preceding copy-and-readback procedure with a small practice file. A successful listing alone does not verify every track of the medium.

Initialize a hard-disk volume

INICDISC formats the selected disk volume and creates its filesystem structures. It operates on the disk named by a logical association such as A. It does not create an execution partition, and ASOCIAR does not reserve or divide physical disk space.

Caution — formatting destroys the selected volume's contents and can change disk-mapping metadata. Back up any data to retain, stop applications using the destination, and verify its physical extent. Never apply the example to S or to a region overlapping the system volume. Minimum privilege is 3.

Select the destination

Begin by inspecting the current assignments:

ASOCIAR

A must designate the intended expendable hard-disk volume, not a floppy or another active device. If no suitable volume is configured, use the following additional-volume guidance before proceeding.

Optional: prepare an additional volume

An additional data volume can be made available when the disk configuration already provides a suitable unused extent and device definition. For a configuration in which DISCO2 names that intended extent, enter:

ASOCIAR A DISCO2

This connects logical letter A to the DISCO2 definition. It does not prove that storage has been reserved or that a usable filesystem exists there. Continue only after verifying that the target extent is available and does not overlap any volume to retain. DISCO2 is a conditional example, not a claim that an additional volume is supplied or configured on every system.

The documented formatting path for DISCO2 uses PISTA both for its cylinder count and its starting cylinder. With PISTA 397, its physical span is cylinders 397–793 inclusive. Changing PISTA can therefore move the beginning of the formatted region as well as change its size. Do not reduce it to shorten formatting without rechecking the entire disk layout. The number 397 is an example for a compatible arrangement, not a universal disk parameter.

The remaining steps initialize a filesystem on that configured extent. A general procedure for creating arbitrary new physical-volume descriptors or resizing populated volumes is not established. A name in SISTEMA.NOMBDISP alone is insufficient to perform those operations safely.

Format and review the dialogue

For an expendable destination whose configured geometry is five heads, 397 cylinders and 32 sectors per track, with 256-byte sectors, enter:

INICDISC A (FORMAT CARA 5 PISTA 397 SECTOR 32 DIRE 64)

FORMAT requests physical formatting. CARA gives the number of heads, PISTA the cylinder count with the placement qualification above, and SECTOR the sector count per track. DIRE requests directory capacity. The utility can adjust that request: this form reports 88 directory entries, not exactly 64. Review the displayed values before accepting them.

At Usted quiere continuar (S/N)?, enter the following only if the destruction warning names the intended destination:

S

The utility asks Etiqueta del disco:. Supply a new volume label; for this example:

PRACTICE

The label identifies the formatted volume. It does not rename association A or the physical device definition. The utility next displays directory capacity, interleave, track offset and geometry. At Es correcta esta informacion (S/N)?, accept only after checking the entire summary:

S

At the optional surface-analysis question, this elementary formatting example selects:

N

This skips the separate surface analysis; the result must not be described as a complete surface test. At Desea reintentos en lectura y escritura (S/N)?, enter:

S

This enables the requested read/write retries. At Quiere incluir ahora alguna pista (S/N)?, the example supplies no additional bad-track entries:

N

Do not reuse that answer to conceal known media defects. Bad-track handling requires accurate information for the actual medium. Wait for formatting to finish and inspect any diagnostics; a command result alone does not certify the data.

Mount and check the new filesystem

Notify the system of the initialized volume:

MONTAR A

MONTAR refreshes media state. Check the format:

VERFORM A

Read the reported errors before proceeding. Then list the catalogue:

LISDIR *.*:A

This lists entries visible to the current account. To check ordinary file use, start with the closed NOTE.TEXT practice file from Chapter 6 and unused destination names. Copy it to the new volume:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:S NOTE.TEXT:A (VERIFI)

VERIFI requests readback verification. Copy it back under a different name:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:A CHECK.TEXT:S (VERIFI)

This preserves the original source. Inspect the returned text:

LISTAR CHECK.TEXT:S

Compare it with NOTE.TEXT. These checks exercise mounting, catalogue access, allocation and file transfers; they do not establish bootstrap compatibility or replace a backup procedure.

IBM-compatible interchange floppies

IBM 3741 interchange is a separate format path. INICIBM writes the headers used by the interchange utilities; it is not a general synonym for INICIAR and does not by itself establish that a virgin floppy has been physically formatted correctly.

For an expendable, correctly prepared compatible floppy on A, enter:

INICIBM A

This changes the medium's headers. To inspect an IBM-format directory, use:

DIRIBM A

DIRIBM reports the volume and file records in that format. Use SECIBM to write sequential system data to an IBM 3741 floppy and IBMSEC for the reverse conversion. These utilities handle record-format and character-code conversion; ordinary COPIFI does not replace them.

Understand protection

ATRIB controls independent write, deletion and read restrictions. These bits affect particular access paths, not a universal “make everything safe” state.

For a practice sequential file owned by your account, enter:

ATRIB NOTE.TEXT:S (E NOCONSUL)

E sets write protection without changing the name. Attribute-only changes use one filename; the two-name form is for renaming. Inspect the result:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

For the sequential practice file, the format indication changes from S to SW while the text is retained. Write protection can block extension while still permitting replacement through deletion and recreation. B protects deletion; L is the internal read-protection bit; ATRIB accepts LE for this operation. The longer word LEER is rejected. Clearing it with NOL requires privilege 255.

Individual protection Read existing contents Replace by deleting and recreating Append to existing contents
E, write Allowed Allowed through deletion/recreation Refused
B, deletion Allowed Refused Allowed
L, read Refused Allowed through deletion/recreation Refused

The table describes the current owner's sequential-file access with each protection applied separately. Replacing a file can involve deleting the old entry and creating a new one, which is why write protection alone does not prevent every replacement. Shared access and combined attributes can impose additional restrictions. Chapter 17 maps these operations to BASIC opening modes. Clear the practice file's E bit when finished:

ATRIB NOTE.TEXT:S (NOE NOCONSUL)

NOE removes write protection. Inspect the file again:

LISDIR NOTE.TEXT:S

The format indication returns from SW to S. Neither attribute-only command renames the file.

Other disk maintenance

The hard-disk initialization procedure prepares a filesystem on a configured volume. Its geometry and placement requirements remain essential for later maintenance. Chapter 12 explains memory partitions; Chapter 13 explains tape initialization and backup.

ORGDIR reorganizes directory entries. ELIMINAR removes an entry without releasing its storage. UNOSEC rebuilds volume metadata. These are maintenance tools, not alternatives to ordinary copying or deletion. Make a usable backup before invoking them.

Do not append to an unfinished sequential file merely to make it usable again. Its incomplete end-of-file information can cause subsequent output to overwrite volume metadata. A correctly closed empty file is different from an unfinished file. Chapter 13 discusses recovery precautions; Chapter 17 gives the programmer's restriction and Chapter 26 explains the write path.

Chapter 12 — Devices, printing and partitions

Device names separate a program's purpose from the hardware serving it. IMPR1 means a logical printer; IMP names a physical printer definition. ASOCIAR connects the two. This chapter first configures terminal and printer devices, then turns to the memory partitions that use them. Disk and floppy associations are covered in Chapter 11.

Inspect associations and device definitions

First list the current associations:

ASOCIAR

This shows which logical names already select devices. Inspect their physical definitions separately:

LISTAR SISTEMA.NOMBDISP:S

This text lists names and definitions. A definition for optional hardware does not mean that hardware is fitted. Compare the list with the machine's actual configuration.

Configure a terminal

The terminal and its driver must agree on communication settings. The console can operate with a 19200-baud, seven-data-bit, no-parity, one-stop-bit connection. Baud specifies transmission speed; data width and parity specify the character framing. Use settings supported by the actual terminal and driver.

A terminal class translates the operating system's cursor and display operations into the sequences understood by that terminal. Class 1 uses ESC G coordinate sequences; class 2 uses an ANSI-like sequence. A class mismatch can leave typed commands working while cursor movement is wrong. The classes are executable translation components; Chapter 28 documents the actual sequences.

For a console on PAN1 that requires class 2, enter:

ASOCIAR CONSOLA PAN1 (C2)

C2 loads the class-2 display translation. The terminal must understand that control sequence family; a terminal accepting ordinary text is not necessarily compatible with cursor positioning and erasure. To select class 1 instead, enter:

ASOCIAR CONSOLA PAN1 (C1)

Choose the class appropriate to the terminal; these two commands are alternatives, not a required round trip. Repeated class changes can lower the partition's available-memory ceiling. The normal dimensions are L79/P23. L is used both by output formatting and as CSI's input budget; a very small L can submit a partial command before Return. Keep the normal values unless there is a specific reason to change them.

For serial input, configure the sending terminal to honor the computer's receive flow control where the connection supports it. The ALP driver requests a pause as its input ring fills and resumes reception after queued input drains. This does not remove the serial controller's smaller receive-buffer limit: pace long input streams if characters are lost. Exact modem-signal wiring depends on the fitted interface.

PAN9 and PAN10 are SS2 terminal device names, with IS9 and IS10 aliases. They do not mean partition numbers 9 and 10. A partition may use one of these consoles through ACTIVAR when the corresponding hardware is fitted; Chapter 28 describes their separate serial paths.

Print a text file

If the fitted printer uses the IMP definition and the stated serial settings, associate it:

ASOCIAR IMPR1 IMP (B4800 PE)

B4800 selects nominal 4800 baud and PE selects even parity. This form selects seven data bits and one stop bit; configure the printer for the same framing. Adding W8 would request a different, eight-bit width. Use the settings required by the actual printer. Now print the practice text:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:S (PRINT1 NOHEAD)

PRINT1 selects logical printer 1; NOHEAD suppresses the listing heading. Confirm that paper output actually appears. A successful command result does not certify physical printing.

IMP uses serial readiness signals. An offline or disconnected printer can produce Impresora no preparada (R/Q):; restore the connection and readiness before requesting R to retry, or choose Q to abandon the operation. CTS can hold transmission until the printer permits it. A successful return from a buffered output call is not proof that the complete page has arrived.

Keep IMPR1 associated if you will continue with deferred printing below.

Deferred printing

SPOOLER collects reports for later printing. Initialization can recover queue state and remove selected backing reports, so review existing work first. With the printer configured, enter:

SPOOLER INIC

This initializes the service. Inspect it:

SPOOLER ESTADO

To pause ordinary queue output, enter:

SPOOLER PARA

PARA does not override every explicit print/relaunch request. To inspect queued reports, enter:

SPOOLER LIST

Collection uses SISTEMA.SPOOLCOL and report files. Chapter 29 explains their relationship, and the command reference separates the remaining queue controls.

Release an unused printer association

The following cleanup is optional. Use it only after applications and queued printing no longer need IMPR1. Pausing a queue is not proof that its printer association is no longer needed; do not disassociate a printer still serving the spooler.

When the association is no longer in use, enter:

ASOCIAR IMPR1

This removes the logical association. It is different from ASOCIAR with no arguments, which lists associations, and it is not a spooler shutdown command.

The remaining sections manage partitions. Device configuration selects hardware; partition allocation supplies memory and execution state. Inspect those resources separately before making changes.

List partitions

To display the allocated memory partitions, enter:

ESTADO MEMORIA

This is the partition listing. Its heading includes Puer, Modu, DIRBA, DIRMX, DIRAL, SP---, PC---, Tama|, Rutina and Usuario. The partition number and memory-module number are separate columns. The addresses describe the partition's memory and execution state; Rutina and Usuario identify its routine and connected account when available. You do not need to interpret every address to find which partitions are allocated.

A stopped partition can remain in this report because PARAR retains its memory. An empty user field therefore does not by itself mean that the partition can be allocated again. The report can also contain resident components such as NUCLEO and SPOOLER; those rows are not additional user accounts.

For the available memory modules, enter:

ESTADO MODULO

A module is a memory resource from which a partition can be allocated. This report helps select the final argument of PONER MEMORIA; it is not a list of disk volumes.

For connected users, enter:

ESTADO USUARIOS

This shows the user connections and their connection dates and times. To list registered accounts, including those not connected, use CUENTA (PANT), as described in Chapter 10. Check these separate reports before allocating, stopping or removing a partition.

Create and activate a partition

A partition is an independently scheduled execution area in memory. It is not a region of a disk and has no filesystem to mount. PONER MEMORIA allocates it; ACTIVAR starts its terminal or command environment. Disk access is configured separately with ASOCIAR and MONTAR, as taught in Chapter 11.

Perform memory configuration from SISTEMA at privilege 5. Use the next partition number after those already allocated: for example, 2 when only partition 1 exists. The public numbering range is 1–16, but available memory and fitted terminals determine how many useful partitions can run.

Inspect available modules first:

ESTADO MODULO

The following configuration requires an available module 1 and a terminal on PAN2. It is an example for that configuration, not a command to repeat on an already occupied partition. Enter:

PONER MEMORIA 2 48000 1

This requests displayed partition 2 in module 1, using 48000 as the size parameter. The parameter is not an exact allocation guarantee. In the demonstrated configuration, the first allocation in that module occupies 0x4C8B–0xFFFF, a region of 45,941 bytes; a request of 40000 also receives that region. This result does not establish the allocation rule for every shared-module configuration. The final 1 is a module selection, not an account number.

Inspect the actual allocation:

ESTADO MEMORIA

Use the reported bounds and size rather than assuming that the requested byte count was assigned. Activate the terminal:

ACTIVAR 2 (PAN2)

The second terminal can now connect to an account. Allocation and activation are separate because a partition needs memory before it can run. Inspect the result:

ESTADO MEMORIA

The command reports partition memory. The accepted numbering range does not guarantee enough RAM for sixteen useful active users.

Stop a partition deliberately

At the target terminal, save and close the application. At its CSI prompt, enter:

FIN

FIN disconnects the account and leaves that terminal ready for another login. To stop the partition itself, return to the administrative terminal and enter:

PARAR 2

PARAR stops the partition while retaining its allocation. It can refuse a connected account. FORZAR can inject FIN into a target, but forced completion can leave a partial application record; use it only for recovery, not routine logout.

To use a stopped, still-allocated partition again, enter:

ACTIVAR 2 (PAN2)

This reactivates its terminal. It uses the existing memory allocation and does not create another account.

Remove an allocated partition
