Stopping and removing are different operations. PARAR releases the active terminal but retains the partition's memory allocation. The removal form of PONER MEMORIA omits the size and module arguments.

The following continues the dedicated-module example above. Partition 2 must be the last allocated partition, must be stopped, and must not be the partition from which you issue the command. If you reactivated it in the preceding example, finish its session and stop it again before proceeding.

From the administrative partition, enter:

PONER MEMORIA 2

With no size argument, this requests removal of partition 2. Do not append 0 as a presumed equivalent: the size parser raises small explicit sizes to its minimum allocation. Check the result:

ESTADO MEMORIA

Then inspect the available modules:

ESTADO MODULO

The partition must no longer appear as allocated. Remove partitions in reverse allocation order; the command refuses removal of a non-final partition. An active target, the caller's own partition, or an incompatible neighboring partition can also prevent removal. The routine can rearrange context storage when partitions share a module. That shared-module case is outside this elementary procedure; use the dedicated-module arrangement shown here.

These commands alter the running configuration. They do not by themselves save it as the next startup configuration. GENSIST writes the system nucleus and is a separate, consequential maintenance operation. Removing a memory partition does not erase disk files or remove accounts.

Chapter 13 — Backup and recovery

A backup must be usable for the kind of recovery you need. File copies protect selected files; archive operations preserve selected files or accounts; full-medium copying serves a different purpose. The presence of a command does not guarantee that its required tape or floppy device is installed.

Make a file-level backup

For the practice text, choose an unused destination and enter:

COPIFI NOTE.TEXT:S SAFENOTE.TEXT:S (VERIFI)

COPIFI duplicates the file and requests verification. Inspect the copy before relying on it:

LISTAR SAFENOTE.TEXT:S

A copy on the same volume helps recover an accidental edit but does not protect against failure of that volume. For independent backup, select a separately configured destination device and verify the resulting files there.

Archive files to floppy media

ARCHIVAR saves selected files, an account's files, or a whole volume. RECARGAR reads media written by ARCHIVAR. These operations differ from copying an ordinary file with COPIFI. Prepare and associate the destination floppy as described in Chapter 11. The following examples give the documented command forms; successful physical operation requires the compatible drive and media.

With a writable archive floppy on A, save just the practice text:

ARCHIVAR NOTE.TEXT:S,A (PANT VERIFI CONSU)

NOTE.TEXT:S selects the source file; A selects the archive destination. PANT displays names, VERIFI requests readback after writing, and CONSU asks before saving each selected file. Read each request before answering. This writes destination media; do not use a floppy containing information that must be retained.

To select the connected account's files instead, the alternative command is:

ARCHIVAR S,A (CUENTA PANT VERIFI CONSU)

CUENTA limits selection to the current account. It does not mean “copy the account database.” This is a broader backup, not a required second step after the single-file example. ARCHIVAR can use a succession of media, but the exact media-change dialogue is not fully specified here; follow the utility's requests and retain the media in their written order.

For a trial restore of the practice file, connect to the intended receiving account and select a destination disk with no NOTE.TEXT file of that account. With the archive on A, enter:

RECARGAR NOTE.TEXT:A,S (CUENTA NOREEM PANT VERIFI CONSU)

RECARGAR selects that archived filename for the connected account, writes to S and refuses to replace an existing file because NOREEM is present. CONSU retains the per-file consultation. If NOTE.TEXT already exists, this is not a test that the archived copy can replace it; choose a separate receiving environment for a real restore test.

After a successful restore, inspect the file:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:S

Check the restored content. Readback while writing an archive and later restoring a usable file are different checks.

Prepare tape access

The tape commands use a logical device such as TAPE1. TAP is the physical tape-driver definition. A cartridge is sequential media, so the system does not present it as a disk directory that can be browsed with LISDIR.

The command language and initialization operations described below are available, but a complete physical tape backup-and-restore cycle is not established. The controller's physical recording details also remain incomplete. Treat the examples as operating instructions for a compatible, working installation, with that limitation; no promised transfer rate, media capacity or sample completion output is implied.

Begin by inspecting the associations:

ASOCIAR

If the fitted controller uses TAP and TAPE1 is available, enter:

ASOCIAR TAPE1 TAP

This loads and initializes the tape driver for that association. It queries the controller, so an absent or incompatible controller can leave the command waiting. Do not proceed to an archive command unless the association completes and the device is ready.

MONTAR is the disk/floppy media-change command. There is no established MONTAR TAPE1 form. Cartridge loading, readiness and positioning belong to the tape utility and controller. Load the cartridge using the drive's physical controls while it is idle, and follow the utility's requests. No standalone universal tape mount, rewind or eject command is specified in this interface.

Initialize a cartridge

INITTAPE prepares a new cartridge or erases a previous tape volume. It uses TAPE1; its command line does not take a disk letter or a cartridge filename.

Caution — destroys existing tape information. Check the cartridge before initialization. Never initialize the archive cartridge you intend to restore.

For ordinary initialization, enter:

INITTAPE

This begins tape initialization and writes a volume label. The utility has confirmation and label-entry steps; their complete dialogue is not reproduced here. Read them on the terminal before allowing the operation to proceed.

For a new cartridge requiring the utility's tensioning pass, use this alternative:

INITTAPE (TENSION)

TENSION traverses and rewinds the cartridge before initialization. It is not a harmless standalone rewind request: it remains part of INITTAPE and therefore belongs only in the initialization procedure. The volume label records a name of up to eight characters. Further archive-label details appear in Appendix A.

Save files or a volume to tape

With TAPE1 ready and an expendable initialized cartridge loaded, begin with one closed practice file:

ARCHIVAR NOTE.TEXT:S,TAPE1 (PANT VERIFI CONSU)

The source is NOTE.TEXT on S and the destination is tape. The options retain visible file selection, write verification and consultation. The operation writes an archive; LISTAR and ordinary character output to TAPE1 do not produce an equivalent tape archive.

To archive the connected account's files, use this broader selection instead:

ARCHIVAR S,TAPE1 (CUENTA PANT VERIFI CONSU)

CUENTA selects that account's files. To select every account on the source volume, the alternative is:

ARCHIVAR S,TAPE1 (VOLUM PANT VERIFI CONSU)

VOLUM broadens the selection to the volume. Arrange for applications to close files before a backup that must represent a consistent set of business records. These three commands are alternative selections, not a sequence to run on the same cartridge without considering the existing archive.

Restore an archive from tape

Use the cartridge written by ARCHIVAR, with TAPE1 associated to the compatible drive. Connect to the receiving account, choose the destination disk, and avoid existing filenames for the first restore trial. Do not run INITTAPE.

To restore the practice file to S, enter:

RECARGAR NOTE.TEXT:TAPE1,S (CUENTA NOREEM PANT VERIFI CONSU)

The tape appears before the comma because it is now the source. S is the destination. CUENTA selects the connected account, and NOREEM prevents replacement of an existing file. After a successful restoration, examine its contents:

LISTAR NOTE.TEXT:S

A full-volume restore has a much wider scope. Its documented form, still retaining nonreplacement and consultation, is:

RECARGAR TAPE1,S (VOLUM NOREEM PANT VERIFI CONSU)

Use this only when the destination volume and ownership requirements have been planned. VOLUM restores across accounts. REEMPLA permits overwriting existing files; COPREEM limits the operation to replacing existing files; NOREEM loads only absent files. These are alternatives. Omitting the filename can select whole-volume restoration by default, so keep the selection explicit.

RESCUE selects another archived account for restoration into the current account; see RECARGAR before using that form. Do not assume that restoring application files also restores every account or startup setting.

Whole-medium copying and tape completion

BACKUP performs binary copying between disks or between disk and tape. It is not ARCHIVAR's file-selection format. The explicit disk-to-tape form is:

BACKUP S TAPE1

This selects the entire source medium S for the tape destination. It requires compatible devices and expendable destination media. Do not run BACKUP without arguments while learning: its defaults are S as source and A as destination, which can overwrite the wrong disk. RECARGAR is documented for ARCHIVAR media; do not assume it restores BACKUP media merely because both use tape labels.

When tape work has completed and the drive is idle, the logical association can be removed:

ASOCIAR TAPE1

This releases the association. It is not a rewind, eject or wait-for-transfer-completion operation. Complete the utility and follow the drive's physical unloading procedure first. The precise controller recovery and full binary-tape restoration procedures remain unspecified.

Diagnose before repair

Symptom Likely question to resolve Next action
Disk full despite free space Is the required contiguous run available? Compare Disponible with Mayor zona
Exact file not found Are account, drive and lookup visibility correct? Check identity, association and wildcard listing
Directory full despite empty slots Has a name-specific probe cycle filled? Stop adding conflicting names; investigate the directory
OPEN waits Does another partition hold exclusive access? Ask the holder to CLOSE the channel
READ waits Are overlapping sectors locked? Identify the holder before interrupting it
Printer silent Is the association valid and hardware ready? Check device configuration and actual output
Prompt appears without login Are account components reachable? Check SISTEMA.CUENTA and CONECTAR.COMANDO

Run a filesystem check

After making a usable backup and arranging maintenance access, enter:

REPARAR S

This scans the volume. Read every diagnostic even if the final command result is 0. Checking can update a free-count field; it is not guaranteed to leave all metadata untouched.

The F option permits repair:

REPARAR S (F)

Caution — repair can remove directory metadata. Use F only after evaluating the check report. It can reconcile the bitmap and remove rejected entries or alter sharing redirects. It does not reconstruct arbitrary lost application records.

Respond to disk errors

The common disk path retries before prompting. R retries again. M invokes the driver's recovery entry and retries. I ignores the failed sector. Q aborts through interruption handling. An ignored error can appear to the caller as success despite missing or invalid data, so I is not a repair command.

A failed write can leave a directory count that exceeds the useful records actually stored. A structurally consistent file can also contain a partial final BASIC record after interruption. Successful CLOSE, a clean allocation count and complete application data are different properties.

Deleted and unfinished files

BORRAR releases allocation; later writes may reuse it. REVIVIR attempts catalogue recovery, but complete collision and recovery behavior is unspecified. Do not continue ordinary writing on a volume from which deleted data is needed.

Unfinished sequential output can have a chain of sectors while its directory counts remain zero. Do not initialize, reorganize or append to such a file merely to make its metadata look normal. Establish which records are complete first. Chapter 26 describes the write order and Chapter 27 describes disk-error handling.

Chapter 14 — Startup maintenance and ending work

Startup rebuilds some state from system files and creates other state in memory. Account connection then adds identity and optional command-file execution. Understanding these stages helps distinguish a missing component from an ordinary application menu.

Startup responsibilities

The bootstrap loads SISTEMA.NUCLEO. The executive initializes hardware and memory, loads SISTEMA.STARTUP and binds CSI, EXECLANG, EXEC work storage and MENSERR. SISTEMA.STARTUP is executable code, not a text command procedure.

CSI starts the connection path when SISTEMA.CUENTA and CONECTAR.COMANDO are accessible. After login, CONECTAR searches for an account-named EXEC file on S and then A–G, considering private and public candidates within each drive. A public S profile can therefore be selected before a private A profile. EXEC work capacity must be available for the automatic procedure.

Cold IPL.EXEC is a separate fallback when login components are unavailable. A command file does not run at every boot merely because its name sounds like a startup procedure. Chapter 21 gives the detailed order.

Inspect before changing configuration

Enter:

ESTADO USUARIOS

This shows connected users. Arrange for them to finish before changing shared facilities. Inspect associations separately:

ASOCIAR

This tells you which logical names currently select each device. Changes to terminal classes, drivers and partitions can affect memory as well as hardware behavior.

GENSIST saves selected current settings into SISTEMA.NUCLEO:

GENSIST

Caution — modifies the kernel file. Use this only when the current configuration is intentional and a usable backup exists. Complete persistence rules for every ASOCIAR/PONER combination are unspecified.

A status operation can update counters, EXEC can write a work frame, and BUZON removes retrieved messages. Plan maintenance according to these actual effects rather than treating every display-oriented command as read-only.

End work in order

Ask each user to save through the application, close its files and return to CSI. Each user then enters:

FIN

An authorized administrator can stop a logged-out partition with PARAR. This is session and partition management; it does not establish a global synchronization operation.

No complete whole-machine power-off procedure or universal shutdown command is specified here. Escape-I requests a restart and can discard pending output. Declining that restart also terminates the interrupted command. Neither is a substitute for application save and close. Do not promise users that a reset will complete their files.

Part IV — Programming

Chapter 15 — Your first BASIC program

BASIC lets you develop and run a program interactively from its source, then compile that source for later execution. In interactive use, you enter numbered lines at BASIC's prompt and ask the interpreter to run them. This makes it convenient to try an idea, correct a line and run the program again.

Compilation performs preparation ahead of execution and saves the result in a BASICCOM file. The compiler converts expressions and resolves source-line references into a form for the BASIC runtime, reducing work left to execution and allowing faster execution where that preparation matters. No fixed speed improvement is specified; the benefit depends on the program and its input/output. This is compilation before running, but it does not produce a statically linked, standalone Z80 program: the BASIC runtime remains necessary.

The following example takes the same greeting through both paths. You need permission to run BASIC, a writable S association and unused names HELLO.BASICOBJ and HELLO.BASICCOM.

1. Enter BASIC

At CSI enter:

BASIC

The dash prompt belongs to BASIC. Commands entered here are BASIC commands, not CSI commands.

2. Add a statement

Enter:

10 PRINT "HELLO WORLD"

10 is the line number. PRINT displays a value. The quotation marks make HELLO WORLD a literal string. Because the line begins with a number, BASIC stores it as program source rather than executing it immediately.

Enter the ending line:

20 END

END finishes program execution. It does not log you out or leave the BASIC editor.

3. Inspect the source

Enter:

LIST

LIST displays the stored source in line-number order. Check both lines. To replace a line, enter that line number with the corrected statement; do not add a second line with an unrelated number unless you intend a second operation.

4. Run it interactively

Enter:

RUN

The interpreter executes the source and displays:

HELLO WORLD

Control returns to BASIC after END. Running did not save the program.

5. Save the source

Enter:

SAVE HELLO:S

SAVE writes HELLO.BASICOBJ:S. Despite OBJ in the name, this is saved, tokenized BASIC source, not a Z80 linker object. Keep it for future edits.

Leave BASIC:

QUIT

QUIT returns to CSI. You can later re-enter BASIC and use LOAD HELLO:S to retrieve the saved source.

6. Compile

At CSI enter:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S

With an explicit saved-source filename, BASIC compiles to HELLO.BASICCOM:S. Read the compiler's diagnostics and require zero compilation errors before continuing. Use the explicit BASICOBJ type; do not assume BASIC and RUN have identical default types.

7. Run the compiled program

At CSI enter:

RUN HELLO.BASICCOM:S

RUN loads the compiled representation and executes it with the BASIC runtime. It displays the same greeting. BASICCOM is runtime code, not a standalone native Z80 executable.

Figure 15.1. The interpreted and compiled development paths.

Change and rebuild

Enter BASIC again. At its prompt enter:

LOAD HELLO:S

LOAD retrieves the saved source. Change line 10 by re-entering it with new text, LIST to inspect it, RUN to try it, and SAVE to retain it. After QUIT, compile again to update BASICCOM. Editing source does not automatically update an older compiled file.

Compiler options

To display a compilation listing, use:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (LIST)

LIST requests a console listing. XREF requests cross-references; NOLIST suppresses the listing; IMPRn directs it to printer n; OBJ=drive selects output placement.

NOOBJ suppresses BASICCOM generation. It does not itself request a source listing, and compiler statistics can still appear. To list source without producing compiled output, enter:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (LIST NOOBJ)

LIST controls source display; NOOBJ controls compiled output. An older BASICCOM can remain from a previous compilation. Running that older file does not show that this invocation generated a new one.

To display compilation passes and source line numbers, enter:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (TRACE)

This produces a compilation trace, including PASO # 1 through PASO # 8. It is separate from tracing execution of a BASIC program. RUN with TRACE produces no visible runtime-address trace for the greeting and numeric examples described in Appendix D.

The supplied help advertises a compiler COMMA option, but this version rejects both COMMA and COMA with Opcion no valida, CR = 8. Use dot-decimal source in these examples. The advertised RUN COMA option also leaves the displayed value of PRINT 1.5 as 1.5 in the demonstrated numeric program; no decimal-display change is established by that example. PRINT 1,5 supplies two items to PRINT, not a decimal-comma literal. Appendix D gives individual examples and the limits of each option.

Chapter 16 — BASIC values, decisions and loops

Once a program can print a greeting, the next step is to store values and choose what to do with them. Enter immediate examples at the BASIC dash prompt. Enter complete numbered programs one line at a time; save previous work before NEW clears the editor.

Assign and display a value

Enter:

A=7

Assignment stores a value in A. To see it, enter:

PRINT A

The program uses the stored value rather than the letter A as text. A name ending in $ holds a string; a name ending in % denotes an integer. Ordinary numeric variables use the real representation.

Enter:

PRINT 7/2

Integer operands give 3: division truncates toward zero. Now enter:

PRINT 7.0/2

The decimal operand selects real arithmetic and gives 3.5. Real values use packed decimal with thirteen significant digits, rather than IEEE binary floating point.

Store and join text

A string is a sequence of characters. Quotation marks enclose literal text, while a variable ending in $ holds a string value. At the BASIC prompt, enter:

S$="UN"

This stores UN in S$. Display its value:

PRINT S$

PRINT displays the stored characters. To join that value to another string, enter:

PRINT S$&"DOS"

The ampersand joins the two strings, producing UNDOS without inserting a separator. Later in this chapter, the same operation places a special separator between fields.

Make a choice

Save any source you want to keep, then clear the editor:

NEW

This starts a fresh program. Enter the following numbered lines one at a time:

10 A=7
20 IF A=7 THEN PRINT "SEVEN" ELSE PRINT "OTHER"
30 END

Line 10 assigns A. IF compares it with 7. THEN introduces the true action; ELSE introduces the alternative. END finishes. Change line 10 to a different value and RUN again to see the other path.

Count with FOR and NEXT

Save the previous example if needed, then start a fresh program:

NEW

Enter this loop one numbered line at a time:

10 FOR I%=1 TO 3
20 PRINT I%
30 NEXT I%
40 END

FOR sets the starting value and limit. PRINT uses the current counter. NEXT advances it and repeats while the range applies. The output values are 1, 2 and 3; after this loop I% is 4. STEP can supply an increment, including -1 for a descending loop. A skipped range still assigns its initial value.

Reuse a subroutine

A subroutine performs a task and returns. Save the previous example, use NEW to start a fresh program, then enter:

10 GOSUB 100
20 PRINT "BACK"
30 END
100 PRINT "SUB"
110 RETURN

GOSUB remembers where to resume and transfers to line 100. RETURN resumes at line 20. END prevents execution from falling into the subroutine a second time. GOTO differs: it transfers without creating that return point.

Computed ON chooses among a one-based target list. For example, ON 2 GOTO 100,200,300 selects line 200 in a program containing those targets. Selector zero produces ON Error Rango. An omitted middle entry, as in ON 2 GOTO 100,,300, continues after the statement.

Read embedded data

Start a fresh program after saving the preceding example. Enter:

10 DATA 11,2.5,"ABC",-4
20 READ A,B,S$,I%
30 PRINT A,B,S$,I%
40 RESTORE
50 READ A
60 END

DATA supplies literals in sequence. READ assigns them to variables. RESTORE returns the data cursor to the first DATA item; it does not undo other assignments. Reading past the available data raises No hay DATA.

Store an array

An array holds several values under one name; an index selects an individual element. Before declaring an array, select its lowest permitted index:

OPTION BASE 1

Then declare:

DIM ARR(2)

With BASE 1 the permitted indices are 1 and 2. Store an element:

ARR(1)=7

Read it:

PRINT ARR(1)

BASE 0 instead permits 0, 1 and 2 for DIM ARR(2). Set the base before declaration. Changing existing arrays and carrying this setting into another program through CHAIN have incompletely specified rules; they are outside this elementary example.

Extract a field from a string

A string can contain several fields separated by a special character. For example, two fields can hold UN and DOS. A delimiter marks the boundary; it is part of the stored string, not an instruction to print punctuation.

CHR$ constructs a one-character string from its numeric character code. This system's field delimiter has decimal code 222, or hexadecimal 0xDE. The following expression joins UN, that delimiter and DOS, then asks EXT$ for the second field. Enter:

PRINT EXT$("UN"&CHR$(222)&"DOS",2,0)

CHR$(222) supplies the separator and the ampersands join the pieces. EXT$ takes the complete string, field number 2 and subfield number 0. Zero in this last position requests the whole field, so the result is DOS. A literal caret is not the delimiter. A field can also be divided into subfields using code 0xDD; this example does not need that further division.

The next chapter applies these values and strings to files. BASIC can also call separately supplied extension modules, but that introduces another interface. Chapter 19 explains what those modules are before showing OPTION USR, and separately describes compiled-program privilege and serial restrictions. Appendix D provides the full statement and function reference.

Chapter 17 — Programming files

A program opens a file, transfers values, then closes it. This chapter first follows that cycle for a sequential file, where values are read in the order written. It then introduces direct access by record number, indexed access by key, and coordination between programs using the same files.

Use a writable S association and unused practice names LINES.DATOS, DIRECT.DATOS and INDEX.DATOS. You need permission to use BASIC and CREAR. Save any BASIC source you want to retain before leaving the interpreter. The file statements below are entered immediately at BASIC's dash prompt; CSI commands are identified separately.

Channels, records and opening modes

OPEN connects a file to a numbered channel. Later READ, WRITE and CLOSE statements use that number instead of repeating the filename. A BASIC channel is not a disk letter or a physical device number. These examples use channel 1 and close it before reusing it for another file.

A record is a group of stored values that a program transfers together. BASIC WRITE serializes the values with the information needed to read them back. Consequently a file written by BASIC is not necessarily ordinary text suitable for EDITAR, even when the value being written is a string.

The mode in OPEN states what access is requested. Its organization word states how records are selected. The examples below use these combinations:

Opening form Purpose in this chapter
OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL Create the new sequential practice file and write values in order
INPUT SEQUENTIAL Read existing sequential values in order
OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL, EXTEND Add output at the end of a correctly closed sequential file
UPDATE SEQUENTIAL Replace existing sequential contents; this is destructive
UPDATE DIRECT Read and write existing fixed-length records by number
UPDATE INDEXED Read and write existing indexed records by key

The word UPDATE does not promise the same effect for every organization. In particular, sequential replacement must be kept separate from direct or indexed updates.

Create and write a sequential file

If you are already at the BASIC dash prompt, continue with OPEN below. Otherwise, at CSI enter the interpreter:

BASIC

At its dash prompt, open the unused practice filename:

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL

OPEN connects the file to channel 1. The hash introduces the channel number, the colon separates it from the file specification, and the comma introduces the opening mode. This example requires an unused name; it is not a way to preserve an existing file's contents.

Write one string value:

WRITE #1: "FIRST"

WRITE sends FIRST through channel 1. Finish the output:

CLOSE #1

CLOSE completes the required output handling and releases the channel. Do not substitute QUIT for CLOSE. You remain at the BASIC prompt, ready to reopen the file for reading.

Read the saved value

Open the existing file for sequential input:

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", INPUT SEQUENTIAL

Read its next value into the string variable A$:

READ #1: A$

The channel selects the file; A$ receives its stored string. Display that value:

PRINT A$

The result is FIRST. READ changes the file position so that another READ asks for the next value rather than rereading the first one.

Recognize the end of a file

This practice file contains only the value just read. Request another:

READ #1: A$

There is no next value. Inspect the channel's end condition:

PRINT EOF(1)

EOF is the end-of-file query; its argument is the channel number. The result here is -1, indicating the end condition. Check this condition before treating a variable as newly read data. A missing record can set EOF without printing a separate diagnostic.

Close the input channel:

CLOSE #1

Append to a correctly closed file

The preceding output was closed normally. Reopen it to append:

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL, EXTEND

EXTEND is a trailing option. It positions output at the end of a valid closed sequential file. Add the next value:

WRITE #1: "TAIL"

Complete the output:

CLOSE #1

Do not READ before WRITE in this append workflow: the shared cursor can advance behind an EOF marker. Do not extend an unfinished file with a zero final-sector address. Extension trusts that field and can damage volume metadata if it is invalid. A correctly closed empty file has a nonzero final-sector address; it is not the same as unfinished output. Chapter 26 explains the storage details.

Check the appended contents

Open for input again:

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", INPUT SEQUENTIAL

Read the first value:

READ #1: A$

Display it:

PRINT A$

It is still FIRST. Read the next value:

READ #1: A$

Display that value separately:

PRINT A$

This time the result is TAIL. Appending retained the original value and added another after it. Close the input:

CLOSE #1

Replace sequential contents deliberately

Replacement is a different operation from appending. The following optional exercise empties the practice file you have just read.

Caution — UPDATE SEQUENTIAL replaces existing contents. OPEN can delete and recreate the old file before WRITE runs. Use only the expendable LINES.DATOS file from this example.

Open it in replacement mode:

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", UPDATE SEQUENTIAL

Close it without writing:

CLOSE #1

The file is now logically empty; FIRST and TAIL have not been retained. Reopening it with INPUT SEQUENTIAL, attempting a READ and checking EOF(1) reaches the end condition immediately. Replacement begins at OPEN, not at the first WRITE, which is why selecting the correct mode matters before any data transfer.

Create a direct file

Direct access selects a fixed-length record by its number, rather than reading all preceding records. The file must first be created with the required record size and capacity. With all channels closed, return from BASIC to CSI:

QUIT

Using the unused practice name DIRECT.DATOS, enter:

CREAR DIRECT.DATOS:S (DIRECTO REGISTRO 300 NUMREG 3)

CREAR makes three fixed-length records of 300 bytes. DIRECTO selects direct organization, REGISTRO 300 specifies the bytes per record, and NUMREG 3 requests three records. Direct organization lets a program select a record by number. Enter BASIC again:

BASIC

At its dash prompt, open the file:

OPEN #1: "DIRECT.DATOS:S", UPDATE DIRECT

#1 selects the channel. UPDATE DIRECT permits reading and writing the existing direct-file records. Write record 2:

WRITE #1,2: "SECOND"

The number after the channel selects the record; the colon introduces its data. Read the same record:

READ #1,2: A$

READ places the stored string in A$. Display it:

PRINT A$

The result is SECOND. Finish the file operation:

CLOSE #1

CLOSE releases the open channel and completes its required output handling. Do not substitute QUIT for CLOSE. Record numbering starts at 1; reading past the configured range reaches the end condition, available through EOF(1).

Create and use an indexed file

An indexed file identifies each record by a key, such as an account code. The index also supports reading records in key order. This differs from selecting a direct record by its number.

After closing the direct file, return to CSI:

QUIT

Create the unused practice file INDEX.DATOS:

CREAR INDEX.DATOS:S (INDEXADO REGISTRO 32 NUMREG 16 LONCLA 4)

INDEXADO requests indexed organization, REGISTRO 32 selects record length, NUMREG requests capacity and LONCLA 4 selects a four-character key. The allocated slot capacity can exceed the requested count; a request for 16 produces 19 slots in this case.

Enter the interpreter:

BASIC

At the BASIC prompt, open the file:

OPEN #1: "INDEX.DATOS:S", UPDATE INDEXED

Write one keyed record:

WRITE #1,"AAAA": "FIRST"

The key identifies the record. Add another separately:

WRITE #1,"MMMM": "MIDDLE"

Read by key:

READ #1,"AAAA": A$

Display the retrieved value:

PRINT A$

The result is FIRST. READNEXT provides ordered traversal for indexed files. A missing key sets the EOF condition without necessarily printing a diagnostic. Close after use:

CLOSE #1

CLAVE creation and UPDATE KEYED provide keyed access without the I-format ordered index area. Do not assume their on-disk layouts are identical.

File protection and BASIC modes

The administrator can set independent read, write and deletion restrictions, as taught in Chapter 11. The table below connects those restrictions to the opening modes you have now used. It describes the current owner with each protection bit separately.

Individual protection INPUT UPDATE SEQUENTIAL replacement EXTEND
E, write Allowed Allowed through deletion/recreation Refused
B, deletion Allowed Refused Allowed
L, read Refused Allowed through deletion/recreation Refused

Sequential replacement can succeed despite write protection because its path deletes and recreates the entry. Combined attributes and shared access can impose further restrictions; do not infer their full behavior from a row that applies only one protection.

Coordinate shared access

An exclusive open prevents a competing opener from entering the same file until the holder closes it. Record locks instead coordinate access to parts of an open file. For the existing direct practice file, this form requests exclusive opening:

OPEN #1: "DIRECT.DATOS:S", UPDATE DIRECT, LOCK

A competing open can wait until the holder closes. UNLOCK is different:

UNLOCK #1

UNLOCK releases locked record spans, not the exclusive OPEN ownership. To finish the exclusive access, enter:

CLOSE #1

Record locks cover sectors, so two different 300-byte records can overlap a sector.

Ownership, sharing grants, open admission and record locks are separate rules. An owner can delete a file despite another partition holding an exclusive open. Avoid deleting active shared files.

Check the right completion condition

EOF, command result and physical write completion belong to different layers. A BASIC WRITE can require multiple kernel transfers, and interruption can leave a partial final BASIC record. Explicit CLOSE is essential, but ignored disk errors can still produce incomplete stored data. Handle reported errors and validate the records your application requires.

Chapter 18 — Writing Z80 machine-code programs

The Secoinsa Serie 20 computer uses a Z80 processor. A native machine-code program consists of instruction bytes executed directly by that processor. Assembly notation gives those bytes readable names, such as LD for loading a register and JP for a jump. This chapter explains a small greeting program, its exact bytes and the format the operating system's loader expects.

No native general-purpose assembler or linker command is specified here. The assembly listing explains the required machine code; it is not a complete installed edit–assemble–link procedure to type at CSI. Chapter 15 provides the complete BASIC development workflow. Here, the aim is to understand how to write the native instructions and represent them as a loadable program.

Registers, labels and operating-system requests

Registers hold values used by the processor. This example uses A for a command result and the DE register pair for the address of some text. A label names a position in the program so that an instruction can refer to it without spelling its numeric address.

To request an operating-system service, the program executes RST 08h followed by a byte selecting the operation. The executive reads that byte and carries out the request. Service 0x02 displays a string ending in a carriage return; service 0x00 ends the command using the result in A. Chapter 19 develops this calling convention with more examples.

Write the greeting

The program below prints HELLO Z80 and ends with command result 0. It has no open files, so it does not demonstrate completion of buffered file output. The names start and message are labels chosen for this listing.

        jp      start
start:  ld      de,message
        rst     08h
        db      02h
        xor     a
        rst     08h
        db      00h
message: db     "HELLO Z80",13,0

Understand each instruction

JP start transfers execution to the label start. It provides a conventional entry jump, although the file format does not require one.

LD DE,message places the address of the text in the DE register pair. LD copies a value; it does not print anything.

RST 08h enters the executive dispatcher. The following DB 02h emits the service selector byte. DB is an assembler data directive, not a processor instruction. Together these bytes request text output using DE.

XOR A clears A to zero. The following RST/DB pair selects service 0x00, which ends the command using that zero completion result.

The final DB directive emits the literal characters, a carriage return (13), and a zero byte. A label identifies the address of the following bytes; it does not allocate an extra byte.

Read the machine-code bytes

The complete program is 22 bytes. With its addresses initially expressed relative to zero, it is:

C3 03 00 11 0B 00 CF 02 AF CF 00 48 45 4C 4C 4F 20 5A 38 30 0D 00

The first three bytes, C3 03 00, encode the jump to start at offset 3. The next three, 11 0B 00, load DE with the message address at offset 11. The two CF bytes encode RST 08h; each is followed by its service selector. The character bytes and terminators follow the instructions.

The final service ends the command; it does not return to CSI through an ordinary application RET. Set the result explicitly rather than treating a processor return instruction as the operating-system exit interface.

Why addresses need relocation

The loader can place the program at different memory addresses. An address written as an offset from zero must therefore be adjusted to the chosen load address. This adjustment is relocation. An R-format executable supplies a list telling the loader which address words to adjust; ordinary data bytes and service numbers must not be changed.

In this example, the JP operand and the LD DE operand contain addresses. Their two-byte words begin at program offsets 0x0001 and 0x0004. Adding the load base to these words preserves the relationships between the instructions and their targets wherever the program is placed.

Package the program for the loader

The executable stores its program bytes in sectors, followed by the relocation information. Pad the 22-byte image to a 256-byte sector. A second sector begins:

02 01 00 04 00

The first byte says there are two relocation offsets. Each offset is a little-endian 16-bit value (LE16): its low byte is stored first. The offsets 0x0001 and 0x0004 identify the JP and LD DE address words. For each, the loader adds the selected load base modulo 65536. The directory records R format, two stored sectors, one allocated 1024-byte block and image length 0x0016.

For example, at a load base 0x5157, the relocated prefix becomes C3 5A 51 11 62 51; the message lies at 0x5162. The resulting program prints HELLO Z80 with CR 0. The base is not universal. Each partition reserves a context area for its state and workspaces before the program. Ordinary loading places code 0x04CC bytes after that context begins. Chapter 20 explains this memory arrangement.

An R image has no mandatory external header or JP magic. Many utilities begin with a jump and version text, but STARTUP begins with register saves. The directory supplies the length and format. BASICOBJ is not a linker object and BASICCOM is not this executable format.

Runtime responsibilities

Use each service's particular register and flag contract. The dispatcher preserves input registers while selecting a target, but the selected routine can modify them. Do not assume Unix-like arguments, a universal stack frame, uniform success in A, or automatic channel closure.

The partition's memory ceiling is the upper boundary available to commands and their stacks. Service 0x37 queries this mutable boundary, which CSI uses to establish the stack; 0x38 changes it. Drivers and classes can lower that ceiling. An SP or entry register value from one loader path is not a complete ABI for every application. Chapter 30 follows known loader placement and termination without inventing the unresolved initial-register contract.

Chapter 19 — Using system services

A system service performs an operating-system operation for a program: output a character, obtain the current privilege, open a file or wait for an event. The program selects the service and supplies its inputs; the service returns the documented result or changes execution as specified. This chapter begins with visible output before introducing file requests, partition state and asynchronous operations.

Native programs use the Z80 calling form introduced in Chapter 18: RST 08h followed by one byte selecting the service. Registers supply many of the arguments and receive results. BASIC extensions provide another route for selected operations and are introduced separately below. Neither an assembly fragment nor an extension call is a command to type at CSI.

Three elementary calls

The fragments below assume execution inside a valid partition with its normal stack and console. They are assembly notation, not CSI commands or complete standalone programs. Chapter 18 explains the instructions and complete bytes of a native greeting; use that context when reading these fragments.

To display one character, put its character code in C:

ld c,65
rst 08h
db 05h

LD prepares ASCII A, RST enters the dispatcher, and DB supplies the selector for console character output. This route applies ordinary output policy, so suppressed output can remain invisible.

To start a new line, use:

rst 08h
db 12h

The newline service needs no explicit character buffer. It is separate from printing a numeric byte value as text.

To obtain the current privilege, use:

rst 08h
db 5Ch

The returned A contains the privilege byte. The call does not print it or change identity. A program that wants a decimal display must separately format the value and send the resulting characters to the console.

Read a service's calling contract

For each service, identify the selector number, input registers, memory that the caller must prepare, result registers and error indication. A pointer is an address of data in memory; returning a pointer does not display the data. Registers that must survive a call need an explicit preservation guarantee.

The Z80 also returns condition flags, including carry and zero (Z). Their meaning depends on the service. For example, a queue read can distinguish an empty queue from a stored zero byte through carry, even though the data value alone is zero in both cases. Do not assume a universal success code in A or the same meaning for every flag.

The dispatcher reads the inline selector and ordinarily resumes after that byte when the service returns. The installed table covers 0x00–0x6E; no range check is established, so use only documented entries. Chapter 32 gives each contract. Its service names are descriptive labels, while the numbers and target addresses identify the actual interfaces. Chapter 24 explains dispatch and the distinction between resident and overlay implementations.

Console input and device output

An output request can pass through several layers. Console policy decides whether ordinary output is suppressed or copied to a printer. A terminal class translates cursor operations into terminal-specific sequences. A driver handles the device itself. Choose the layer whose behavior the program needs; a raw output call does not automatically perform the work of the higher layers.

Service 0x05 applies console policy, including Escape-O suppression and printer copying. Service 0x07 applies kernel cursor controls and terminal-class translation while bypassing that suppression. Service 0x40 with console association 9 reaches the raw driver and does not update tracked cursor coordinates. Three calls to 0x07 carrying 0x10, column, row perform the documented coordinate operation.

A buffer is caller-provided memory in which input can be stored. The input budget limits the number of accepted characters, but space can also be needed for terminators. Line input 0x01 takes DE as the buffer address and B as the budget. Allocate room for terminators: an exhausted five-byte budget stores five characters plus CR; four characters followed by typed CR store that CR and an additional CR. A reports accepted count; DE and BC change. B=0 and arbitrary editing paths are not fully characterized.

The ALP family supplies serial-terminal drivers. DMA is direct memory access, used here for output transfers; DMA readiness and available input are different conditions. Device status is family-specific. ALP status returns A=0 in both empty and queued cases; Z tests input occupancy, carry tests DMA readiness. Its status call can flush pending output. Input 0x3F can return a real NUL with the same AF as an unassigned association, so establish the association independently. Event wait 0x6B can wake without a character; recheck the condition. It returns with interrupts enabled in the specified paths.

File channels and storage requests

The BASIC channel operations in Chapter 17 have lower-level service counterparts. A native caller must prepare the channel identity and provide a request describing the operation and its workspace. A workspace is memory used during the operation; its pointer is the address of that memory. These objects must exist before OPEN is called.

Initialize a channel name/class through 0x18, then pass a writable four-byte request to OPEN 0x20. It contains the channel number, opening-mode byte and a two-byte little-endian workspace pointer. OPEN modes and channel status are distinct fields; one must not be substituted for the other. OPEN can modify mode bits. Services 0x21–0x23, 0x28–0x2D and 0x5E/0x5F provide closure, record and counted-byte operations. Appendix E gives organization-specific channel tails; do not interpret an indexed tail as a sequential cursor.

Directory lookup 0x14 normalizes a writable drive/name/type request on every retry. A=0 with the established flags means found; A=1 can mean absent with a reusable slot. Carry-set failure may leave HL as a hash or last probe address, not an entry pointer. Classify the return before dereferencing it.

Allocation reserves disk blocks for a file. A contiguous allocation is one unbroken run of blocks, rather than the same total spread across several gaps. The allocator distinguishes exact contiguous allocation 0x1B from minimum-size allocation 0x63. Freeing 0x1C changes bits and counters without a demonstrated double-free or bounds guarantee. Direct sector access bypasses application-level record validation and retains the common retry/ignore policy.

Obtain the current partition's context

The executive keeps a context for each partition: a memory structure containing its identity, memory boundaries, channels and execution state. Code that needs that structure first obtains its address. Ordinary character-output calls do not require the caller to decode it.

Service 0x30 returns the current context address in IY, a Z80 index register:

rst 08h
db 30h

IY now provides a base for documented context fields; the call itself prints nothing. Field offsets are measured from that base. Chapter 20 explains the memory arrangement and Appendix E defines the fields. Do not assume every use of IY in every component denotes this structure.

Call a system-related extension from BASIC

A BASIC extension is a separately supplied machine-code module that the BASIC runtime calls on a program's behalf. Selecting a module and calling it are separate steps. The module has its own argument and result convention; it is not selected by a native system-service number.

For this example, enter BASIC at CSI if it is not already active:

BASIC

At the BASIC dash prompt, select the privilege getter:

OPTION USR "GETPRIV"

This selects GETPRIV.BASICUSR. Call its entry at offset 0 with numeric argument 0:

PRINT USR(0,0)

The first zero selects the entry at the beginning of this module; the second is the numeric argument required by this getter. The result is the current privilege, which PRINT displays. Other modules require their own interfaces; these arguments are not a general template for arbitrary extensions.

GETID similarly returns the account number. GETUSER uses an eight-byte string workspace to return the account name; its complete example is in Appendix C. Extension modules return through BASIC's wrapper rather than ending the CSI command. This differs from the native greeting in Chapter 18, whose service 0x00 terminates the command.

Set a compiled BASIC program's requirements

An application can require a minimum privilege or a matching installation serial before continuing. These are restrictions on execution, not ways to grant authority to its caller.

OPTION PRIV sets the minimum runtime privilege for a compiled BASIC program. OPTION SERIAL imposes a serial binding. The exact serial value is system-specific and must not be copied from an unrelated configuration. Their individual forms are given in OPTION PRIV and OPTION SERIAL. The checks belong to the BASIC runtime, not to the native service selector in the preceding assembly fragments.

Arrange a callback

A callback is a routine entered by the system when an event or requested delay occurs. Its code can run later than the request that registered it. The caller must therefore keep the callback code and its required storage valid, and must understand which partition supplies the execution context.

Timer service 0x35 uses six writable bytes followed by callback code, converts the supplied delay to eight-millisecond ticks, and invokes the callback in its owning partition. Normal A=0 operation is documented; nonzero-A ownership exceptions do not establish general cross-bank cancellation. Chapter 25 explains the node layout, timing and entry paths.

Coordinate access with locks

A lock records ownership so that another partition can wait rather than use the protected resource at the same time. A wakeup tells a waiter to check again; it does not automatically transfer ownership to that waiter.

Byte locks 0x54/0x55 use a one-byte owner tag. Release broadcasts to waiters, which must recheck their own lock. Repeated same-owner acquisition adds no recursion count. File-open admission and record spans are different structures with different cleanup rules; Chapter 17 explains their programmer-visible distinction.

Exchange bytes through a memory queue

A queue stores bytes until a consumer removes them. The queue services use a caller-provided header and payload area rather than allocating that storage themselves. Capacity, occupancy and read position are distinct header values.

Services 0x47/0x48 use this caller storage. Test carry for empty reads: successful zero data also sets Z. Do not append to a full queue; the original cached-full loop cannot notice an ordinary consumer. Code-derived capacities 1–128 avoid the demonstrated eight-bit indexing defects, but do not establish complete concurrent safety. Chapter 25 describes the algorithm and its limits; Appendix E gives the layout.

Distinguish banked copying from callback entry

Banking selects which memory is visible to the processor. Copying bytes through another partition's bank and executing a routine in that partition are different operations. These advanced interfaces require the memory and scheduler model in Chapter 20 and Chapter 22; their register-level contracts are in Chapter 32.

Services 0x68/0x69 copy through a scheduler record's bank, not an A-selected physical bank number. They advance HL/DE, return BC=0 and disable interrupts on the specified returns. A zero count is not an empty-copy convention. Callback 0x56 instead changes execution context and can clear stopped/wait state; it is not equivalent to a memory copy.

Interpret numeric-helper results

Arithmetic helpers use registers to supply operands and return results. Error handling matters as much as the ordinary result: an overflow result can be an intermediate value rather than a truncated answer.

Unsigned multiply 0x27 must be specified for carry. On overflow, HL can hold an early partial accumulator, not the low sixteen product bits. 0x9000 × 3 returns 0x9000 with carry, while reversed operands return 0x3000 with carry. Division 0x26 by zero returns HL=0, DE=the original dividend and carry, without a trap.

Numeric parsers can return carry clear without consuming any digit. They do not skip spaces or accept signs. Validate consumed and trailing text as well as carry. Overflow consumes the offending digit and returns an intermediate partial result.

Handle a disk-error callback

A disk-error callback lets a program respond during a failed disk operation. It runs within that operation's locking and register context, so the ordinary retry, ignore and abort meanings must be understood before installing one.

A disk-error callback registered by 0x4A runs while the disk lock is held. Returning A=0 consumes the failure; positive A supplies the normal error/retry policy; negative A aborts. Preserve the operation's working registers. Reentrant disk I/O inside that callback is not established. Clearing the cause and returning zero is not a retry.

Chapter 27 explains the retry path and its consequences for stored data. Use the exact service entry, data layout and failure qualifications together; the services do not supply one uniform return or cleanup convention.

Part V — System fundamentals

Chapter 20 — Organization and memory

The system divides its work between a common resident executive, banked partition memory, replaceable service overlays and ordinary files containing commands and drivers. Understanding these boundaries explains why a utility can disappear from memory after execution while its open-file state remains visible to the executive, and why an address alone does not always identify a byte of RAM.

Common and banked memory

The Z80 presents a 16-bit address space. Addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF are common to the partitions. The interval 0x4000–0xFFFF selects one of five 48-KiB banks in a 256-KiB configuration: 16 + 5 × 48 = 256 KiB. The low bank-selection bits of port 0xFD select the bank; bit 7 controls the ROM mapping at 0x8000. An emulator must preserve common storage while changing the selected upper region. A flat 64-KiB model cannot implement that configuration.

Figure 20.1. The CPU window and its backing storage. The diagram describes mapping, not simultaneous visibility of all banks.

Resident components and partition allocation

The NUCLEO body occupies 0x0000–0x39C1; the resident spooler follows at 0x39C2–0x4C8A. Their combined extent is 19,595 bytes. The first partition context begins at 0x4C8B and its program area at 0x5157. The boot file includes further initialization and overlay material; the native resident extent is not the same measurement as its 113 stored sectors.

A partition context reserves 0x4CC bytes before executable code. Its first 0xAC bytes hold identity, callback, console and command state. Seventeen 32-byte channels follow, then a 256-byte sector workspace and a 256-byte command buffer. Consequently the executable start is context + 0x4CC. A memory ceiling in the context bounds the upper end available to commands and their stacks. Drivers and terminal classes can be loaded downwards from that ceiling.

Loaded addresses are therefore configuration-dependent. Replacing a terminal class can change the ceiling; repeated C1 → C2 → C1 replacement was observed to reduce it from 0xFB64 to 0xF919 and then 0xF6CE. Treat a published load address as an address in a stated configuration, not a universal link address.

Overlays and ordinary commands

The RST 0x08 dispatcher and frequently used routines remain common. Less frequently used file and loader services are reached through stubs which select an overlay at 0x424A. Overlay 1 supplies indexed/keyed operations, overlay 2 supplies channel and sequential/direct operations, and overlay 3 supplies loading, termination, messages and initialization. The selected bytes at an overlay address must be known before disassembling that address.

CSI, the command processor, is an executable loaded into the current partition. Most named commands are separate .COMANDO files. BASIC has its own load file, overlays and runtime. EXEC is a separate interpreter with persistent level frames. These are cooperating components, not built-in syntax of one monolithic command processor.

The small common named-component table at 0x04BC provides exact eight-byte lookup. Its named spooler entry is SPOOLER → 0x39C2. It is not a directory, scheduler table or general symbol table. Chapter 24 describes its layout and lookup limitations.

See Appendix B for the canonical address and I/O maps, and Appendix E for context layouts.

Chapter 21 — Boot architecture

Startup transforms a physical sector stream into a banked multiuser environment. The physical bootstrap and the logical filesystem use different addressing rules. Keeping those stages separate is essential when rebuilding a disk or implementing the controller.

Firmware and the first loader

The firmware is 4,096 bytes mapped at 0x8000, with a reset-time alias at zero. Its initial jump bytes are C3 03 80, transferring control to 0x8003. Firmware reads physical cylinder 0, head 0, sectors 0–3 into memory beginning at 0x0100. Those four sectors form a 1,024-byte disk loader.

The loader relocates itself to 0xA000 and enters at 0xA023. An observed earlier SP is 0x3FFD; the relocated loader subsequently uses SP=0xA600 and IY=0xA700. These successive values belong to different stages and must not be presented as conflicting reset-stack specifications.

Physical sectors 4 and 5 contain volume descriptors. The loader uses the system-volume mapping to obtain the kernel. The loader obtains the kernel start from its catalogue entry. For example, a 113-sector kernel occupies 28,928 bytes (0x7100), first read at 0x0100. Its location is an allocation value, not a universal fixed sector. The following downward relocation copies 0x9000 bytes to zero, including memory beyond the bytes just read. That extra copied region must not be described as initialized kernel-file content.

Figure 21.1. The documented disk boot path. Authentication occurs after CSI has been loaded.

Executive initialization

Before initialization, the newly relocated kernel's RST 0x00 vector jumps to 0x5157. Initialization changes the vector; at the observed login state it jumps to 0x34F7. SP is observed at 0xA5FE before initialization establishes its own stack at 0x5157. Initialization relocates overlay material into bank 4, installs common service pointers and constructs the available partition records. Service 0x0C illustrates the distinction between startup and running state: the disk table initially points to 0x5157; the live table points to 0x331B, a routine returning HL=0x5542. The meaning of that signature has not been established.

Service 0x4B with B=0xFF performs cold system-volume initialization. It locates NUCLEO, CSI, EXECLANG, EXEC1 and MENSERR, caches their positions and writes clock state into the kernel file. Startup therefore updates system state on disk before ordinary user work begins.

Account startup and profiles

CSI is loaded before account authentication. Startup uses account maintenance and connection components with a temporary owner value of 0xFF before restoring account 0 for CONECTAR. Missing account components can leave the environment at account 0, privilege 5, with an unresolved displayed name and then attempt IPL.EXEC or the prompt. This failure path does not provide a sound account-recovery procedure.

A connected account can have an EXEC profile named after it. The search visits S and then A–G, considering private and public candidates on each associated drive. Public S can therefore outrank a private A profile. The first matching nonsequential candidate stops the search instead of continuing to a later valid script; zero-capacity candidates are skipped. A profile must have the right format as well as the right spelling.

An explicit EXEC failure can mention EXEC.COMANDO, although the separate EXECLANG interpreter is involved. Diagnose the complete search and file format before concluding that a command executable has disappeared.

Restart is a new boot

The accepted Escape-I path reaches 0x2D01, writes 0x80 to port 0xFD and jumps into ROM at 0x8000. It reruns firmware diagnostics, physical bootstrap and cold initialization. Declining with N calls service 0x00 and ends the interrupted command; it does not resume it. Neither route supplies an implicit CLOSE for buffered application output. The operational consequences are described in Chapter 14.

Chapter 22 — Partitions, identity and protection

The partition is the system's scheduled execution environment. An account supplies identity and authority within that environment. These concepts are related during login but are not interchangeable: changing account does not allocate a new memory bank, and a lock owner is not an account number.

Three identifiers

The common scheduler uses a zero-based record index. Each three-byte record contains a context pointer and a bank/state byte. User commands display partition numbers using their own convention, commonly beginning at 1. The bank is the low nibble of the record's third byte. It can differ from the record index: record 1 can select bank 2.

The account number is context byte +0x1A. The account name occupies +0x12–+0x19, and privilege is +0x11. Lock structures instead store scheduler index + 1 so that zero can mean unowned. An engineer who decodes owner byte 2 as account 2 will misattribute synchronization state.

Scheduling and waiting

Multiple partitions are preempted by the timer; a computation containing no OS calls does not monopolize the processor. Programs can also yield through service 0x4F. Ordinary selection scans circularly for the first configured record whose high state nibble is zero. There is no specified per-record priority comparison in that path.

State bit Meaning in the established paths
4 Waiting for a byte lock
5 Waiting for a device event
6 Disk-related wait state
7 Stopped partition

These are bits, not a set of mutually exclusive state numbers. Serial receive callbacks and cross-partition callback entry can clear the whole high nibble. A callback to a stopped, allocated partition can thus resume its dormant yield loop without restoring its console. Chapter 25 gives the exact selection, saved-frame and timer behavior.

Figure 22.1. Selected documented transitions. The diagram does not prescribe a universal state machine for every combination of bits.

Protection boundaries

The system combines account ownership, public-file lookup, sharing aliases, file protection bits, command minimum privileges and software serial bindings. They protect different operations. The standard command prologue checks privilege and installation serial before the utility body; the utility may impose additional checks. BASIC implements corresponding runtime guards of its own.

File protection uses read, deletion and write bits in the directory format byte. The observed sequential replacement operation first deletes the old file. Consequently write protection alone can permit replacement while preventing append. Deletion protection alone prevents replacement but permits append. Read protection prevents INPUT and the established append path; it does not prevent same-owner replacement. Chapter 11 explains the administrative operations; Chapter 17 gives the BASIC-mode protection matrix and its limits for combined attributes.

An exclusive open controls admission to an open-file table. It does not reliably prevent native BORRAR from deleting the file: the deletion path calls the exclusive-admission helper but fails to test its carry before proceeding. The existing channel can survive while its blocks have been freed. This defect makes an accurate account of the behavior particularly important for compatible implementations.

No hardware-enforced kernel/user protection boundary has been established. Programs contain ordinary Z80 instructions and can call common routines; several low-level services perform no index or pointer validation. The historical design relies substantially on trusted programs and command-level discipline. This observation describes the specified architecture rather than a modern security certification.

Chapter 23 — Storage and device concepts

Files are catalogue entries interpreted according to a format byte. The catalogue identifies ownership and allocation, while an application's record format gives meaning to the bytes within that allocation. These layers explain why a structurally readable file can contain invalid BASIC records, or why a successful filesystem check can leave an application unusable.

Allocation, sectors and records

A logical sector is 256 bytes. The allocation bitmap manages blocks of four sectors, or 1,024 bytes. A file can therefore own more storage than its current payload consumes. Sequential files reserve the last two bytes of each sector for a link, leaving 254 payload bytes. Direct files instead expose fixed-length numbered records over full-sector storage; a 300-byte record necessarily crosses sector boundaries.

R and A executable formats use contiguous storage. An R file carries relocation records after its image; an A file carries an absolute load address in its directory entry. I files combine an ordered slot index with key/data/next-link slots. K files use hashed fixed slots without that prefix. None of those distinctions is encoded solely by the filename type.

Figure 23.1. Filesystem layout for a volume with 151 directory sectors beginning at sector 9. These are configuration values, not universal defaults.

For the example geometry, the directory holds 151 sectors × 8 entries = 1,208 records. Its exact-name lookup is a probing algorithm rather than a scan of every record. A defect can exhaust a short repeating probe path while many catalogue entries remain unused. Wildcard listing scans all directory sectors and can expose entries that exact lookup misses. Chapter 26 distinguishes physical presence, catalogue reachability and application readability.

Logical and physical devices

Each partition has a 24-byte association map. An association names a use, such as S, console input or IMPR1; its byte selects a physical device descriptor. A descriptor in turn identifies a driver and device-specific parameters. These three levels let two partitions use the same logical name for different hardware without changing application code.

Figure 23.2. Device resolution. Descriptor fields form a family-dependent union; character and disk entry offsets do not imply identical functions.

Character drivers normally provide status, input, output, initialization and termination at offsets +0, +3, +6, +9 and +0x0C. The floppy drivers have four entries with different meanings: select/configure, recalibration request, read and write. Their byte at +0x0C is data, not a fifth jump. DIS10's public character stubs do not perform tape records; utilities call a private interface at driver +0x0F.

The terminal class translates the OS's logical controls into a terminal's output sequences. A raw device output service bypasses that translation and the executive's tracked cursor. The spooler supplies another layer by replacing printer output with a resident disk collector. Accepted output at any of these layers is not proof that a physical printer or serial peer received it.

Part VI — Operating system internals

Chapter 24 — Kernel entry and shared structures

Applications enter the executive through an inline-number restart convention. The common dispatcher is small; most complexity lies in the service-specific code and in the context selected when that code executes. This chapter connects the calling convention to the resident and overlay maps.

The RST 0x08 path

A call consists of opcode 0xCF followed immediately by a one-byte service number. RST pushes the address of that inline byte. The vector jumps to 0x08BD, where the dispatcher saves working HL, DE and AF, fetches the service number and advances the return address past it. A word table at 0x08E0–0x09BD selects one of 111 entries, numbered 0x00–0x6E.

There is no established bounds check for a larger number. The presence of a byte-sized selector does not establish 256 services. Symbolic names in Chapter 32 are descriptive editorial names unless an original name is explicitly identified.

Figure 24.1. Service dispatch. A service that terminates or replaces a command does not follow the ordinary return arrow.

Stubs at 0x071F–0x0791 call 0x0792 and carry inline overlay-selection information. Overlay descriptors at 0x0713, 0x0717 and 0x071B are patched during startup. Thus the stored kernel code and initialized memory can legitimately show different contents in this area. Address tables in Appendix B identify their scope.

Context and channel storage

The current context pointer is the little-endian word at 0x0033. Service 0x30 returns it in IY. Service 0x50 instead returns IY=0, enabling access to common globals. Do not decode every IY-relative instruction as a partition field: driver code, BASIC and temporary wrappers use indexed registers for other structures.

Within a context, +0x00 is saved SP, +0x02 is the context base, +0x04 is the current memory ceiling and +0x06 is the allocated endpoint, with zero representing 0x10000. The sector workspace begins at +0x2CC and the command buffer at +0x3CC. The complete byte atlas in Appendix E keeps unknown fields explicitly unknown.

Service 0x18 initializes one of seventeen channel records by copying eighteen bytes of drive, filename, type and class, then clearing fourteen bytes. OPEN consumes a separate four-byte caller request containing channel, mode and a little-endian workspace pointer. The request is mutable: the executive and BASIC caller can both change its mode. An internal channel's status byte must not be substituted for that request byte.

Shared tables

The common scheduler rows begin at 0x048C. Record-lock rows begin at 0x1B15; file-open rows begin at 0x1DA8. Their owners all relate to execution state but their lengths and keys differ. The record-lock table's 65 initial rows include its terminating row, leaving 64 usable rows by code and layout. The file-open region contains 129 four-byte rows; a practical maximum supported by a saturation test has not been established.

The named-component table at 0x04BC has ten-byte rows: eight key bytes and a little-endian address. Service 0x6C compares all eight bytes exactly and stops when a row's first byte is zero or space. The initialized image has eight slots followed by a zero at 0x050C. The routine does not impose an independent count limit, store a bank or owner, or normalize case. Exact SPOOLER returns 0x39C2; a NUL-padded or lowercase spelling fails. In the established interface, success is AF=0x0044 and HL=address; absence is AF=0x0101 with the caller's HL preserved.

Interrupt entry is a separate interface

The reconstructed hardware uses IM 0 supplied restart opcodes. The timer enters through RST 0x10, the disk interrupt through RST 0x20 and the ALP serial path through RST 0x28. RST 0x18 is used by the tape-related path; RST 0x38 can invoke a per-context inline hook beginning at +0x39. These are not extra RST 0x08 service numbers.

A suspended scheduler frame contains ten register words and a continuation PC, but it is not a complete description of every interrupt's immediate stack. Appendix E documents this frame and Appendix B the observed vectors. Physical interrupt arbitration beyond the implemented and observed paths remains incomplete.

Chapter 25 — Scheduling, callbacks and synchronization

The scheduler switches a saved context, not merely a bank number. Its behavior also depends on shared wait bits and callback state. Those dependencies matter both to programs that coordinate work and to emulators that must reproduce a blocked application's next instruction.

Ordinary selection and quantum

Service 0x4F enters 0x0558. With one configured partition it preserves AF and returns after enabling interrupts. With several partitions it saves main and alternate registers, stores SP in context +0x00, switches to the common stack and scans three-byte rows. It selects the first eligible row after the scan origin, wrapping at the configured count. With none eligible, it restores the previous saved state, enables interrupts and HALTs before retrying.

The selected row updates current index 0x000C, current-row pointer 0x048A and context pointer 0x0033. Mapping proceeds through 0x21DE; the selected high state nibble is cleared. Restore at 0x062F–0x063E pops HL′, DE′, BC′, AF′, IY, IX, HL, DE, BC and AF, enables interrupts and returns through the saved continuation.

The timer subtracts 8 from the remaining quantum byte at 0x0554 and branches on sign or zero. This byte arithmetic has visible consequences. The measured TIEMPO values 10, 128, 135 and 136 produce 2, 16, 17 and 1 timer decrements respectively: approximately 16, 128, 136 and 8 milliseconds in the reconstructed timer configuration. A value of 136 does not mean a longer slice than 135. The 8-ms period follows the installed timer setup; arbitrary hardware clock variations remain separate.

Callback entry saves the remaining quantum in 0x0556 and sets hint 0x0555. If the saved amount is at least 4, the next special preparation backs the ordinary origin up one row and reuses that residual; otherwise it reloads the default at 0x000B. The origin at 0x0551/0x0553 can therefore differ from the currently executing partition. These globals are not per-partition priorities.

Callback context

Service 0x56 takes a target record index in A's low seven bits and a callback address in HL. Bit 7 suppresses the final interrupt-enable instruction. Cross-partition entry saves the sender, selects the target bank, restores the target's register frame and jumps to the callback with its continuation still on the stack. A callback RET resumes the target computation. The sender's seeded registers are not supplied as callback arguments.

Same-partition and single-partition branches take shorter paths. Invalid targets are not a safe error-return interface. The routine masks target state with 0x0F, including a stopped bit. A callback can therefore make an allocated PARAR context runnable while its console remains unassigned. Use ACTIVAR to restore the observed console lifecycle; do not equate callback injection with creating a new partition.

Timed callbacks use service 0x35. A six-byte writable node immediately precedes the callback code. Delay DE is converted to floor(DE/8) ticks; zero removes a matching node. The list stores delta times and partition ownership. Expiry unlinks and clears the six bytes before entering node +6 through service 0x56. A=0 is the established normal mode. A bit-0 exception bypasses ownership only for a numerically matching head and does not provide a documented general cross-bank cancellation operation.

Byte locks

A byte lock holds zero when free or current scheduler index +1 when owned. Service 0x54 acquires it, returns immediately for the same owner, or sets wait bit 4 and yields for another owner. There is no recursive acquisition count. One release is sufficient after two same-owner acquisitions.

Service 0x55 ignores another owner's lock. For the current owner it clears bit 4 in every configured row and then clears the byte. This is a broadcast wakeup: a partition waiting on an unrelated lock wakes, tests its own condition and may wait again. Neither release nor wakeup guarantees fairness or transfers ownership directly.

Generic command cleanup releases a fixed list of locks at 0x29A7, 0x2462, 0x21DD, 0x046C, 0x2108, 0x2109 and 0x0057, followed by the disk transaction. It is not established to release arbitrary application-defined lock bytes.

File admission and record spans

An open-file row keys on physical device and first logical sector; its fourth byte contains owner and an exclusive bit. Shared opens can coexist. An exclusive open conflicts with every same-key open, while a shared open conflicts with an existing exclusive one. Pending OPEN calls yield and retry without using byte-lock wait bit 4. Two shared channels of one owner create identical rows; CLOSE removes one row, and the last close or owner cleanup admits an exclusive waiter.

Record locks instead key on physical device, channel and an inclusive sector interval. With multiple partitions, direct UPDATE mode 0x58 becomes 0x5A, enabling record locking. A direct read optionally releases old channel spans, acquires its span and retains it after successful I/O. A write-back releases spans when bit 1 is set and bit 2 is clear. UNLOCK/service 0x53 releases record spans but leaves exclusive file admission intact.

The interval implementation is imperfect. With 300-byte records, retained read 1 can own sectors 0xDA48–0xDA49. Same-owner read 2 requests 0xDA49–0xDA4A but returns without widening that row. Another partition can then acquire record 3 at 0xDA4A–0xDA4B. Successful read 2 has not acquired all the coverage its caller might expect.

The lower disk-read path independently checks existing record spans. A direct request that skips acquisition can still wait for another owner's already-held sectors. This second check is why disabling the upper acquisition bit does not bypass all record contention.

Memory queues

Services 0x47 and 0x48 operate on caller storage containing capacity, occupancy, read index and payload. They must not be described as a reliable blocking queue abstraction. The append routine caches occupancy before its full test and does not reload it while waiting; a timer callback can remove a byte and the writer can still spin indefinitely. The loop does not yield.

The index arithmetic wraps at eight bits before all reductions. Capacities above 128 can select the wrong payload or overwrite header bytes; capacities 254 and 255 have demonstrated header corruption.
