Serialized capacities 1–128 avoid these particular arithmetic defects by implementation-defined bounds, but this is neither an original advertised limit nor a complete concurrency guarantee. Queue consumers must distinguish empty A=0/carry set from a successfully read zero byte with carry clear.

Chapter 26 — Filesystem implementation

A file operation passes through several independent decisions: name normalization, account and drive search, catalogue probing, format-specific channel setup, allocation and physical I/O. A failure or inconsistency at one layer does not establish failure at every other layer. This chapter gives the algorithms needed to reproduce that distinction.

Lookup and normalization

Service 0x14 receives a writable 17-byte request: drive followed by eight name bytes and eight type bytes. It folds lowercase, retains spaces, dollar signs, digits and letters, and substitutes $ for other bytes. A leading character below A, except $, receives 0x11. A leading blank can therefore become 1, then B on a later normalization pass.

Normalization repeats during account and drive retries. Account 0 searching an explicit blank name can look for 1 and fail; account 2 can first fail privately and then normalize again to find public B. This behavior is not an ownership transfer. CSI separately treats underscore as deletion when context +0x0A bit 6 is set, whereas quoted BASIC names retain underscore until filesystem normalization changes it to $.

The name hash sums eight big-endian words from the sixteen normalized name/type bytes, modulo 65,536. For an example volume, let N=151 directory sectors and F=9 be the first directory sector. The initial LBA is hash % N + F. Each sector contains eight entries. Zero terminates the search; 0xFF is a deleted candidate remembered for reuse. A matching private entry precedes the account-0 fallback unless that fallback is inhibited by context +0x0C bit 6.

The subsequent-sector rule is specified exactly: if L<N, add F; if L=N, use 1+F; otherwise use F. At initial L=9 it cycles through only seventeen sectors. Filling those seventeen sectors occupies only 136 of the nominal 1,208 slots. The bounded search can therefore exhaust its cycle while slots elsewhere remain available. OUTPUT allocates a block before detecting this condition and releases it on the established normal failure path.

Wildcard LISDIR scans all 151 sectors instead. It can list an intact later entry hidden from exact lookup by an earlier zero marker. OUTPUT can then create an ordinary same-name/type/owner duplicate at the earlier reusable location. Exact lookup selects the new entry; deleting it exposes the old one. REPARAR accepts these duplicates. Neither successful creation nor a clean checker result proves global name uniqueness.

Allocation and deletion

The bitmap begins at byte 0x20 of logical sector 1 and continues through sector 8. Block b=LBA//4 uses byte 0x100 + 0x20 + b//8 in the logical volume stream and mask 0x80 >> (b%8). Set bits represent allocation. The header and cached descriptor also track free blocks.

Service 0x1B requires an exact contiguous count. Service 0x63 accepts desired and minimum counts: it takes the first run meeting the desired length, or otherwise the largest qualifying smaller run, preferring the later run on a tie. Total free space is therefore insufficient to predict whether a contiguous allocation succeeds. A request for 38 blocks failed with 38 free but a largest run of 37.

Deletion marks the directory entry 0xFF before releasing its blocks. Sequential chains can be fragmented, so their runs are freed separately. No transactional rollback across all writes is established. The native deletion path's ignored exclusive-open conflict is described in Chapter 22; an open file is not protected merely by being open.

Sequential open, transfer and close

Sequential OUTPUT mode 0x88 replaces an existing file. BASIC UPDATE SEQUENTIAL also selects 0x88 unless EXTEND is specified. The implementation deletes the old entry, allocates a block, creates zero count/end metadata and initializes payload with 0x1A before returning. A crash after OPEN can therefore leave a valid allocation with an unfinished directory entry.

EXTEND starts with mode 0x84 and returns 0x8C. It loads the directory's final-sector word directly, restores counts and scans backwards through trailing 0x1A to determine the append position. Trailing 0x1A data are indistinguishable from padding and can be overwritten by later append. A properly closed empty file has a nonzero final sector. An unfinished entry with final sector zero is different: EXTEND can load logical sector zero, and CLOSE can overwrite it and zero sectors 1–3. This malformed-file case destroys reserved metadata on that malformed-file path.

Record input 0x22 copies through CR, but treats 0x1A as end of file without copying the marker or appending a terminator. A partial record before 0x1A remains in the destination. There is no caller destination-capacity argument in the specified disk loop. Counted input 0x5E instead copies raw payload bytes, including 0x1A, and follows links. B=0 requests 256 bytes. BASIC gives those returned bytes an additional field/type interpretation.

Counted reads eagerly follow a link at a sector boundary. Reading exactly 254 bytes from the final sector can copy all requested bytes and nevertheless return A=1. A larger request can copy only the available prefix and leave the remainder of the destination untouched. EOF latches in channel state; subsequent reads copy nothing. BC is not a universal copied-byte count.

Counted output 0x5F increments the channel's record count once per call and can leave a partial sector solely in the caller buffer. BASIC's application WRITE may span several such calls and is not atomic. A READ then WRITE under sequential UPDATE shares the cursor; reading a 0x1A marker can place subsequent output behind an embedded application end marker, producing counted but normally unreachable records.

Figure 26.1. Sequential durability boundaries. Ignoring a failed disk write can make a later stage run without the preceding bytes reaching disk.

Direct and keyed files

Direct record n begins at (n-1) × record_length. Read and write can span sectors; write preserves surrounding bytes with read/modify/write operations. Record zero and out-of-range requests return A=0x80 in the code. Successful selected operations preserve BC, DE, HL, IX and IY; write protection can be accepted at OPEN and refused only at WRITE.

An indexed I file begins with N little-endian slot numbers, rounded up to a 1,024-byte boundary. Its first C entries identify active slots in key order. Each slot contains fixed key, fixed data and a two-byte next-slot link; remaining index entries are zero. The links follow the same ordered sequence. The index prefix holds ordered slot references, not a set of independent hash-chain heads.

A K file has fixed key/data slots without an index prefix or next word. Creation rounds the requested capacity using a prime-search rule: start with requested OR 3 and add 4 until prime. A request for 16 yields 19. Hashing applies h=(10*h+byte)%N through the key until NUL. Starting at h+1, collision steps use a state initialized to -N and incremented by 2, adding its absolute value modulo N. Zero marks an unused slot; 0xFF is a reusable tombstone. Appendix E supplies byte layouts and equations for both formats.

Repair is an independent interpretation

REPARAR scans all 1,208 records, skipping zero, deleted and exactly 0x80 entries. Its supported format mask includes R, A, S, D, I and K, but its filename acceptance is stricter than kernel creation. It can reject and, with F, delete an interior-space name that BASIC successfully created and read. It does not validate application record counts or repair every semantic inconsistency.

The F path can delete bad entries, clear sharing redirects and reconcile the bitmap. Even a checking invocation may update the free counter. Checking directory allocation and validating application records answer different questions. A consistent bitmap does not certify complete application data.

Chapter 27 — Disk I/O and physical translation

The common disk path resolves a partition association, checks synchronization and protection, converts a logical request into driver coordinates, and applies error policy. The final controller transaction is only one part of the public service. A successful return can follow a deliberately ignored controller failure.

Address translation

For a disk configured with 830 cylinders, five heads and 32 sectors per track, using 256-byte sectors, capacity is 132,800 sectors or 33,996,800 bytes. Sector IDs on this hard-disk interface are zero-based. The linear sector index is (cylinder*5 + head)*32 + sector.

A volume mapping can run in a descending cylinder direction. Divide logical LBA by 32 to obtain track t and sector s, then divide t by five to obtain q and r. In a five-head volume descending from cylinder 396, the mapping is cylinder 396-q, head 4-r. Logical filesystem order need not match ascending physical cylinder order.

A descriptor for this example geometry begins with five heads and a little-endian 397-cylinder count. Forty-five source/replacement track pairs occupy offsets 0x03–0xB6. The runtime substitutes a matching track once and stops at zero or a larger source. Bootstrap traversal differs: it lacks the same zero-stop rule, so source zero can match. For the descending mapping, replacing logical track 169 with 1939 redirects C363/H0 to C9/H0. Complete behavior for the special 0xFD/0xFE descriptor signatures and 0x55/0x99 mapping variants is unspecified.

The descriptor's cylinder span is not itself the usable volume capacity. A filesystem declaring 1,939 logical tracks of 32 sectors has 62,048 sectors, corresponding to 15,512 four-sector allocation blocks. Use declared filesystem bounds for allocation.

Common request path

Single-sector services 0x0A and 0x0B take B=association, DE=LBA and HL=buffer; both force C=1. Multi-sector services 0x32 and 0x33 use C as count. Selected successful eight-sector calls preserve BC, DE, HL, IX and IY and return AF=0x0044. The single-sector forms preserve the specified DE, HL, IX and IY but return C=1 rather than the caller's original C.

The common path acquires the disk transaction lock and checks record spans. An invalidated descriptor can cause a preliminary header read before the requested transfer. Thus one requested sector can generate two physical reads without a retry. Service 0x09 invalidates cached descriptor bytes +0x14/+0x15; it is not a general flush operation.

For writes, descriptor +0x13 bit 7 rejects the request with driver error 2 before issuing a controller command. The WD path uses command 0x20 for read and 0x30 for write, transferring through port 0xB8 before examining status. The specified failure status at the relevant WD registers maps to error 7. The bytes returned after a physical fault are not guaranteed valid.

Track formatting and sector size

INICDISC issues WD command 0x50 to format a track. With 256-byte sectors, it sends a full 256-byte buffer through port 0xB8. The beginning of that buffer supplies pairs of bad-block flag and sector ID; for 32 sectors, 64 bytes hold the pairs and the remaining bytes complete the transfer buffer. Sector Count determines how many entries are used. The formatter builds the sector-ID permutation; its interleave selection should not be inferred from the logical order in which files are read.

The SDH register at 0xBE separates size bits 6:5, drive bits 4:3 and head bits 2:0. Size encoding 00 means 256 bytes, 01 means 512 bytes, and 11 means 128 bytes. Encoding 10 is not a defined sector size here. Thus setting bit 5 alone selects 512 bytes, not 256. The system's 256-byte requests use size bits 00 while setting head and drive independently.

A controller transfer can finish its normal data/completion sequence while reporting an error. The driver must examine status; completion is not a substitute for successful status. Chapter 11 covers the operator's formatting dialogue and the effect of PISTA on volume placement.

Retry, ignore and abort

The installed retry count is four. R retries; M calls the driver's +3 entry and retries; I consumes the failed sector; Q leaves the operation nonlocally. For the resident WD driver, +3 is RET, so M is not demonstrated to perform a controller reset.

An ignored failed read can return normal success with invalid buffer contents. There is no general guarantee that failed reads supply zeros. An ignored failed write returns normal success without writing a valid sector. Software must not interpret A=0 as independent evidence of media integrity after operator intervention.

A registered 0x4A callback runs before retry-count decrement while the disk lock is held. A=0 ignores, positive A substitutes an error code for ordinary policy, and negative A aborts. Removing write protection in the callback and returning zero skips that failed write rather than retrying it. CLOSE can then commit metadata for a record whose payload never reached disk.

Standalone Q normally produces CSI result 254 through an active interruption callback. During protected CLOSE, that callback may already be cleared; the Q handler returns A=0 and subsequent generic termination reports result 0 despite lost buffered data. Result codes describe control flow, not a transaction's durability.

Floppy interface

DIS1 and DIS2 use a distinct four-entry ABI. Private read/write inputs are A=sector count, B=head, C=zero-based sector, DE=track (E used), HL=buffer and IY=descriptor. The packet uses read opcode 0x06 or write 0x05, with 0x40 added for the established 256-byte mode. The first physical sector is C+1 for 256-byte sectors, or 2*C+1 for 128-byte sectors. End-of-track and count are correspondingly doubled for 128-byte translation.

DIS1 polls ports 0xCC–0xCE and can remain in initialization when the expected device does not respond. DIS2 uses 0xC0–0xC2 and can report not-ready error 1 without an available drive. The DIS2 transfer path writes the bank byte followed by buffer-address high and low bytes to port 0xC2, then polls bit 7 for completion. Controller result bytes come through 0xC1. The physical adapter wiring, bus timing and address carry across a 64-KiB boundary remain incompletely specified.

The FLOPPY2 format selected by INICIAR with CARA 2 has 77 cylinders and 26 sector IDs per track: cylinder 0 uses FM/128-byte sectors on both heads, and the remaining cylinders use MFM/256-byte sectors. The physical sector order is 1,14,2,15 through 13,26. These describe the software format, not a particular drive manufacturer or model. Chapter 11 explains formatting, mounting and readback checks. File transfers alone do not establish floppy bootstrap, IBM interchange or a complete removable-media archive workflow. Appendix B gives the packet and error mappings.

Chapter 28 — Terminal, serial, printer and tape drivers

The console's apparent simplicity conceals several layers: application policy, logical controls, terminal-class translation, device queues and controller interrupts. Drivers for other devices reuse some entry offsets but retain their own contracts. This chapter follows the software path from a character request to its driver and explains why accepted output is not proof of physical delivery.

Console policy and class translation

Service 0x05 applies context output suppression and printer-copy policy before service 0x07. Service 0x07 interprets logical cursor controls and calls terminal-class +3, with raw fallback when the class returns carry. Service 0x40 writes to an associated device directly and does not perform class translation or update the tracked cursor.

Escape-O toggles context +0x0A bit 1. It suppresses service 0x05 and echo generated by service 0x04, but direct 0x07 and 0x40 output continues. Escape-S runs a local pause loop with scheduler yields; other partitions continue. Resumption drains queued input, so a character typed while paused need not be delivered to the application. Escape-Q interrupts the established noncooperative loop and returns CSI result 254.

Class files have input entry +0 and output entry +3.CLASE1's control-pointer table begins at +0x8C; CLASE2's at +0xBE, each containing 32 little-endian pointers. Sequences terminate at 0xFF. Controls 0x00, 0x10, 0x15 and 0x19 receive kernel handling rather than an ordinary table emission. Control 0x10 consumes column and row bytes. CLASE1 encodes these as ESC G followed by coordinate+0x20; CLASE2 emits ESC [ row+1 ; column+1 H. Arithmetic wraps as bytes; it is not a clipping operation.

The descriptor's L and P values have caller-specific uses. CSI passes L directly as its line-input budget, and ordinary output tests the horizontal threshold before advancing. LF increments and caps the row at P. Default L79/P23 must therefore not be reinterpreted categorically as an 80-by-24 display size.

The transport is seven-bit in the reconstructed console setup. An emitted high-bit control can consequently appear differently on the terminal. Deferred form-feed state can consume the next echo while leaving the input character valid. Diagnose input bytes, echo and cursor tracking separately.

ALP serial drivers

DIS18–DIS21 implement PAN1–PAN4 on port pairs 0x32/0x33, 0x30/0x31, 0x36/0x37 and 0x34/0x35. DIS23–DIS26 supply the optional second group's pairs 0x3A/0x3B, 0x38/0x39, 0x3E/0x3F and 0x3C/0x3D. The 887-byte driver contains an 80-byte input ring and two 83-byte output buffers.

The ring at driver +0x240 stores capacity, count and read index followed by payload at +0x243. Full input is dropped. The established flow-control branches act when the previous count is 75 on receipt and 5 on consumption. The resulting occupancies are 76 and 4 respectively; confusing pre- and post-update values misstates the driver's pause/resume thresholds. Ports 0x24–0x27 supply separate receive-ready outputs, while SCC WR5 controls RTS. Their exact physical combination remains incompletely traced. Software-ring backpressure and the SCC's smaller receive FIFO are separate limits; stopping the sender does not retract a character already in flight.

Output buffers begin at +0x293 and +0x2E6. Output flushes around Escape and after CR, FF, ETX or count above 82; status polling can also flush and wait for DMA. A returned character-output call may therefore describe buffering only. DMA readiness is held in fixed common bytes 0x02A6/0x02A7, not in a descriptor-local busy flag.

Service 0x67 registers a three-byte callback row at 0x23FF + 3*A, for indices 0x02–0x20. The row contains owner index and callback pointer. PAN1 receive/DMA indices are 0x11/0x15, PAN2 0x12/0x16, and the later channels follow the installed map in Appendix B. Receive dispatch enters the registered owner through the cross-partition callback path and clears the high state nibble. DMA completion clears only wait bit 5.

A blocking input routine flushes output, disables interrupts, attempts a ring pop and calls 0x6B if empty. With one partition, 0x6B enables interrupts and HALTs; with several it marks event-wait bit 5 and schedules. Wakeup is not proof of queued input. DMA completion, consumed escape input and other callbacks can wake a reader which must test again.

SS2 serial drivers

DIS16 and DIS17 are 650-byte drivers for PAN9/IS9 and PAN10/IS10 at 0x7E/0x7F and 0x7C/0x7D. Their input rings hold 128 bytes; output is polled rather than ALP-buffered. Status carry derives from controller bit 2, while other flags reflect ring occupancy.

Initialization registers callbacks at indices 0x0B and 0x10. Termination waits on controller register 1 bit 0, resets the channel, unregisters the callback and enables interrupts. RST 0x10 dispatch reads port 0x60 masked with 0xE0. Source 0xE0 takes the SCC path through 0x2386, which reads RR3 at 0x7E: bits 3–5 select callback 0x0B, otherwise the selected path is callback 0x10. Source 0xC0 takes the timer path and is acknowledged by writing 0xA0 to port 0x60. Physical arbitration and complete source-mask wiring remain unspecified. The serial drivers require their own fitted controller and correctly framed terminal connection; they are separate from the ALP ports.

The driver temporarily replaces context callback +0x28 during status/output. In a controlled busy retry, it overwrote its saved pointer and returned with its own handler still installed. Ready-only output restored the previous handler. This is a driver lifetime defect rather than a different contract for service 0x39.

Printers

IP/DIS11 uses ports 0xA4/0xA5 and reports disconnection in the specified unmapped configuration, leaving its allocation removable by ASOCIAR. CDC/DIS12 can patch its ports to 0x50/0x51. It uppercases lowercase, substitutes ? for the observed unsupported special characters, complements bytes and writes them. IMP/DIS22 uses ports 0x78–0x7B with the SCN2651 register layout: data, status, alternating mode registers and command. The port assignment is supported by the driver and board component identification; complete signal and clock wiring remains unspecified. Its status tests require transmit-ready, DCD and DSR, while CTS gates serial transmission. The normal initialization requests nominal 9600 baud with eight data bits, odd parity and one stop bit. The B4800 PE form selects seven data bits, even parity and one stop bit.

IMP maps 0x80 to 0x1F before output. It can also mistake an all-ones 0xFF status from an unavailable interface for readiness, so status acceptance alone does not prove delivery. An offline printer can trigger the native R/Q prompt. These are byte-transfer and readiness contracts; the physical printer model and its page-description behavior remain unspecified.

The common printer policy for associations 0x0C–0x0F polls readiness and manages controls, page state and delay. In the established default path, 0x1F and FF become CR, FF before the driver. An unassigned printer can silently return success. The printer input entries are RET stubs; their incidental A values are not input characters. These software returns do not certify that a page has been printed.

Tape

DIS10 is a 2, 980-byte driver. Its public status, input and output entries are XOR A/RET stubs; actual record operations use driver +0x0F with private selectors 0x80–0x8C. Utilities obtain that address through the physical descriptor. These selectors are not system calls beyond 0x6E.

Initialization registers callback index 0x03 and waits on ports 0xA0–0xA3. Initialization can wait indefinitely without the expected controller response. Complete read, write, rewind and archive-recovery procedures are unspecified.

Some private paths are especially uncertain: operation 0x8B has a branch that returns before restoring a pushed DE, operation 0x8C stores 0xDF where a reset interpretation might suggest zero, and 0x83 can return zero without output on a selected branch. These incomplete contracts require caution when implementing compatible behavior. The selector and 80-byte label tables in Appendix A are sufficient to identify the specified protocol without presenting it as a specified backup service.

Chapter 29 — Deferred printing and messages

Deferred printing and messaging both cross the boundary between an immediate request and later activity. They use different storage and execution mechanisms. The resident spooler intercepts printer output, live messages inject a callback into another partition, and the mailbox utilities maintain an ordinary sequential file.

Resident spooler

The named resident entry at 0x39C2 leads to administration code at 0x3FB2 and a selector table at 0x3FC4–0x3FE9. Selectors 0x00–0x12 have no specified independent bounds check. Collection enters at 0x39C8 and continues at 0x3B9C. Global word 0x0044 identifies the active spooler.

SPOOLER.COMANDO queries resident name SPOOLER through service 0x6C at file offset 0x0160. The missing-resident branch refers to CARGAR SPOOLER, stored at 0x0D47; a separate VACIAR SPOOLER string is at 0x0D1D. Both standalone executables are absent. Because the resident SPOOLER entry already exists, these missing companions do not prevent the documented initialization and paused collection. The references suggest a separate resident-component lifecycle, but do not establish its complete interface or make DESCARGAR a synonym for VACIAR.

Before initialization, SPOOLER LIST can report an empty queue with result 0 while ESTADO reports its uninitialized condition with result 12. After INIC, the established paused configuration replaces the printer driver's output-vector target with 0x39C8. The common printer path detects nonzero 0x0044 and bypasses its ordinary formatting, allowing the collector to retain the input stream.

A report can be collected while ordinary printing is paused. Form-feed and end-report controls affect its page count and completion state. Queued data describe collected output; they do not certify that a physical printer has produced it.

The sixteen collection slots at 0x39DD have a 16-byte stride. They hold flags, free-buffer count, buffer pointer, current sector, pages, blocks, queue number, queue selection, flags/copies and line state; the final byte remains unresolved. SPOOLCOL is a direct file of 64 sixteen-byte records in one block. A queue record stores an eight-byte report label, submitting partition index+1, three-byte timestamp, flags, queue and page count. The producer's equality wrap appears to use records 1–63; that effective capacity remains implementation-defined rather than saturated natively.

Report flags distinguish 0x80 open, 0x40 closed, 0xC0 printing and zero printed, with retention bit 0x20, copy count in bits 2–4 plus one, and printer in bits 0–1 plus one. The backing file's directory count is pages, not application writes. The collector writes its own metadata rather than relying on generic sequential CLOSE.

Report encoding and restart rules

Report data uses 0x06 followed by a byte to represent spaces; ordinary lengths are encoded as length+0x20. The exact decoder wraps, so malformed 0x21 and 0x20 counts yield 257 and 256 spaces. Byte 0x0D means CR/LF, 0x8D means bare CR, 0x1A ends the report and 0x1F skips the rest of a sector before following its chain. Other bytes, including 0xFF, are literal on the specified path. This is report-level encoding, unrelated to the directory read-protection bit 0x20.

On the implementation-defined INIC restart path, exactly 0x80 incomplete queue entries are deleted. Retained 0xA0 and multicopy 0x84 cases can survive; printing states 0xC0–0xFF become 0x40–0x7F. Failed report lookup clears flags. Backward cleanup of inactive tail entries clears the first label byte rather than every field. Invalid chains can encounter zero links; no visited-sector cycle guard is established. Native collection is documented, while the complete restart and physical-output lifecycle remains unresolved.

Live message transfer

Service 0x58 serializes a message with owner lock 0x21DD and a tagged transfer byte at 0x21DC. It enters callback 0x21BE in the recipient through service 0x56. The callback uses the recipient's console output policy. The sender's DE advances past NUL or CR; BC, HL, IX and IY are preserved in the selected calls.

Message bytes are masked to seven bits. NUL terminates, CR emits CR/LF and terminates, LF emits CR/LF and continues, and DEL is skipped. Control 0x10 consumes two coordinates, masks them to seven bits and changes 0x7F to 0x7E. A self or out-of-range target returns early, but still calls the lock-release routine; a caller-owned 0x21DD can therefore be released without delivery.

The service itself checks neither account nor message-OFF policy. MENSAJE imposes its own addressed-user rules, and the specified privilege-5 wildcard path bypasses OFF. Result 0 can mean no live delivery. Do not treat this interface as console input or a persistent message queue.

Persistent mailbox

MENSAJE deposits and BUZON retrieval use SISTEMA.BUZON, a sequential file owned under temporary account 0xFF. Each message starts with a 33-byte header: recipient eight bytes, sender eight, date eight, time eight and CR. Body lines end with CR. Byte 0x7F pads to a 254-byte payload boundary and ends the message; the final file sector uses 0x1A padding.

BUZON displays and removes messages for the exact account name, compacts surviving messages and updates blocks/end state through raw-sector operations. Its directory count can remain stale. A recipient prefix accepted during deposit need not match the exact name used on retrieval. The mailbox is service state and need not exist before stored messaging is used. Appendix E records the header separately from the sequential sector wrapper.

Chapter 30 — Command processing, loading and termination

CSI resolves a command into an executable or interpreter invocation. The loader then interprets the file's format and relocations, while the executable supplies its own policy and runtime behavior. A command result belongs to the final control path; it is not a substitute for inspecting file-close and device results.

Parsing and command selection

CSI uses terminal geometry to set the line-input budget. Service 0x01 can submit automatically when that budget is exhausted, so an accidentally small L setting can split a typed command before CR. Prepared command text supplied through service 0x36 bypasses this interactive budget. Service 0x36 accepts printable bytes, uppercases lowercase, terminates at CR and returns through cleanup into CSI; no length guard is apparent in the copy loop.

The original abbreviation table has 45 entries. It defines command spellings and minimum prefixes, not an inventory of installed programs. CLASIF and CARGAR have no matching executable or EXEC file. DESCARGAR is stored as nine letters, but lookup uses the eight-character field DESCARGA, for which neither file is installed. None of these entries supplies a built-in implementation. EXEC is a special case: EX and EXE select its path but transfer the wrong original token position to EXECLANG, permitting a different script to run. Spell EXEC in full.

CSI expands table abbreviations at file offsets 0x053A–0x0589. Its final file-not-found path at 0x06E1–0x06F2 restores the entered eight-byte name before displaying the error. Thus an error naming CAR.COMANDO after entering CAR does not mean expansion to CARGAR failed. The spelling shown in that error is not a complete record of the names searched.

Custom synonyms are considered before the standard abbreviation table. Within each searched disk association, CSI checks the original token's COMANDO file, then its EXEC file when IMPEXEC is enabled, before checking the expanded names. It then proceeds to the next association. This ordering means a real file can take precedence over the expected standard abbreviation. Chapter 35 gives the operator-visible settings and exact accepted prefixes.

Implicit EXEC execution depends on the relevant setting and file search. BASIC and EXEC language keywords are interpreted by their own components, not by CSI's command table. This separation explains why an editor command or BASIC statement cannot be pasted at the OS prompt and expected to have its language meaning.

R loading

An R file has no mandatory fixed header. Its load image begins at file offset zero and can contain both instructions and initialized data. The command-execution interface enters a command at its loaded base; a separately loaded overlay or driver follows its own entry convention. BASIC overlays begin with a component byte before their JP slot, while SORT overlays begin with a control/data area. Appendix C gives these layouts. Loading such a component is distinct from transferring control to its entry. The directory supplies image byte length, stored-sector count and start LBA. When present, relocation records begin after the image rounded up to a 256-byte sector boundary. Some small R modules have no relocation sector. Each relocation sector begins with a count from 1 to 127 followed by little-endian offsets. At each offset, the loader adds the load base to a little-endian word modulo 65,536.

CSI's load image, including code and initialized data, is 3,104 bytes (0x0C20), with relocation storage beginning at file offset 0x0D00. Its fifteen stored sectors include two relocation sectors with 250 fixups. At a load base of 0x5157, those fixups adjust the embedded addresses accordingly. A parser must distinguish image byte length, image-sector count and total stored sectors.

Service 0x1E loads and enters a command at context +0x4CC for R format; A format uses its absolute load address from directory +0x1E. Service 0x16 loads a named module without entering it. Service 0x62 constructs temporary metadata from a compact seven-byte descriptor for R submodules. These load interfaces share machinery but are not interchangeable calling conventions.

Figure 30.1. Command execution. The CLOSE step is an application responsibility, not an automatic loader or termination operation.

Command policy and runtime return

The optional standard prologue is CF 41. Four preceding bytes contain minimum privilege and a three-byte installation serial binding. A zero first serial byte bypasses the comparison. Failure displays message 19 or 20 and ends with result 252. The surveyed prologues use serial bytes 16 00 00 and privilege values 0–5.BASIC's OPTION PRIV/SERIAL guards use its own compiled representation and cleanup; their established failures return 253 instead.

The loader's full initial register and return-trampoline contract remains incomplete. The documented small Z80 example in Chapter 18 uses only established services and ends through 0x00; it does not assume a C runtime, argument vector, automatic heap or a RET-to-CSI convention. Small BASICUSR modules are different: their defined wrapper calls them and restores IX/IY around a normal RET.

Cleanup and continuation

Service 0x00 stores A at context +0x68, removes current-owner lock/open state, clears execution state and reloads CSI. It does not implicitly flush a partial sequential output buffer. An explicit CLOSE before termination is required for the established sequential durability path. A BASIC error handler may itself call CLOSE; this application cleanup must not be generalized to the executive.

Service 0x46 adds a continuation ceiling to command replacement. It sets the memory ceiling and SP from HL, marks context +0x38 bit 5 and uses the service-0x36 path. CSI later clears that bit and jumps to the ceiling. The code establishes the mechanism, but a complete general-purpose caller example and register contract remain unspecified.

FIN ends the account session and can unload/reload the console for login. PARAR stops a partition while retaining its allocated RAM. Accepted IPL restarts the machine. These operations have distinct scopes and none supplies evidence for a universal whole-system shutdown-and-flush command.

Part VII — Reference

Chapter 31 — Command reference

Look up a full command name here after learning the basic file operations. Each entry explains the purpose, syntax, options and one elementary example. Examples are individual operations, not a sequence to run from top to bottom. Practice files such as NOTE.TEXT come from Chapter 6; other filenames are explicit illustrations requiring compatible files to exist first.

A device letter in a maintenance example must be checked with ASOCIAR before use. Destructive examples explain syntax and are not instructions to alter your system disk. Some optional facilities have only a documented interface; their entries say where a complete operating procedure is unavailable. Names with no specified callable interface have an inspection or help example instead of an invented invocation.

Full command names are used throughout the examples. Chapter 35 gives their permitted abbreviations, and its option tables distinguish full keywords from their shorter accepted forms.

Privilege is the standard minimum check where known. A command can make additional checks. See Chapter 33 for exact messages; a completion code is not the same as a system message number.

ACTIVAR — activate a partition or background command

Activate a partition or background command.

Syntax

ACTIVAR partition (PANn)

Options. Input/output consoles and ASOCIAR device options; command help also describes a command in the first free partition.

Minimum privilege. 2.

Example

ACTIVAR 2 (PAN2)

After allocating partition 2 as described in Chapter 12, this activates it on terminal PAN2. Allocation must precede activation.

Use parentheses around PANn. Displayed partition numbers and memory-module numbers are different.

ARCHDIR — list an archive floppy

List an archive floppy.

Syntax

ARCHDIR drive [(options)]

Options. IMP prints; VOL n starts at the selected archive volume (default 1).

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

ARCHDIR A

With archive media in the device associated with A, this lists the archive contents. An archive catalogue is different from an ordinary LISDIR listing.

ARCHIVAR — archive files, accounts or volumes

Archive files, accounts or volumes.

Syntax

ARCHIVAR [name.type:]source,destination [(options)]

Options. Two dates bound selection; CUENTA selects current account, VOLUM all accounts; PANT/NOPANT, VERIFI/NOVERIFI and CONSU/NOCONSU control listing, verification and prompting.

Minimum privilege. 2.

Caution — Writes archive destination media.

Example

ARCHIVAR NOTE.TEXT:S,A (VERIFI CONSU)

This syntax example selects NOTE.TEXT on S for an archive on A, requests verification and retains consultation. It requires a compatible archive device and expendable destination media; a complete physical-device procedure is unspecified.

ASIGNAR — assign channels

Assign channels.

Syntax

ASIGNAR [channel device-or-file] [DESASI]

Options. Channels 1–16; CONSOLA or name.type:drive; DESASI removes assignments.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

ASIGNAR 1 CONSOLA

This assigns application channel 1 to CONSOLA. It does not change which physical terminal CONSOLA denotes; ASOCIAR handles that relationship.

ASOCIAR — associate logical and physical devices

Associate logical and physical devices.

Syntax

ASOCIAR [logical [physical [(options)]]]

Options. L line length; P page length; B baud; C class; LF/FF delays; PE/PO parity; ENAB protocol; PP page parity; O blank lines; ALF automatic LF; H head load; R retries; STP seek; STS latency; PUBLIC public device.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

ASOCIAR

With no arguments the command lists current logical-to-physical associations. Inspect this list before changing a disk or printer destination.

ATRIB — rename or protect files

Rename or protect files.

Syntax

ATRIB old [new] [(options)]

Options. PANT/NOPANT; CONSUL/NOCONSUL; E/NOE write protection; B/NOB deletion protection; LE read protection (L is also accepted; LEER is not).

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

ATRIB COPY.TEXT:S OTHER.TEXT:S (NOCONSUL)

This renames the practice copy to OTHER.TEXT and suppresses consultation. Check that the destination is unused. Allocation is retained; no second data copy is created.

E, B and read protection apply to different access paths. Sequential replacement can bypass E by deleting and recreating the entry; see Chapter 11.

To change attributes without renaming, omit the second filename. For a practice file owned by the current account:

ATRIB NOTE.TEXT:S (E NOCONSUL)

E enables write protection; the sequential practice file's format indication changes from S to SW. To clear that protection:

ATRIB NOTE.TEXT:S (NOE NOCONSUL)

NOE returns the indication to S. Inspect each result with LISDIR. Repeating the same filename as both arguments is not the attribute-only form and produces Error sintactico, CR = 8.

AYUDA — display command help

Display command help.

Syntax

AYUDA [command]

Options. No topic gives the summary; a matching topic normally gives its command help. Failed lookup can invoke the named utility with AYUD.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

AYUDA LISDIR

This displays the help topic for LISDIR. It describes the command rather than performing the directory listing.

If lookup fails, AYUDA can invoke the named utility with AYUD. AYUDA BORRAR takes this path and reports Falta tipo del fichero, CR = 8; it does not display the intended topic. Use the BORRAR entry for deletion instructions. The working LISDIR example does not establish that every topic request is passive.

BACKUP — copy a complete disk or tape medium

Copy a complete disk or tape medium.

Syntax

BACKUP [source destination]

Options. Original defaults are S and A.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Replaces destination contents.

Example

BACKUP S A

This syntax example copies the complete source medium S to destination A. It is appropriate only when A is a correctly configured compatible backup destination whose contents may be replaced. Do not rely on omitted-device defaults.

BASIC — edit, interpret or compile basic

Edit, interpret or compile BASIC.

Syntax

BASIC [name.type:drive] [(options)]

Options. LIST/NOLIST listing; IMPRn printer; XREF references; NOOBJ suppress BASICCOM generation; NOPANT suppress console; OBJ=drive output location; TRACE compilation passes and line numbers. COMMA is advertised in help but rejected by this version.

Minimum privilege. 2.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S

This compiles saved BASIC source to HELLO.BASICCOM:S. First create and save the source as taught in Chapter 15. BASIC without a filename enters the editor/interpreter.

NOOBJ controls compiled output independently of LIST. To display source without generating BASICCOM:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (LIST NOOBJ)

NOOBJ alone can display compilation statistics without listing source. An older BASICCOM may remain; it must not be mistaken for output from this invocation.

COMMA and the alternative spelling COMA both produce Opcion no valida, CR = 8, as compiler options in this version. Use dot-decimal source; a working compiler decimal-comma option has not been established. Appendix D retains the advertised form with this restriction.

BORRAR — delete files and release storage

Delete files and release storage.

Syntax

BORRAR name.type[:drive] [(options)]

Options. PANT/NOPANT names; CONSUL/NOCONSUL consultation for wildcard selection. Exact-name deletion is immediate even with CONSUL.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Caution — immediate deletion. Exact-name selection has no confirmation stop.

Example

Inspect the exact filename with LISDIR before proceeding. This example deletes immediately, even if CONSUL is supplied.

BORRAR OTHER.TEXT:S

This deletes the named practice file immediately and releases its storage. There is no confirmation stop for this exact-name form, even with CONSUL. Inspect the exact filename with LISDIR before entering the deletion command.

CONSUL can request confirmation on the wildcard-selection path: N declines a selected file and S permits deletion. That behavior does not apply to the exact-name example above. Do not introduce a wildcard merely to obtain a confirmation prompt; it broadens the selection.

BSC — configure 2780/3780 communications

Configure 2780/3780 communications.

Syntax

BSC [parameter-file] [AYUDA] [(options)]

Options. COMU1 selects the logical channel; MODIF edits or creates saved protocol parameters.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

AYUDA BSC

This displays the central BSC help topic without establishing a communications connection. BSC AYUDA requests the utility's own options display, but requires COMU1 association even for that request. Without it, the command reports COMU1 no esta asociado, CR = 16. Protocol operation also requires a compatible driver and peer; the DIS27 dependency is not a guaranteed installed component.

BUSCAR — test file existence

Test file existence.

Syntax

BUSCAR [name.type[:drive]] [(option)]

Options. PANT displays (default); NOPANT suppresses; PUBLIC includes public files.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

BUSCAR NOTE.TEXT:S (PANT)

This searches for the practice file and displays the result. A negative result can reflect the current account or association, not global absence from all storage.

BUZON — read and remove stored messages

Read and remove stored messages.

Syntax

BUZON

Options. No established options.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

BUZON

This retrieves messages for the connected account. Retrieval removes the selected stored messages, so record anything needed before finishing.

CAMBIAR — change a command's minimum privilege

Change a command's minimum privilege.

Syntax

CAMBIAR name.type:drive (PRIV n)

Options. PRIV gives the new threshold.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Changes executable policy.

Example

CAMBIAR DEMO.COMANDO:S (PRIV 4)

This maintenance illustration raises the selected practice executable's standard minimum privilege to 4. Use only a disposable copy of a command, not an essential system utility. It does not raise your own privilege.

CAMCU — change an existing account number

Change an existing account number.

Syntax

CAMCU

Options. Interactive interface.

Minimum privilege. 5.

Example

CAMCU

This enters interactive account-number maintenance. Account 0/SISTEMA and its synonyms are excluded; numbers above 254 are excluded. The complete dialogue and effects on every owned file are unspecified, so no renumbering sequence is prescribed.

CAMPRO — transfer private-file ownership

Transfer private-file ownership.

Syntax

CAMPRO name.type[:drive] (account [options])

Options. PANT/NOPANT and CONSUL/NOCONSUL.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Transfers file ownership.

Example

Before this example, connect as the non-SISTEMA owner of NOTE.TEXT and check that its privilege is at least 3. The destination SISTEMA account is not the account from which to select the private source file.

CAMPRO NOTE.TEXT:S (SISTEMA NOCONSUL)

Run this while connected as the current non-SISTEMA owner of NOTE.TEXT, with privilege 3 or higher. It transfers the file to SISTEMA. Administrative identity alone does not select another owner's private files: from the wrong account the command can report No se ha cambiado ningun fichero, CR = 12. Inspect the recipient's entry and contents after transfer. Account 0 participates in public fallback, so transfer to SISTEMA does not guarantee that the former owner loses lookup access.

CARGAR — command-table entry; executable absent

CSI recognizes CARGAR and expands its minimum abbreviation CAR, but the required executable is not installed. Both CARGAR alone and CARGAR SPOOLER fail with a file-not-found error. The table entry supplies a spelling, not an implementation.

SPOOLER.COMANDO contains CARGAR SPOOLER in the path used when the resident spooler cannot be found. This indicates an intended resident-component loading role; it does not establish the input file type, allocation rules or a general loading interface. CARGAR must not be treated as a documented linker.

Syntax and options. No usable interface has been established.

Minimum privilege. Unknown.

Example: consult available help

AYUDA

AYUDA displays available help topics. This is a help example, not an invocation of the missing CARGAR utility. For the installed printing facility, see SPOOLER. Its resident component can already be present even though CARGAR is absent.

CEDIT — edit direct, indexed or keyed records

Edit direct, indexed or keyed records.

Syntax

CEDIT

Options. Interactive record editor described by command help.

Minimum privilege. 2.

Example

CEDIT

This enters the structured-record editor. It can modify records in direct, indexed or keyed files. Its complete selection/save/exit dialogue is unspecified; do not apply EDITAR commands to it.

CIFRA — calculate a file check value

Calculate a file check value.

Syntax

CIFRA name.type[:drive] [(option)]

Options. P console (default); I printer.

Minimum privilege. 4.

Example

CIFRA NOTE.TEXT:S (P)

This asks for the check value of the sequential practice file on the console. Record the result if comparing repeated copies. The algorithm is not specified as a modern hash or CRC.

CLASIF — command-table entry; executable absent

CSI recognizes CLASIF and expands its minimum abbreviation CL, but no corresponding executable or EXEC file is installed. Invoking either spelling fails; CSI does not redirect it to SORT. Whether CLASIF was an earlier name, an optional utility or an obsolete table entry is unknown.

Syntax and options. No usable interface has been established. SORT's options cannot be assigned to CLASIF.

Minimum privilege. Unknown.

Example: consult the installed sorting utility

AYUDA SORT

This displays help for SORT, whose executable and two overlays are installed. Continue with the SORT reference for its own syntax and examples. CLASIF is not an alias for SORT.

The CLASIFICAR and ORDENAR words, and references to a CLASIF file, belong to a separate context within CONSULTA. They do not make CLASIF an available CSI command.

CLAVIN — installed CLAVE.CUENTA helper; startup failure

CLAVIN.COMANDO is installed and loads, but invocations both without arguments and with a test argument return CR = 254 during startup, including under SISTEMA at privilege 5. This failure occurs before the apparent main operation; neither form establishes a usable interface.

The executable identifies itself as CLAVIN ver 1.0 01JUN83. Its code contains separate paths apparently intended to write an argument to CLAVE.CUENTA and to read and display a value when no argument is supplied. Together with CLAVOUT.BASICUSR, it appears to exchange a counted string or value through that file. The value's application meaning and the intended argument format remain unknown.

Syntax and options. No working operational syntax is established; no central help topic is available.

Minimum privilege. 5, as specified by the executable's prologue. Meeting this requirement does not prevent the startup failure.

Example: inspect the installed executable

LISDIR CLAVIN.COMANDO:S

This lists the utility's catalogue entry on the system disk. It establishes that the file is present; it does not establish that the utility works.

Invocation and failure

CLAVIN

The executable displays its version heading and returns CR = 254. This documents the failing invocation, not a successful value-reading operation. The same result with an argument does not demonstrate a working write operation.

CLAVE.CUENTA is not present in the documented installation. Creating an empty file does not correct the startup code and relocation inconsistencies. No reliable repair is established; Appendix C describes the executable and its BASIC companion.

CLAVE.CUENTA is not the login-password database. Account credentials belong to SISTEMA.CUENTA; use CUENTA for the documented account and password operations.

COMPART — grant, revoke or list shared access

Grant, revoke or list shared access.

Syntax

COMPART name.type[:drive] [(PANT)]

Options. PANT displays sharing accounts; the ordinary dialogue grants or revokes.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Example

Before this example, connect as a non-SISTEMA account with privilege 3 or higher and create the practice file under that identity.

COMPART NOTE.TEXT:S (PANT)

Connect as the file's non-SISTEMA owner with privilege 3 or higher before using this example. It lists accounts granted access; with no grants, an empty result and CR = 0 are expected. SISTEMA is excluded and receives La cuenta sistema no puede compartir ficheros, CR = 12. Omit PANT only when intending the separate grant/revoke dialogue. Grants retain the original owner.

CONECTAR — connect to another account

Connect to another account.

Syntax

CONECTAR account

Options. Password prompting and account profile execution depend on the account.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

CONECTAR SISTEMA

This requests connection to SISTEMA when its account number is not in use by another partition. Enter its password when requested. A duplicate connection is refused before the password check, with Esta cuenta esta siendo utilizada por la particion followed by the partition number and CR = 10. End the existing connection before retrying; an alias with the same account number does not avoid this restriction. A successful connection can run that account's EXEC profile.

CREAR — create structured files

Create structured files.

Syntax

CREAR name.type:drive (options)

Options. DIRECTO, INDEXADO or CLAVE; NUMREG count; REGISTRO length; LONCLA key length; VACIAR empties an existing file.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

CREAR DIRECT.DATOS:S (DIRECTO REGISTRO 32 NUMREG 10)

This creates a new direct file with ten records of 32 bytes. DIRECTO selects organization, REGISTRO supplies record length and NUMREG supplies the requested count.

VACIAR empties an existing file and destroys its records. I and K capacities and allocation formulas differ.

CUENTA — maintain user accounts

Maintain user accounts.

Syntax

CUENTA [(option)]

Options. PANT; IMPRIMIRn; VACIAR history; ELIMINAR account system; COPIAR=drive; HISTORI.

Minimum privilege. 5.

Example

CUENTA (PANT)

This displays registered accounts, including the password column. Keep the listing private. CUENTA without options enters maintenance: N adds an account, C changes its password and privilege, B removes one named account, and F writes the revised list and finishes. At privilege 5, use the SISTEMA account. Chapter 10 gives separate step-by-step procedures for creation, password changes and removal.

ELIMINAR removes the account system; VACIAR clears account history. Neither is an ordinary single-account deletion command.

DESCARGAR — command-table entry; executable absent

DESCARGAR is the original nine-letter name in CSI's abbreviation table. The command lookup field holds only eight characters, so the expanded lookup name is DESCARGA. Neither a matching COMANDO executable nor an EXEC file is installed, and invocation fails.

The minimum abbreviation is DESCAR; DESCARG and DESCARGA are also accepted prefixes. Recognition of these spellings does not supply the missing program. An error naming DESCARGA.COMANDO after entering DESCARGAR reflects the lookup field's length.

Syntax, purpose and options. Unknown. In particular, the name does not establish an unloading operation or make DESCARGAR equivalent to VACIAR.

Minimum privilege. Unknown.

Example: consult available help

AYUDA

This displays the available help summary. It is not an example of unloading a component. SPOOLER.COMANDO separately contains VACIAR SPOOLER, but the standalone VACIAR executable is also absent. The VACIAR options of CUENTA, CREAR and INICIAR belong to those individual commands and remain separate from these unavailable programs.

With a suitable IBM-3741 floppy in A, this lists its volume and file information. Track/sector fields use PPSSS notation; this is not a listing of the native filesystem.

ESTADO — inspect system settings and state

Inspect system settings and state.

Syntax

ESTADO option

Options. ALL, QUIEN, HORA, FECHA, SISTEMA, FORFECHA, PRIVILEG, RELOJ, DIA, MENSAJE, TIEMPO, USUARIOS, MEMORIA, MODULO, DISCO, ERRNUM, ERRTEXT, INCOM, ABREV, IMPEXEC, HISTOR, RTCAVAIL, SISFECHA, INIREL, QET, ALEMAN, ESCAPE, SUBIR, BAJAR, IZQU, DERECHA, RUBOUT, CANCEL, SINONIMO, VERSION. List memory modules

This shows usable memory modules, which supply partition storage.

This reports current user connections. CUENTA (PANT) instead lists registered accounts.

INICDISC — initialize or relabel a disk

Initialize or relabel a disk.

Syntax

INICDISC drive (options)

Options. ETIQU requests a label change; VACIAR erases files; DIRE requests directory entries; WP/NOWP changes software write protection; FORMAT requests physical formatting; CARA specifies heads; PISTA specifies cylinders/tracks per head and can affect volume placement; SECTOR specifies sectors per track; BOOT=drive supplies a bootstrap source; INCR selects the format increment/interleave parameter; its complete hardware-specific effect is unspecified.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Initialization or formatting can destroy contents.

Example

INICDISC A (FORMAT CARA 5 PISTA 397 SECTOR 32 DIRE 64)

This formats an expendable, correctly configured volume on A using five heads, 397 cylinders and 32 sectors per track, and requests directory capacity. It is not a universal geometry. For DISCO2, PISTA also sets the starting cylinder: 397 gives cylinders 397–793. DIRE 64 can be adjusted to 88 entries. The Chapter 11 procedure explains association, geometry checks, each dialogue response, mounting and file verification.

INICIAR — initialize a floppy

Initialize a floppy.

Syntax

Options. ETIQU, VACIA, DIRE, WP/NOPE, FORMAT, CARA, PISTA, SECTOR, BOOT=drive.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Initialization or formatting can destroy contents.

Example

INICIAR A (FORMAT CARA 2)

This requests the utility's double-sided, double-density floppy format using its remaining geometry defaults. A must denote the intended expendable floppy, and the drive and medium must support that format. It destroys existing contents. Chapter 11 explains the geometry options and subsequent access checks.

LCOMPART — unavailable sharing/account utility

Availability. There is no matching COMANDO executable or standard abbreviation-table entry. The help topic describes inspecting or reconstructing sharing and accounts; it does not establish an available operating procedure.

LISDIR — list catalogue entries

Syntax

O8PRECOM — disk-precompensation and migration utility

O8PRECOM changes disk-controller precompensation state. CAMBVERS.EXEC invokes it during a 6.1/6.1A-to-6.2 migration. It is not a language precompiler. Its controller path writes disk data; complete parameters and recovery behavior remain unspecified. Inspection example only.

Syntax

No documented operational syntax

Options. No central help topic.

Minimum privilege. Unknown standard minimum.

Example

LISDIR O8PRECOM.COMANDO:S

This inspects the utility's catalogue entry without starting its controller-write path. The historical migration role does not supply a safe general maintenance recipe; no execution or compiler workflow is prescribed.

Interface limit. The missing operating contract is not supplied by the filename alone.

PANTALLA — design data-entry screens

Design data-entry screens.

Syntax

PANTALLA [screen-name]

Options. Embedded ;I include, ;M modify, ;B delete field, ;S save, ;F finish, ;G/;R field save/retrieve, ;D/;H move, ;P grid, ;C centre, ;N number, ;L cursor, ;A help.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

PANTALLA AYUDA

This requests the screen designer's help branch. The editor creates .PANT output, but its full save/replace behavior is unspecified. Do not assume files with other screen-related types are interchangeable.

Editor controls include ;I include, ;M modify, ;B delete field, ;S save, ;F finish, ;G/;R save/retrieve field, ;D/;H move, ;P grid, ;C centre, ;N number, ;L cursor and ;A help. Uppercase and lowercase forms differ; these are individual controls within the designer, not CSI commands. Lowercase ;i/;j, ;s/;t and ;p/;q select inverse-video, underline and blink controls. Chapter 35 gives the literal forms, window-movement syntax and remaining limits.

PARAR — stop a partition

Stop a partition.

Syntax

PARAR partition

Options. No established options.

Minimum privilege. 5.

Example

PARAR 2

After the user in partition 2 has saved work and disconnected, this stops that partition while retaining its allocated memory. It is not whole-system shutdown.

PONER — change system or session settings

Change system or session settings.

Syntax

PONER option value

Options. FECHA; ABREV, INCOM, IMPEXEC, MENSAJE, VERSION, ERRNUM, ERRTEXT, HISTORIA, SISFECHA ON/OFF; FORFECHA 1/2/3; ESCAPE, SUBIR, BAJAR, IZQ, DER, CANCEL control definitions.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

PONER IMPEXEC ON

This enables invocation of accessible EXEC files by bare script name. PONER IMPEXEC OFF disables that convenience; full EXEC remains usable.

Partition forms

Memory configuration requires privilege 5. To allocate displayed partition 2 from available module 1, when it is the next unallocated partition, enter:

PONER MEMORIA 2 48000 1

This requests partition 2 in module 1 using 48000 as the size parameter; it does not guarantee that exact byte count. Inspect ESTADO MODULO before allocation and ESTADO MEMORIA afterward. In the demonstrated configuration, the first allocation occupies 0x4C8B–0xFFFF, or 45,941 bytes. Activation is a separate operation. To remove partition 2 after stopping it, when it is the last allocated partition and is not the caller, use the distinct form:

PONER MEMORIA 2

Omitting the size selects removal; appending a zero size is not equivalent. Chapter 12 teaches listing, allocation, activation and removal, including the restrictions on shared modules. The general forms are PONER MEMORIA partition bytes module for allocation and PONER MEMORIA partition for removal.

The timing form requires privilege 5 and is explained in Chapter 25. TIEMPO arithmetic has a discontinuity at 135/136.

REPARAR — check and reconcile filesystem metadata

Check and reconcile filesystem metadata.

Syntax

REPARAR drive [(F)]

Options. F updates allocation and can remove rejected entries or redirects.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Example

REPARAR S

This checks filesystem metadata on S. It can report errors while returning command result 0; read the entire report. The F option authorizes additional repairs and possible entry removal.

Even checking can update the free counter. F can remove rejected entries or sharing redirects; it cannot reconstruct arbitrary application data.

REVIVIR — recover a deleted ascii catalogue entry

Recover a deleted ASCII catalogue entry.

Syntax

Options. Optional numeric search start described by help.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Writes recovery metadata.

Example

This syntax illustration starts the recovery search at sector 160 for the named deleted ASCII entry. The number is illustrative, not a prescribed location. Recovery writes metadata; success and collision handling are not guaranteed.

RUN — run compiled basic

Run compiled BASIC.

Syntax

RUN name[.type[:drive]] [(option)]

Options. Default type BASICCOM. Help advertises TRACE for runtime addresses and COMA for decimal comma; the demonstrated greeting and numeric programs do not show those effects. See the qualifications below.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

RUN HELLO.BASICCOM:S

This executes the compiled BASIC program from Chapter 15. The BASIC runtime is required; the file is not a native Z80 command.

A successful RUN result does not prove that an optional word was recognized. RUN with TRACE produces no visible runtime-address trace for the greeting and numeric examples, and COMA leaves PRINT 1.5 displayed as 1.5 in the numeric example. An arbitrary option also completes with the same numeric output. These observations do not establish input-conversion behavior or exclude other internal effects. See runtime TRACE and runtime COMA.

BASIC with TRACE traces compilation, a separate operation described in Chapter 15. Do not substitute it for a runtime debugger.

SECIBM — export sequential records to ibm-3741

Export sequential records to IBM-3741.

Syntax

SECIBM source.name:drive destination-drive [(options)]

Options. Optional output name and record length, maximum 128 by help; default longest source record.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Caution — Writes destination media.

Example

SECIBM NOTE.TEXT:S A

This syntax example exports sequential records from S to an IBM-compatible floppy on A, converting ASCII to EBCDIC. The medium must have suitable headers; overlong records can be truncated.

SECTOR — list a file's occupied sectors

List a file's occupied sectors.

Syntax

SECTOR name.type[:drive]

Options. No established options.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Example

SECTOR NOTE.TEXT:S

This lists the file's occupied logical sectors. It describes allocation, not the file's text. Logical sector numbers require device translation before becoming controller addresses.

SERIAL — edit an executable serial binding

Edit an executable serial binding.

Syntax

SERIAL name.type:drive (options)

Options. SERIAL nnnnn, PANT, NOPANT in embedded help.

Minimum privilege. 5.

Caution — Changes executable policy.

Example

SERIAL DEMO.COMANDO:S (SERIAL 22 PANT)

This syntax illustration binds a disposable executable copy to serial 22 and requests display. Replace 22 only with an intended binding value. This changes executable policy; it does not configure a serial port.

SORT — sort records by keys

Sort records by keys.

Syntax

SORT [input [output [(options)]]]

Options. FICH parameter file; CLAVE position length with A/D and N/U/I; REGSAL, CLAVESAL, NUMSAL, DIRSAL; NOCLAVE; NOSORT; MONTAR/MONTARE/MONTARS/MONTARW; WORK drive; PANT; ESTADIS.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

SORT INPUT.TEXT:S SORTED.TEXT:S (CLAVE 1 4 A U REGSAL)

This documented-form illustration sorts by a four-character uppercase key beginning at position 1, ascending, and writes sorted records. Prepare a compatible input and unused output; complete record-format and option interactions are unspecified.

With only one filename, SORT treats it as a parameter file. Two filenames identify input and output.

SPOOLER — manage deferred printing

Manage deferred printing.

Syntax

SPOOLER option

Options. INIC, SAL, ACTIV, ESTADO, PARA, CAMBIA, ELIM, LIST, FINAL, ALINE, SALVAR, FORM code, IMPR n, RELANZA n page, COPIAS n, RETE/NORETE, COLA A–Z.

Minimum privilege. 0.

Example

SPOOLER ESTADO

This reports the deferred-printing service state. If it is not initialized, diagnose that state before submitting output; Chapter 12 gives the basic initialization sequence.

PARA pauses ordinary output but explicit print/relaunch requests can take precedence. Physical printer completion must be checked separately.

The command first checks for the resident SPOOLER component. The documented system already provides it, so ordinary initialization does not require CARGAR. If that resident component is absent, the fallback refers to the unavailable CARGAR SPOOLER command. A separate VACIAR SPOOLER reference also lacks its standalone executable. These references do not provide a usable procedure for loading or unloading the resident component; see Chapter 29.

TECLAS — emit terminal programming sequences

Emit terminal programming sequences.

Syntax

TECLAS

Options. No argument parser established.

Minimum privilege. Unknown standard minimum.

Example

TECLAS

This invokes the terminal-programming sequence emitter. The compatible terminal family and exact effects are unknown, so treat this as identification of the invocation, not a recommended setup step.

TEDITAR — edit small files

Edit small files.

Syntax

TEDITAR name.type[:drive]

Options. No established options.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

TEDITAR NOTE.TEXT:S

This opens the small-file editor for the practice text. Its complete editing and exit dialogue is unspecified; use Chapter 6's EDITAR workflow when you need a dependable creation procedure.

TERMINAL — use a console as a remote terminal

Use a console as a remote terminal.

Syntax

TERMINAL [(options)]

Options. COMUn (default COMU1); IBER or HAZELTIN remote-system mode.

Minimum privilege. 1.

Example

TERMINAL (COMU1)

This selects COMU1 for using the console as a remote terminal. Establish the compatible peer and termination method before entering; a complete escape/exit procedure is unspecified.

TITULO — print large lettering

Print large lettering.

Syntax

TITULO channel title1 [title2 ... title12]

Options. P console; In printer n; up to twelve titles, each up to eight characters by help.

Minimum privilege. Unknown standard minimum.

Example

TITULO P HELLO

This requests large lettering for HELLO on the console selected by P. Each title is limited to eight characters in the documented interface. The utility references MAESTRO.TITULO, whose probable character-pattern role and storage details are described in Appendix C.

UNOSEC — reconstruct logical sector 1

Reconstruct logical sector 1.

Syntax

UNOSEC drive [(options)]

Options. C=heads (default 4), P=tracks (243), S=sectors (42), E=directory entries; otherwise infer directory boundary.

Minimum privilege. 5.

Caution — Overwrites volume metadata.

Example

UNOSEC A (C=5 P=397 S=32 E=1208)

This is a parameter-reading illustration, not a repair recipe: it specifies heads, tracks, sectors and directory entries. Every value must come from the target volume's actual format. The command overwrites volume metadata.

VERFORM — check media formatting

Check media formatting.

Syntax

VERFORM drive

Options. No established options.

Minimum privilege. 3.

Example

VERFORM A

This checks the format of the volume associated with A. Use it after initialization and MONTAR, then verify an ordinary file copy with COPIFI (VERIFI), as taught in Chapter 11. Read diagnostics as well as the completion result. Its complete side-effect contract is not specified; do not substitute it for a backup or assume it is an assured read-only diagnostic.

VOLCAR — dump file or logical-sector bytes

Dump file or logical-sector bytes. Interface unspecified; inspection example only.

Syntax

Complete syntax not established

Options. Code has file and inclusive first/last-sector paths; P-leading console and I-leading printer options.

Minimum privilege. 2.

Example

LISDIR VOLCAR.COMANDO:S

This inspects the utility entry. VOLCAR displays hexadecimal and character bytes, but its complete invocation grammar is unspecified and some output paths write files or operate tape. No guessed dump command is offered.

Interface limit. The missing operating contract is not supplied by the filename alone.

Chapter 32 — System service reference

All 111 RST 0x08 entries appear below in numeric order. Names describe functions; they are not official symbolic identifiers. Each entry specifies its function, stored and initialized targets, inputs, outputs, known register effects and restrictions. An omitted register guarantee is unknown; it must not be inferred from another service. Error flags are service-specific.

The common entry convention is RST 08H followed by DB number; the return address skips that byte. The instruction spelling is illustrative Z80 notation, not proof of an installed assembler. For example, service 0x02 is encoded CF 02. Chapter 18 provides an executable byte-exact example using 0x02 and 0x00; Chapter 17 supplies documented BASIC callers of file services. Each entry includes a calling-sequence example. Labels such as request, buffer and callback denote caller-prepared storage or code; they are not built-in symbols. Allocate and initialize them according to the input contract and Appendix E before making the call. Fragments demonstrate argument placement and selection, not a standalone assembled program. Maintenance calls retain their full preconditions and risks.

Data structures are defined in Appendix E; executable and runtime formats in Appendix A. Chapter 24 explains dispatch and overlays, Chapter 25 synchronization and Chapter 27 error policy. The table below is the canonical target index.
