EXEC instruction reference

EXEC directives belong inside an EXEC text file. The ampersand distinguishes them from ordinary commands that the file asks CSI to execute. Chapter 8 teaches file creation, invocation, variables, comparisons and loops. The following index accounts for all nineteen dispatcher names; END is an additional block delimiter rather than a dispatcher entry.

Directive Operation or interface limit
&TYPE Display fields and substituted values; Chapter 8.
&BEGTYPE Display literal lines through END; Chapter 8.
&SPACE Output line spacing; see the example below.
&CONTINUE Continue reading the next command-file line; not BASIC execution resumption.
&QUIT Finish this EXEC level, optionally returning a result; Chapter 8.
&STACK Queue a line for program input.
&BEGSTACK Queue literal lines through END.
&SKIP Move the command-file reading position by a line count.
&GOTO Branch to a command-file label; Chapter 8.
&CONTROL Select echo/control and queued-input handling.
&IF Test a condition before executing the rest of the instruction; Chapter 8.
&CRT Send screen-control output or position the cursor.
&ESC Control selected Escape handling; complete mode contract remains unspecified.
&READ Read an input line into named EXEC variables.
&WAIT Request the executive pagination pause; see the example below.
&WHILE Repeat while a condition holds, paired with REPEAT; Chapter 8.
&UNTIL Repeat until a condition holds, paired with REPEAT.
&REPEAT Return to the controlling WHILE or UNTIL statement.
&ERROR Register error handling for later command results; Chapter 8.

SPACE: separate output lines

This syntax illustration belongs in a command file:

&SPACE 2

SPACE issues two line-spacing operations through the executive. With no count it issues one; zero is also treated as one. The count is processed through an eight-bit value, so it should not be treated as an unrestricted page-layout quantity. SPACE is separate from BASIC's SPACE$ string function.

CONTINUE: proceed to the next line

For example, a labelled destination may contain:

-READY &CONTINUE

When execution reaches READY, CONTINUE goes to the next command-file line. It neither restarts a terminated program nor performs BASIC's CONTINUE operation.

READ: obtain an answer

This form occurs in interactive command procedures:

&READ &ANSWER

READ obtains input and assigns a field to ANSWER. Keep beginner answers to a single word within EXEC's eight-character value width. Multiple-variable and empty-input handling need more care than this elementary form; a READ without a variable follows a different command-processing path and is not the same operation.

STACK: provide input to the next program

A command procedure can queue a response instead of waiting for an operator to type it. For example, a procedure that is about to enter BASIC can queue its exit command:

&STACK FIFO QUIT

The queued text is QUIT. FIFO means first in, first out: older queued lines are consumed first. The optional LIFO form instead places newer input ahead of older input. Omitting the order selector uses FIFO. Queuing does not itself execute QUIT; the consuming program must interpret it. BASIC recognizes QUIT, while another program could interpret that same line differently.

To enter BASIC after queuing that response, the next line of the file is:

BASIC

This illustrates the input mechanism. Use it only after saving any BASIC work. A queued response can answer a destructive confirmation as readily as an editing prompt; do not reuse input queues without knowing the receiving program's dialogue.

BEGSTACK: provide several literal input lines

This syntax illustration supplies BASIC statements as queued input:

&BEGSTACK FIFO
PRINT 1
QUIT
&END

BEGSTACK reads literal lines until END and queues them in the selected order. These lines are input for a later program, not EXEC instructions, and the block does not substitute EXEC variables. A following BASIC command would consume the PRINT and QUIT lines. This differs from repeated STACK instructions, where substitutions can construct each response.

CONTROL: echo and queued-input policy

Command procedures commonly begin:

&CONTROL OFF NOSTACK

OFF suppresses command echo. NOSTACK selects a queued-input control mode; it does not prohibit later STACK instructions. It sets bit 3 of the partition's execution-control byte, while STACK clears that bit. The complete downstream effect of this flag remains unspecified. Existing procedures combine this mode with queued responses for the utilities they invoke. STACK selects the opposite control mode:

&CONTROL STACK

These CONTROL words are separate from FIFO/LIFO, which choose queue insertion order. Complete interactions with interruptions, nested interpreters and abnormal program termination remain unspecified; use an existing procedure's deliberate sequence rather than changing modes by analogy with another command language.

SKIP: bypass lines

The following instruction appears in source-insertion procedures:

&SKIP 2

It advances past two subsequent command-file lines. The count addresses physical file lines, so changing comments or inserted text can alter the destination. A labelled GOTO is clearer for new procedures when the destination should survive such editing. Negative-count handling exists but is not an elementary backward-loop interface.

UNTIL: repeat until a condition is true

This syntax illustration uses the same condition and REPEAT machinery as Chapter 8:

&A = 1
&UNTIL &A > 3
&TYPE &A
&A = &A + 1
&REPEAT
&QUIT

UNTIL tests the condition at the beginning of the group. The group continues while A is not greater than 3; REPEAT returns to that test. It therefore differs from languages in which an UNTIL test always occurs after the first iteration. Source-insertion procedures use this form to collect a fixed number of arguments.

CRT, ESC and WAIT

CRT CLEAR is used before interactive procedure displays:

&CRT CLEAR

It requests the terminal clear operation. The numeric CRT form takes horizontal and vertical positions; the interpreter subtracts one from each before emitting the terminal-positioning control sequence. For example:

&CRT 47 14

This requests position 47 on line 14 on a compatible terminal. Device handling remains significant; it is not a portable screen-graphics API.

Procedures also use this Escape-control form around operations with their own diagnostics:

&ESC O OFF

The matching form is:

&ESC O ON

These select and restore an O-mode control flag. The complete meaning of that flag and all ESC modes remains unspecified; these two lines are forms to recognize when reading procedures, not a recommendation to suppress errors or interruption generally. WAIT invokes the executive's pagination-pause service. Its elementary form is:

&WAIT

It calls service 0x31 and resumes command-file reading after that service returns. No duration is parsed; this is not a timed sleep instruction. The exact pause and cancellation behavior follows the active console's pagination policy; see service 0x31.

Special operands

Special operands supply values or form part of expressions; they are not commands to enter at CSI.

Operand Meaning or interface limit
&RETCODE Result of the preceding command or child procedure; see Chapter 8.
&INDEX Number of positional arguments, up to sixteen; see Chapter 8.
&NULL Empty, space-filled value; used in argument and variable comparisons.
&PAGE, &LINE Values obtained through console geometry service 0x3B; not a document page counter. Complete operator interpretation remains unspecified.
&TYP, &LEN, &LIT Recognized expression names; full argument, result and quoting contracts remain unspecified.
&CAT String-construction operand used by vocabulary procedures; full general quoting and concatenation rules remain unspecified.
&SUB Inclusive start/end substring selection; see Chapter 8.

For example, this procedure fragment checks whether ARG has no value:

&IF &ARG EQ &NULL &GOTO -MISSING

MISSING must be a label in the same procedure. Do not substitute the literal word NULL for &NULL. For general string joining, use the documented vertical-bar expression from Chapter 8 until the complete CAT contract is available.

EDITAR internal commands

EDITAR edits sequential text and displays its own asterisk prompt. Its command names are independent of CSI abbreviations and BASIC's editor commands. Begin with the working CASE U, INPUT and FILE procedure in Chapter 7.

The table below preserves all forty-seven internal names. A stored minimum is a parser threshold, not a guarantee that every prefix selects that row: earlier matching rows can take precedence. Full names avoid that ambiguity. A name and handler identify an available parser branch, but do not by themselves establish its arguments, default range or modifying effects. Where those details are unspecified, no invented edit command is supplied.

Full editor command Stored minimum length Operation or limitation
AGAIN 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
TABSET 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
HELP 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
NEXT 1 Shares the DOWN handler; complete movement arguments unspecified.
UP 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
BOTTOM 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
TYPE 1 Shares the LIST handler; complete range/output syntax unspecified.
FILE 4 Write and leave the editor; see Chapter 7 for pending-interruption behavior.
SAVE 4 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
LOCATE 1 Source-insertion procedures use LOCATE followed by a marker string. Full matching rules unspecified.
ZONE 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
UNNUMBER 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
HTAB 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
X 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
Y 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
Z 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
VERIFY 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
WRAPMODE 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
FIND 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
RENUMBER 3 Source-insertion procedures use RENUMBER 10 10 with line mode active. Full range and collision rules unspecified.
PAGE 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
TOP 2 Used to position source-insertion operations; subsequent editing mode matters.
QUIT 4 Exit path; do not assume it saves changes. Use the Chapter 7 FILE procedure.
? 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
INPUT 1 INPUT text inserts the following text; Chapter 7.
REPLACE 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
DELETE 2 Used with a numeric argument by source-insertion procedures; default range and recovery are unspecified.
MODIFY 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
CASE 2 CASE U preserves uppercase input; Chapter 7.
COLUMN 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
COMBINE 3 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
CSI 3 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
TRUNCATE 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
NAME 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
LINEMODE 3 ON/OFF forms occur in source-insertion procedures; effects on numbering and position require care.
PROMPT 3 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
CHANGE 1 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
DUP 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
LIST 2 Shares the TYPE handler; complete range/output syntax unspecified.
DOWN 1 Shares the NEXT handler; complete movement arguments unspecified.
GET 3 Source-insertion procedures use GET name BASIC S to import sequential source. Full insertion-position and error rules unspecified.
PUTD 4 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
NUMBER 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
PUT 3 Source-insertion procedures use PUT name BASIC S followed by a numeric limit. Complete limit semantics unspecified.
ERROR 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
SKIP 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.
SPLIT 2 Recognized editor command; complete argument and effect contract unspecified.

For a safe first insertion at the asterisk prompt, enter:

INPUT A SHORT LINE

This inserts A SHORT LINE into the current text. It does not execute that text. Save through FILE as described in Chapter 7. Advanced source-insertion procedures deliberately change LINEMODE, position, import text and renumber lines; those sequences are not interchangeable with BASIC editor commands bearing the same names. CEDIT and TEDITAR are separate editors whose complete internal dialogues remain unspecified.

CONSULTA vocabulary and dictionary dependencies

CONSULTA interprets Spanish query text against file and attribute definitions. CONTROL.VOC supplies vocabulary maintained by the VOC and GENVOC procedures. CONSULTA uses a work file whose name ends with the execution partition number, such as CONSULTA.TRAB1 for partition 1. See Appendix C for those support tools and Chapter 31 for entry requirements.

The following statement is the dictionary-listing form used by VOC. It belongs to CONSULTA, not CSI. It requires a correctly initialized CONTROL.VOC and compatible work storage:

LISTAR CONTROL VOC VIS NOMBRE ATRIBUTO CAMPO LONGITUD FORMATO ROTULO SUP.ID CABECERA 'Diccionario del fichero CONTROL' @FECHA

LISTAR selects listing; CONTROL VOC names the dictionary file. VIS selects terminal output. The following attribute names choose the displayed fields. SUP.ID and CABECERA control presentation; the quoted text supplies a heading, and @FECHA requests date substitution. These attribute names come from the dictionary and are not universal reserved words for every application file. The complete width, pagination and query-error contract is unspecified. The wrapper example below avoids typing a long query at the system prompt.

At CSI, this procedure form requests the same dictionary listing:

EXEC VOC LIST

VOC queues its query and enters CONSULTA. It requires the dictionary to exist; it is not an instruction to create one automatically. To print rather than display, the wrapper's IMPR function uses a configured printer.

Reserved vocabulary

The table groups words with identical parser-result bytes. This shows where the parser gives several spellings the same token; it does not guarantee that whole statements can be rearranged freely. Punctuation is significant: POR.DESC, NO.COMP, @PAGINA and comparison symbols are literal forms. SON occurs twice in the vocabulary, with different result pairs.

Words Parser result bytes Interpretation boundary
LISTAR, BUSCAR 0x00, 0x00 Same parser result; surrounding statement grammar still applies.

The vocabulary includes operations, selection words, comparison synonyms, output controls, substitutions and connecting words. It is not a list of independent system commands. In particular, CONSULTA's CLASIFICAR does not establish a CSI command called CLASIF. A complete grammar for arbitrary queries is not yet available; the dictionary-listing form above should not be extended using SQL conventions.

PANTALLA: case-sensitive screen controls

PANTALLA designs data-entry screens. Its uppercase semicolon controls are listed in Chapter 31. The following lowercase controls are additionally advertised for label appearance:

Literal controls Label attribute
;i / ;j Inverse video
;s / ;t Underlining
;p / ;q Blinking

For example, the literal control is:

;i

It belongs to the inverse-video pair inside PANTALLA. The complete set/clear direction, persistence and behavior on each terminal are unspecified; do not assume it is equivalent to uppercase ;I, which inserts a field. Likewise lowercase ;s is distinct from uppercase ;S, which saves the design.

Window movement is advertised in this form:

;<arrow>nn<return>

After the semicolon, press an arrow key, enter the number of positions and press Return. The angle-bracket expressions denote keys, not literal text. Exact arrow encoding and movement limits depend on the terminal and remain incompletely specified.

Appendix A. Program, language and archive formats

This appendix distinguishes executable relocation, tokenized BASIC source, compiled BASIC instructions, runtime values and EXEC work frames. Similar token bytes can mean different things in these namespaces. There is no established general assembler-object/library specification merely because legacy messages mention ABS, REL, COM, symbols or PABs.

Native executable and submodule formats

An R load image starts at file offset 0 and includes code and initialized data. The command-execution path enters a command at its loaded base, but loading an R submodule does not by itself specify where to begin execution. BASIC overlays have a component byte followed by a JP slot at offset 0x0001; SORT overlays place their entry slots after a 0xF0-byte control area. The caller must follow the component-specific interface described in Appendix C. Directory+0x1E supplies image length, +0x11 total stored sectors and +0x15 firstLBA. When present, relocation records start at ceil(image_bytes/256)*256; a module requiring no fixups can have no relocation sector. Each relocation sector has a one-byte count 1–127 followed by that manyLE16 image offsets. Add the load base to theLE16 word at each offset, modulo 65536. Validate that both bytes lie in the image before applying a fixup in a loader or format reader.

A images load at directory+0x1E. The complete general A-image runtime contract is less well specified than R loading. An optional CF41 prologue is preceded by minimum privilege and three serial bytes, as described in Chapter 30; it is not a mandatory file header.

The service 0x62 compact submodule descriptor is seven bytes: load addressLE16 at+0, image lengthLE16 at+2, stored-sector byte at+4 and relativeLBALE16 at+5. Its caller supplies the baseLBA.BASICUSR modules can return through an defined BASIC wrapper; this does not establish a general linker/library toolchain.

BASICOBJ source records

Record offset Size Meaning
00 1 Total record length N, including this byte and terminator
01 2 Source line number, big-endian
03 1 Number of spaces between line number and statement in specified source
04 N−5 Tokenized statement body
N−1 1 CR (0Dh)

A zero record-length byte ends the source stream; 0x1A follows as sequential padding. A record length must include its final CR. A malformed length or missing terminator is a source-format error; do not guess subsequent line boundaries.

Bytes Tested interpretation
20 followed by remaining text REM statement; comment spelling/spaces preserved
29 END statement
3A PRINT statement
05 hi lo Integer literal, big-endian word; sign depends on source context
06 length text... String literal; one-byte length followed by exact characters
1C before numeric literal Unary minus in specified source
03 followed by 8 bytes Packed decimal numeric literal, also used above 32767

BASICCOM header and expressions

Offset Size Meaning
00 1 Required zero checked by runtime; not the R-executable magic
01 1 Array-base policy:00 sets 0,01 sets 1;FF retains current policy
02 2 BE Program-relative linked-definition head;zero means no list
04 2 BE Program-relative DATA head copied toIX+4B/+4C;zero when no DATA
06 variable Main compiled statement stream begins
Low opcode Following bytes Role
03 8 Packed decimal literal
04 2 BE Integer literal/value word
06 1 length + that many bytes String literal
07 Encoded name through its first high-bit-set byte Variable reference
08 none Unary minus
0F/10/11 none NOT/AND/OR
12/13/14 none Greater/less/equal comparisons
18/19/1A/1B/1C none Power/multiply/divide/add/subtract
1D none One-dimensional array access after variable/index operands
1F none String concatenation
30 none ABS function marker within an expression
21 none Native-function apply with one argument in specified form
26 none User-function apply with one argument in specified form

Expression opcode bit 7 marks the end of the expression. It is distinct from the high bit terminating an encoded variable name. For example, compiled 2+3*4 is 04 00 02 04 00 03 04 00 04 19 9B: operands precede multiply and add, with the final add carrying the end bit. Compiled PRINT constants can therefore use 84 for integer, 83 for decimal and 86 for string without introducing three new underlying literal types.

Tested encoded names include A=C1, AB=41 C2, I%=89, S$=F3, FNT=46 4E D4 and X=D8. A selected ARR reference reduces to40 80; a complete general name-encoding algorithm has not been established. Do not infer it from those examples alone.

Branches and mutable loop state

Compiled form Fields after opcode Runtime meaning
2E IF condition expression; false-target BE16 Zero selects the target; nonzero skips its two bytes.
2D GOTO target BE16 Transfer to target; also emitted to skip ELSE.
2C GOSUB target BE16 Save continuation and transfer.

2A range FOR NEXT offset BE16; encoded name; initial, limit and step expressions Assign initial value, initialize NEXT state, test range and enter/skip body.
53 expression-list FOR NEXT offset BE16; encoded name; expressions ending with 0Dh Evaluate one expression for each body iteration.
Offset from NEXT Integer 34 Real 63 List 64
+01 BE16 encoded-name offset in FOR same same
+03 BE16 loop-body offset same same

+07 / +0D BE16 signed step at+07 eight-byte step at+0D none
Total record bytes 9 21 7
Position Size Meaning
+00 1 36h, computed ON opcode
+01 variable Selector expression, ending with its expression-opcode bit 7
after expression 1 2Dh GOTO or 2Ch GOSUB in documented forms
next 1 Unsigned target count N
next 2×N Big-endian target offsets relative to BASICCOM byte zero, including its six-byte header
after table — Next statement and computed-GOSUB continuation

Compiled GOSUB pushes a three-byte continuation FF plus a big-endian program-relative offset; the live continuation-top pointer atIX-2 is little-endian. Interpreted computed calls instead use a seven-byte source continuation, whose final four context bytes have only selected meanings. NEXT state is mutable in RAM and begins zero in the compiled file's corresponding fields.

ON's selector is one-based. A missing target is stored 0000 and falls through; computed GOSUB returns after the complete table. Selector 0 takes the observed error 0x14 path and result 253. ON ERROR uses opcode 55 plus a BE16 target; its live pointer is atIX-0x0C/-0x0B. The complete grammar of all error/branch combinations remains incomplete.

Eight-byte decimal and data records

Offset Size Meaning
00 1 byte Exponent biased by 80h;00 is the arithmetic zero convention
01 bit 7 1 bit Sign:0 positive,1 negative
01 bits 6–4 3 bits Zero in specified normalized output; runtime result normalization clears them
01 bits 3–0 1 decimal digit Leading significant digit
02–07 6 bytes Remaining twelve digits, high nibble then low nibble in each byte

Let E=byte 0-0x80 and C be the 13-digit decimal coefficient from the low nibble ofbyte 1 and the twelve following BCD digits. A nonzero value is sign*C*10^(E-13). Exponent byte zero is the arithmetic zero convention. Examples: 45.5=82 04 55 00 00 00 00 00; -0.5=80 85 00 00 00 00 00 00. Bits 6–4 ofbyte 1 are zero in normalized specified output; arbitrary malformed-input behavior is not established.

BASIC WRITE fields use 03 plus decimal payload, 04 plus signed BE16 integer, and 06 plus one-byte string length and characters. Byte 00 terminates a record; 0x1A denotes EOF at a field/type boundary. Source literals instead use 05 for the specified small integer and a separate unary-minus token where observed. These distinctions prevent a source decoder from misreading data records.

DATA chains use the compiled header+4 head. READ opcode 54 carries 07-encoded names; RESTORE opcode 3F supplies a BE16 resolved target. DATA literals do not use the expression-end high bit. Runtime IX offsets and scalar structures follow.

Relative to IX Size Code-supported role
−34h/−33h 2 LE Compiled program base
−02h/−01h 2 LE Current BASIC continuation-record top; compiled GOSUB records are FF+BE16 relative offset, while interpreted 230 records occupy seven bytes
−30h/−2Fh 2 LE Current expression/temporary stack pointer
−2Ch/−2Bh 2 LE Variable/definition linked-list head
+1Dh 1 Array-base byte 0/1, also compared with compiled header+1
+4Bh/+4Ch 2 LE Program-relative DATA cursor initialized from header+4
Flags Payload after flags
03 Eight-byte packed decimal
04 BE16 signed integer
06 BE16 string-descriptor pointer
0B BE16 upper index; consecutive eight-byte decimal elements
Offset Bytes Observed meaning
+00 1 Continuation-token offset within the calling source record;14h at its final CR in these calls
+01 2 Calling source line, big endian
+03 1 Copy of pre-call context IX+1Eh; broader meaning unknown here
+04 1 Copy of IX+1Fh
+05 1 Copy of IX+20h
+06 1 Copy of IX+3Ah

EXEC level frame

Each level uses 512 bytes in a direct work file. Arguments occupy 0x000–0x07F as sixteen eight-byte values. Variables occupy 0x080–0x17F as sixteen name 8/value 8 pairs. Bytes 0x180–0x1F1 remain unclassified. Byte 0x1F2 saves context+0x38 with bit 0 cleared; 0x1F3–0x1F5 save context+0x2A–0x2C; 0x1F6–0x1FF save context+0x2D–0x36. Within the latter, 0x1F6–0x1FD contain the script name and 0x1FE is an opaque word.

Level N begins at byte 512*(N-1), or direct record 4*N-3 for 128-byte records. Original EXEC1–EXEC4 files each contain sixteen 128-byte records, sufficient for four 512-byte frames per file. This is an installation allocation, not a universally documented maximum nesting depth. CONECTAR derives capacity from twice the low block-count byte; malformed/extreme cases remain unknown.

The strings OASIS and OASIS-ARCHIVE support lineage identification; they do not prove compatibility with another release's tape utilities. Archive payload ordering, controller handshake, retries and complete restoration remain unresolved. The label widths 04096 identify numeric template spans, not independently documented universal record-size guarantees.

Appendix B. Memory, I/O, interrupt and routine maps

Addresses here refer to the initialized common image unless a bank, overlay or relative driver offset is specified. Dynamic driver addresses must be obtained from descriptors. Low port numbers identify decoded device groups; Z80 I/O instructions generate 16-bit addresses and the complete hardware upper-address decode remains unspecified. Account for the full address presented by the instruction.

FE W Active-high interrupt mask Probable, execution validated

RST 0x00 changes during startup: the newly relocated kernel jumps through it to 0x5157 before initialization; at the observed login state its target is 0x34F7. These are successive boot phases, not interchangeable live entry addresses. See Chapter 21.

For WD FORMAT 0x50, port 0xB8 receives a sector-sized table buffer containing flag/sector-ID pairs. With 32 sectors and 256-byte size, 64 bytes describe the entries and the host still supplies the full 256-byte buffer. The count is at 0xBA. Normal completion signaling can accompany an error; inspect 0xBF and the error register at 0xB9. The original OS's Ignore policy can subsequently return success without valid data, as explained in Chapter 27.

Interrupt and DMA map

Path Supplied opcode/vector Established use
SS2 level 2 D7 / RST10 /0x0010 Serial source E0 and timer source C0 selected through port 60
WD level 4 E7 / RST20 /0x0020 Disk
ALP level 5 EF / RST28 /0x0028 SCC receive and DMA completion
Tape-related RST18 /0x0018 Registry 03 route; physical delivery unspecified
Context hook RST38 /0x0038 Context+0x39 inline hook

Port 0xFE supplies an active-high mask in the specified interrupt model. Highest-active-level arbitration remains provisional. The timer uses 8254 counter 1=8000, mode 2; a 1-MHz source gives an 8-ms tick. A 4-MHz CPU configuration is used for the timing values here; the complete physical clock routing is unspecified.

DMA0 channels 0/1 serve PAN1 TX/RX and 2/3 PAN2; DMA1 channels 0/1 serve PAN3 and 2/3 PAN4. The installed receive path uses SCC interrupts and CPU reads, not RX DMA. TX uses DTR/REQ; channel-pair bank latches select DMA memory independently of portFD. The serial source 2.4576 MHz supports the observed baud configuration; SS2 source routing remains inferred.

Floppy packet and return translation

The private inputs areA=count, B=head, C=logical sector within track, DE=track, HL=buffer andIY=descriptor. Transfer adapter receives common bank byte 0x046B and buffer address. DIS2 programs port 0xC2 with the bank, address high byte and address low byte. The successful driver path advances C and H by count without an established general carry propagation; physical adapter carry across 64 KiB remains unspecified.

Packet offset Meaning / construction
00 Read 06 or write 05; OR40 whenN=1
01 Saved unit with bit 2 cleared, OR B rotated left twice
02 Track E
03 Head B
04 First physical sector: C+1 forN=1;2*C+1 forN=0
05 Size code N:1 for 256-byte sectors;0 for 128-byte sectors
06 Last physical sector: first+count−1 forN=1;first+2*count−1 forN=0
07 Gap value 0E forN=1;07 forN=0
08 Data-length value 00 forN=1;80 forN=0
Condition Returned A Exact common-kernel message
ST0 bit 3 01 no prep.
ST1 bit 1 02 prot. escritura
ST1 bit 0 03 no inicializado
ST2 bit 5 04 error transferencia
ST2 bit 4 08 no existe pista
ST2 bit 1 or bit 0 03 no inicializado
ST1 bit 4, DIS1 only 09 error cabeza
ST1 bit 5 09 error cabeza
ST1 bit 2 07 no existe sector
No specified condition 00 Success path

Appendix C. Operating-system files and language components

System files supply the executive, command environment, programming languages and device support. Other files hold account definitions, messages and temporary work state. This appendix groups related components so that a numbered family can be understood as a whole. Utility COMANDO files are described through their commands in Chapter 31.

The sizes and dates below identify particular component versions; replacement programs and growing work files can have different values. Optional components need not be present on every system.

Reading the file details

Each table below identifies a file in the Component column, followed by its storage format, size information and catalogue timestamp. Read the Format column before interpreting Contents (bytes): the meaning of the size depends on the file's organization.

Format column

The following codes are used in the Format column of this appendix's component tables. They describe how a file is stored or loaded, independently of its name or type suffix.

Format code Meaning What Contents (bytes) measures
A Absolute machine-code program, loaded at a specified address Stored bytes in whole 256-byte sectors; the total can include padding.
D Direct file containing fixed-length records Record capacity: number of records multiplied by bytes per record.
R Relocatable machine-code program, adjusted to its load address Load-image length, including code and initialized data but excluding relocation information and sector padding.
S Sequential file, read as text or serialized binary records Text tables count CR-inclusive content without terminal padding. Tables labelled “stored binary payload” count all linked payload, including padding.
I Indexed file with keyed records and an ordered index Active record data fields; excludes keys, index entries and unused record slots.

For example, R beside SISTEMA.CSI means that it contains relocatable machine code. D beside SISTEMA.EXEC1 means that it provides direct-record work storage, not another copy of the EXEC interpreter. These letters describe file formats; S in this column is unrelated to the :S system-volume association in a filename.

Contents, allocated space and dates

Contents (bytes) gives the size defined by the format table above. For S-format text files, the count includes every complete CR-terminated record before the terminal 0x1A padding. Sector links, padding and unused allocation are excluded. This interpretation applies to these text files; it is not a general instruction to truncate every binary S-format file at its first 0x1A byte.

Allocated (bytes) gives the disk space reserved for the file in 1,024-byte blocks. For example, SISTEMA.CSI has a 3,104-byte load image containing code and initialized data, but occupies 4,096 bytes of allocated space, which also accommodates relocation information and padding. SISTEMA.AYUDA contains 37,676 bytes of text and record delimiters within a 38,912-byte allocation. For D files, Contents is record capacity, not a count of occupied records. For the additional binary S-format tables, the subsection explicitly identifies the count as stored binary payload: each linked sector contributes 254 payload bytes after its two-byte link. Padding is included because the exact program or data endpoint is unspecified. These are not decoded executable lengths.

Read protection is an attribute independent of native format. The compiled BASIC support programs use S storage with read protection; a catalogue display can show SR. They are not R-format relocatable Z80 programs. Indexed MAESTRO.VOC uses yet another measurement: its six active 512-byte data fields total 3,072 bytes, excluding keys and index structure.

Catalogue date and time gives the timestamp stored in the file's catalogue entry. It is file metadata, not necessarily a compilation date or software release date. Work files can change as the system is used, and their dates and allocation sizes can change too.

Where program and record sizes are stored

Program-loading information is held in the 32-byte catalogue entry rather than a universal header at the beginning of every program. For R files, entry offset +0x1E holds the image length, +0x11 the stored-sector count and +0x15 the starting sector. When relocation records are present, they follow the image rounded up to a 256-byte boundary. Some small R modules need no relocation records. For A files, +0x1E instead holds the absolute load address. For D files, +0x11 is the record count and +0x17 the record length. These fields must be interpreted according to format. Appendix A explains loading and language formats; Appendix E gives the byte layouts.

Executive and command environment

SISTEMA.NUCLEO contains the executive, initialization material and kernel overlays. The bootstrap loads it before the command environment. Its absolute load address is 0x0000; the stored component occupies 113 sectors, or 28,928 bytes. GENSIST can save configuration into this component, so it is not a text configuration file to edit by hand.

SISTEMA.STARTUP is executable initialization code that prepares system state, including overlay storage. SISTEMA.CSI is the command processor that reads commands and selects programs. On program completion, the system can reload CSI rather than return to a permanent command-processor stack. SISTEMA.EXECLANG interprets EXEC procedures, including variables, branching and nested execution. It is used by EXEC HELLO from Chapter 8; an EXEC.COMANDO file is not required.

SISTEMA.STARTUP begins by saving AF, DE, BC, HL and IY: its first six bytes are F5 D5 C5 E5 FD E5. Its 548-byte load image is followed by sector padding and one relocation sector containing 41 offsets. The complete stored length is 1,024 bytes. There is no opening jump-and-version header.

SISTEMA.IPL is an absolute-format component with load address 0xE000. Its code searches for SISTEMA.NUCLEO, accesses floppy-controller port families and copies 0x7000 bytes into low RAM before transferring through the reset vector. This indicates a kernel-loading role, but its launch requirements and participation in the hard-disk startup path remain unspecified. Its copy length differs from the 0x7100 stored bytes of the NUCLEO component listed here. It is not a documented operator reboot procedure.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SISTEMA.NUCLEO A 28,928 29,696 1986-04-15 10:48
SISTEMA.STARTUP R 548 1,024 1985-02-04 16:07
SISTEMA.CSI R 3,104 4,096 1981-12-11 07:54
SISTEMA.EXECLANG R 5,146 7,168 1981-12-11 07:55
SISTEMA.IPL A 1,792 2,048 1984-01-09 16:59

Account, help and device definitions

SISTEMA.CUENTA is the sequential account database. It holds names, identifiers, passwords where assigned and privileges; its special owner byte is 0xFF. Use the CUENTA procedures for listing accounts, creating or removing an account and changing a password. A listing can reveal stored passwords and should be kept private.

CONECTAR.COMANDO performs account connection and profile processing. It reads and displays SISTEMA.CONECTAR as sequential welcome/banner text during connection. The banner is display data, not executable connection code; its wording can be installation-specific.

SISTEMA.AYUDA holds command-help topics. AYUDA reads those topics, with the fallback limitation described in the command reference. SISTEMA.MENSERR holds 200 fixed records of 64 bytes, giving 12,800 bytes of message capacity. Message-record numbers are separate from BASIC error codes and command completion results; the stored message catalogue provides the lookup.

Account definitions in SISTEMA.CUENTA and device definitions in SISTEMA.NOMBDISP are system configuration, whose contents depend on the installation. SISTEMA.AYUDA and SISTEMA.MENSERR instead provide system reference text. Their shared use by system facilities does not make account names, banner wording or device choices universal defaults.

SISTEMA.NOMBDISP contains sequential device-name definitions. They supply the physical-name vocabulary used for device associations; a definition does not imply that the corresponding hardware is fitted. Chapter 12 explains active associations.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SISTEMA.CUENTA S 40 1,024 1990-01-01 00:37
SISTEMA.CONECTAR S 436 1,024 1984-01-07 20:14
CONECTAR.COMANDO R 2,141 3,072 1982-09-24 00:15
SISTEMA.AYUDA S 37,676 38,912 1985-06-19 14:09
SISTEMA.MENSERR D 12,800 13,312 1983-12-07 16:17
SISTEMA.NOMBDISP S 783 1,024 1984-01-01 04:29

EXEC work files: SISTEMA.EXEC1–SISTEMA.EXEC4

These four files have the same purpose for different execution partitions. The numeric suffix selects the displayed partition number: EXEC1 belongs to partition 1, EXEC2 to partition 2, and so on. They hold EXEC nesting state and are managed by the interpreter. They are not four versions of EXEC and do not contain user-written command procedures. Saved frames can remain after use; nonzero work bytes alone do not establish that a procedure is still running, and zero-filled work storage can still be required.

Each listed file contains sixteen 128-byte direct records, giving 2,048 bytes of capacity. EXEC uses a 512-byte frame per nesting level, so this allocation holds four frames. The record size and the frame size are different units. Four frames describe this allocation, not a universal maximum nesting depth. Missing or unusable work capacity can prevent automatic account profiles. The frame layout is described in Appendix A.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SISTEMA.EXEC1 D 2,048 2,048 1983-10-14 08:21
SISTEMA.EXEC2 D 2,048 2,048 1987-01-01 00:04
SISTEMA.EXEC3 D 2,048 2,048 1987-01-01 00:04
SISTEMA.EXEC4 D 2,048 2,048 1987-01-01 00:05

BASIC entry programs and runtime

BASIC.COMANDO selects interactive editing and interpretation when invoked without a source filename, or compilation when given a saved BASICOBJ source file. RUN.COMANDO starts compiled BASICCOM programs through the BASIC runtime. BASIC.LOADFILE supplies the compiled-program runtime, including compiled-header handling, statement and expression evaluation, and loading named BASICUSR extensions. It is an implementation component, not a saved user program.

Follow Chapter 15 to run the same program interactively and after compilation. Keep BASICOBJ source for editing: BASICCOM contains prepared runtime instructions rather than standalone Z80 code.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
BASIC.COMANDO R 854 2,048 1981-12-11 08:00
BASIC.LOADFILE R 24,175 27,648 1983-04-11 15:02
RUN.COMANDO R 1,842 3,072 1981-12-11 07:59

BASIC.OVERLAY1–BASIC.OVERLAY4

These files contain different portions of the BASIC environment. An overlay supplies code when it is required so that all language facilities need not occupy the execution area simultaneously. Their suffixes identify components selected by BASIC, not user numbers, execution partitions or successive versions of one file. BASIC loads them as needed; they are not separate commands to invoke at CSI.

The following responsibilities explain why the family contains several different images. They describe identified facilities, not a complete assignment of every editor command or numbered compiler pass.

Component Role
BASIC.OVERLAY1 Source parsing and tokenization, editor/language keyword tables, and embedded syntax help.
BASIC.OVERLAY2 Source-reference reporting, including line/label and variable/constant references.
BASIC.OVERLAY3 Compilation and compiled-output construction, including branch emission and compiler statistics.
BASIC.OVERLAY4 Interpreted statement execution, including file operations and named BASICUSR loading.

Each overlay begins with its component number, 0x01 through 0x04, followed by a JP entry slot at file offset 0x0001. BASIC.LOADFILE likewise begins 04 C3 0A 01. The component byte is data, not an instruction to execute as an ordinary command. The BASIC loader selects the appropriate entry. Preserve the matching family when maintaining the language environment.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
BASIC.OVERLAY1 R 22,482 26,624 1981-12-11 07:55
BASIC.OVERLAY2 R 7,757 9,216 1981-12-11 07:56
BASIC.OVERLAY3 R 9,438 11,264 1982-05-07 17:30
BASIC.OVERLAY4 R 28,458 32,768 1983-04-11 14:45

Device drivers: the SISTEMA.DIS family

DIS numbers identify driver components through the physical device definitions. They do not denote a sequence of driver revisions. ASOCIAR selects devices through those definitions; the appropriate driver supplies the input/output routines. Similar terminal drivers can have the same length while serving different terminal ports. Equal lengths do not make them duplicate files: their port and configuration bytes differ.

SISTEMA.DIS1 and SISTEMA.DIS2 begin with four JP entry slots at offsets 0x00, 0x03, 0x06 and 0x09. The terminal, printer and tape drivers listed below have five opening slots through 0x0C. These are driver-specific entry tables; they must not be interpreted as a command program's version header.

Terminal classes: SISTEMA.CLASE1 and SISTEMA.CLASE2

A terminal class translates logical display operations into terminal-specific control sequences. CLASE1 uses ESC G coordinate sequences; CLASE2 uses ANSI-like cursor sequences. The files are executable translation routines, not editable lists of terminal settings. Select the class appropriate to the connected terminal through ASOCIAR, as described in Chapter 12. Repeatedly replacing a class can consume partition memory.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SISTEMA.CLASE1 R 259 1,024 1984-05-31 19:10
SISTEMA.CLASE2 R 328 1,024 1984-12-04 23:59

BASIC extension modules

A BASICUSR file supplies a relocatable routine callable through BASIC's USR interface. OPTION USR selects a module; the selected routine determines the arguments and result. Sharing this file type does not make two routines interchangeable.

GETPRIV returns the current privilege, GETID returns the account number, and GETUSER fills an eight-byte string with the account name. Their elementary calls follow the table. Other modules have partly understood implementation roles, listed below, while their general argument and side-effect contracts remain incomplete. They are BASIC-loaded extension routines, not automatically members of the executive or interchangeable USR interfaces.

Module Established operation and limits
CAMPAR Calls scheduling/yield service 0x4F, then returns. Its complete image is three bytes, CF 4F C9; it occupies one stored sector and has no relocation sector.
PUTQUET Preserves HL, passes HL = 0 to callback-setting service 0x39, restores HL and returns. Its name does not establish a queue operation.
CLAVOUT Temporarily selects public-account lookup for CLAVE.CUENTA, restores the caller's account byte and reads the first sector into caller storage. If lookup fails, it zeroes the caller's first byte. The general calling contract remains incomplete.
LINPAN Parses input text and changes device-descriptor fields; it is not a read-only line-count query.
ESPERA Installs a callback, changes port 0x91 state, waits through service 0x6B and removes the callback. Its general input and device requirements are unspecified.
OCONV, OCONV2 and KEYSET Extension routines used by application-support facilities; their complete transformation grammars are unspecified.

NOMVOL, TAMDIS and DIREC also remain without a complete documented calling contract. The elementary GETPRIV, GETID and GETUSER examples below should not be adapted to another module by changing only the module name.

GETPRIV.BASICUSR R 6 1,024 1981-06-01 12:37
GETID.BASICUSR R 6 1,024 1981-06-17 21:20
GETUSER.BASICUSR R 25 1,024 1981-04-16 12:38
CAMPAR.BASICUSR R 3 1,024 1984-11-16 14:26
PUTQUET.BASICUSR R 8 1,024 1983-05-25 14:22
NOMVOL.BASICUSR R 18 1,024 1983-04-27 10:58
TAMDIS.BASICUSR R 41 1,024 1983-06-01 09:25

CLAVIN, CLAVOUT and CLAVE.CUENTA

CLAVIN.COMANDO is the installed command-side companion of CLAVOUT.BASICUSR. Its header reads CLAVIN ver 1.0 01JUN83; its load image is 6,146 bytes with 686 relocation records, and its prologue requires privilege 5. The apparent main body has argument-writing and no-argument-reading paths associated with CLAVE.CUENTA. The pair appears to exchange a counted string or value, whose application meaning remains unspecified.

The command fails during startup with CR = 254, before those apparent main-body operations. Its stored code and relocation records are inconsistent: relocation at file offset 0x010D modifies bytes that include an instruction opcode, and another entry at 0x0191 leads to an unintended stack-pointer value on the observed startup path. The fault's origin and an authentic repair remain unknown. Creating CLAVE.CUENTA does not fix this failure. See CLAVIN for the invocation and its limits.

CLAVE.CUENTA is absent from the documented installation and is separate from the account database SISTEMA.CUENTA. Use CUENTA for account and password administration; neither helper provides an established alternative.

Read the current privilege

At the BASIC prompt, select the routine:

OPTION USR "GETPRIV"

OPTION USR loads the named extension for subsequent calls. Now call it and print its numeric result:

PRINT USR(0,0)

The number displayed is the current privilege. Control returns to BASIC.

Read the account number

Select the account-number routine:

OPTION USR "GETID"

This replaces the previously selected extension. Call the new routine:

PRINT USR(0,0)

The result is the current account number, which is distinct from the execution partition number.

Read the account name

Select the string-returning routine:

OPTION USR "GETUSER"

Provide an eight-character workspace and print the returned name:

PRINT USR$(0,SPACE$(8))

SPACE$(8) supplies the eight bytes that GETUSER fills. USR$ retrieves a string result; USR in the preceding examples retrieves a number.

SORT and CONSULTA overlays

SORT.OVERLAY1 and SORT.OVERLAY2 supply different parts of the sorting utility. Both begin with the literal SORTCB and a 0xF0-byte (240-byte) shared control/data area. The executable JP entry slots follow at offsets 0xF0, 0xF3 and 0xF6. SORT copies the control area into the selected overlay before calling it and copies it back afterward. OVERLAY2 appears to perform merge/output work; the complete sorting algorithm and phase division remain unspecified.

CONSULTA.COMANDO requests CONSULTA.OVERLAY1, OVERLAY2 and OVERLAY3; OVERLAY3 in turn requests OVERLAY4. These dependencies do not imply that every query loads all four components. A complete functional description of each overlay remains unavailable. In both families, the numbers distinguish code components selected by the utility, rather than independent commands or successive releases.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SORT.OVERLAY1 R 4,183 6,144 1982-02-19 10:17
SORT.OVERLAY2 R 4,982 7,168 1982-02-18 09:07
CONSULTA.OVERLAY1 R 21,320 24,576 1983-02-07 13:34
CONSULTA.OVERLAY2 R 19,547 22,528 1983-01-27 16:15
CONSULTA.OVERLAY3 R 20,252 23,552 1982-09-24 00:50
CONSULTA.OVERLAY4 R 15,811 18,432 1983-02-07 13:37

CONSULTA.TRAB1–CONSULTA.TRAB4

These are direct work files used by CONSULTA for temporary structured state. Unlike the CONSULTA overlays, they hold data rather than code. Each listed file provides 400 records of 256 bytes, or 102,400 bytes of capacity. Their complete record layout and selection rule are unspecified; the numeric suffix alone is insufficient to promise the same partition mapping as the EXEC work files. CONSULTA maintains their contents, which cannot be replaced with text from EDITAR. Stored work records may remain after a query; their presence alone does not establish current activity.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
CONSULTA.TRAB1 D 102,400 102,400 1985-01-01 09:52

Spooler components and generated reports

SPOOLER.OVERLAY1 supplies executable spooler code. SISTEMA.SPOOLCOL is the direct queue file: its 64 records of 16 bytes describe reports, submitting partitions, dates, flags, queues and page counts. Its 1,024-byte capacity is queue metadata, not the combined capacity of the reports.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
SPOOLER.OVERLAY1 R 1,617 3,072 1984-04-27 12:44
SISTEMA.SPOOLCOL D 1,024 1,024 1981-12-11 07:59

Report contents are stored separately in generated files named SISTEMA.SP$ followed by a report number. Their lengths and dates change with submitted work. The queue links each report to its state and page count. Use the spooler to inspect the current queue:

SPOOLER LIST

This displays queue information. LIST stops at the queue terminator; nonzero bytes beyond that terminator need not represent active reports. Do not delete report backing files while queue entries still refer to them. Chapter 29 explains report encoding and restart rules.

Large-lettering support data

MAESTRO.TITULO is a direct-data file referenced by TITULO.COMANDO, the utility for printing large lettering. Its compact records appear to provide character-pattern data. The full record interpretation and whether this particular file was distributed unchanged by the manufacturer remain unspecified. Its relationship to the utility supports treating it as probable utility support rather than classifying it from the MAESTRO name alone.

The file provides 100 records of 50 bytes, giving 5,000 bytes of capacity. Its sector-rounded stored length and allocation are both 5,120 bytes. It is data read by a utility, not a program to invoke directly. The TITULO reference explains the command and gives a console example.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
MAESTRO.TITULO D 5,000 5,120 1983-02-08 00:06

Mailbox and duplication workspace

SISTEMA.BUZON

The sequential mailbox contains recipient/sender headers and message bodies. Its message header occupies 33 bytes; Appendix E documents the fields. This is a generated service file, so a fixed program size or catalogue date would be misleading. It need not exist before stored messaging is used.

To retrieve messages for the current account, enter:

BUZON

Retrieval removes the messages it displays. This operation is not a repeatable read-only view of the mailbox.

SISTEMA.DUPLICAR

This direct workspace supports floppy duplication. The preparation procedure allocates 4,000 records of 256 bytes, a capacity of 1,024,000 bytes. That is a requested workspace size, not an executable program length. Its catalogue date depends on its creation; no fixed date is specified. Chapter 11 gives the preparation and duplication steps. A workspace alone does not establish that a compatible floppy device is ready.

SISTEMA.BUZON, SISTEMA.DUPLICAR and generated spool report files are conditional service files; they should not be assigned a fixed installed size merely because a program can use them. CONECTAR also refers to SISTEMA.HISTORIA, whose full record contract and availability are unspecified. CLAVE.CUENTA is absent; its relationship to CLAVIN and CLAVOUT is described with the BASIC extension modules above.

Embedded component identification

These literal strings identify individual components. The offset is measured from the beginning of the file before relocation. It is not a universal header location. A date within a marker is reproduced as written and is separate from the catalogue date in the size tables.

Component File offset Literal identification text
SISTEMA.CSI 0x0003 CSI ver 5.5 Copyright by Tim Williams
SISTEMA.EXECLANG 0x0003 EXEC ver 5.5 Copyright by T.S.Williams
BASIC.COMANDO 0x0003 BASIC VER 1.0 11DIC81
RUN.COMANDO 0x0003 RUN VER 1.0 11DIC81
SORT.COMANDO 0x0003 SORT ver 1.0A 18FEB82
SORT.OVERLAY1 0x00FC SORTLTAG Ver 2.0 08SEP81
SORT.OVERLAY2 0x00FC SORTMTAG Ver 1.0 02APR81
CONSULTA.COMANDO 0x0003 ACCESS ver 1.0D 01OCT82 Copyright de SECOINSA-D.TECNICA
CONSULTA.OVERLAY1 0x0003 CONSULTA ver 1.0 01OCT83 Cpyright de SECOINSA-D.TECNICA
CONSULTA.OVERLAY2 0x0003 CONSULTA ver 1.0 01OCT82 Cpyright de SECOINSA-D.TECNICA
CONSULTA.OVERLAY3 0x0003 CONSULTA ver 1.0 01OCT82 Cpyright de SECOINSA-D.TECNICA
CONSULTA.OVERLAY4 0x0003 CONSULTA ver 1.0 01OCT82 Cpyright de SECOINSA-D.TECNICA

CONSULTA.OVERLAY1 contains the date 01OCT83 even though its catalogue timestamp is 1983-02-07 13:34. OVERLAY3 has a catalogue date of 1982-09-24, preceding its embedded 01OCT82 date. These disagreements are retained; neither field establishes a reliable compilation chronology. ACCESS and Cpyright are literal spellings in the components. Component versions such as CSI 5.5 and BASIC 1.0 are distinct from operating-system Version 6.2.

User-created source and program files

HELLO.BASICOBJ and HELLO.BASICCOM are created by the example in Chapter 15. BASICOBJ holds saved source for the BASIC editor; BASICCOM holds compiled input for the runtime. NOTE.TEXT and HELLO.EXEC are likewise teaching files. They are not bundled operating-system components.

Optional support procedures and compiled tools

The following files extend the BASIC programming and business-file environment. An EXEC procedure supplies prompts, input queues and calls to programs; a BASICCOM file contains compiled instructions consumed by RUN and the BASIC runtime. Identical stems therefore need not denote duplicate programs. For example, ACTUCAP.EXEC prepares arguments for ACTUCAP.BASICCOM. A direct RUN ACTUCAP omits that wrapper's preparation.

These components are optional support software, not resident executive facilities. Their presence alone does not establish that every required dictionary, screen definition, printer or companion program is available. The descriptions below distinguish the callable wrapper from the compiled component and identify modifying operations. Provenance of every optional component as an original manufacturer product has not been established.

Capture definitions and source generation

A capture definition describes the fields and behavior of a data-entry program. The support tools store those definitions in LOCAL.MACROS, maintain vocabulary in CONTROL.VOC, and can generate BASIC source. They do not constitute a general Z80 assembler or linker.

ACTUCAP.EXEC accepts an optional program or screen name, queues it and calls ACTUCAP.BASICCOM. Without the name the compiled program requests it. Its help describes creating or updating the screen, file and modification criteria in LOCAL.MACROS before source expansion. ACTUCAP2.BASICCOM supplies special-process definition support; the complete transition to that program is unspecified. ACTUCAP.PANTALLA and ACTUCAP2.PANTALLA hold supporting screen data.

Begin by requesting the wrapper's help:

EXEC ACTUCAP AYUDA

This selects its explanatory branch instead of opening the definition-maintenance dialogue. The operational form is illustrated by:

EXEC ACTUCAP ENTRY

ENTRY is a chosen definition name, not a supplied application. This form can modify LOCAL.MACROS and requires initialized support files. The field definitions must include every key and non-key field the generated program is to access. The complete interactive dialogue is not specified here.

EXPAND.EXEC reads an existing definition and drives EXPAND.BASICCOM, followed by BASIC source loading, indentation and saving. Optional compilation creates BASICCOM output. Its advertised options are PANT, COMPILAR, NOCOM, DISCO=df, NOB and BORRAR. Selected branches delete intermediate BASIC or BASICOBJ files, so expansion is a source-generating maintenance operation, not a read-only preview.

To read the available invocation information first, enter:

EXEC EXPAND AYUDA

A source-generation syntax illustration is:

EXEC EXPAND ENTRY

It requires a compatible ENTRY capture definition, the macro files and model, and the BASIC components. It is not a complete example that creates those definitions from scratch. EXPAND.MACMODEL supplies BASIC text with placeholders such as {PNAME}, {READ}, {WRITE}, {DELETE} and {SPECPROC}. USOIMPR.BASICMAC supplies printer-related BASIC source that opens IMPR1 and reconstructs screen lines for output. Neither is an executable command or a relocatable object library.

TESTCAP.EXEC queues an optional screen name and calls TESTCAP.BASICCOM. Its help describes testing a screen with one key: the first data field is the key and the final datum is an end-of-screen label. It states that this tool does not maintain files and that the key cannot be changed in modification mode. To read those restrictions, enter:

EXEC TESTCAP AYUDA

IMPCAP.EXEC queues a required screen name and calls IMPCAP.BASICCOM to print its definition data. For a screen named ENTRY and an appropriately associated printer, the syntax is:

EXEC IMPCAP ENTRY

This is a printing request, not screen execution. IMPCAP returns syntax result 8 when the required name is omitted. SCAPTURA.BASICCOM and GENPAN.BASICCOM belong to screen/capture support, but their complete standalone invocation contracts remain unspecified. SCAPTURA includes a named DIREC.BASICUSR call; that dependency does not turn it into a general file-inspection command.

The following EXEC and model entries count CR-inclusive text bytes.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
ACTUCAP.EXEC S 945 1,024 1981-11-25 11:18
EXPAND.EXEC S 2,577 3,072 1982-08-06 00:41
TESTCAP.EXEC S 818 1,024 1981-11-25 09:21
IMPCAP.EXEC S 485 1,024 1981-11-24 16:13
EXPAND.MACMODEL S 15,898 16,384 1984-06-25 04:59

Dictionary creation and vocabulary maintenance

GENFICH.EXEC creates the working files used by capture and query support. It accepts an optional logical disk letter, defaulting to S. First request its help:

EXEC GENFICH AYUDA

The creation form is:

EXEC GENFICH S

Use this only when preparing a new support environment with unused working filenames. It creates the indexed files below, copies seed vocabulary through a temporary CONTROL.ANTVOC file, calls COPIVOC.BASICCOM and deletes that temporary file. Existing contents and interrupted creation require separate review; this is not an automatic repair procedure.

Working file Record bytes Initial record capacity Key bytes
LOCAL.MACROS 512 100 8
PROMPT.MACROS 34 200 11
CONTROL.VOC 512 100 30

MAESTRO.VOC supplies the seed vocabulary. Its indexed allocation has room for eleven 512-byte data fields; six are active in the component described here. Each slot also contains the 30-byte key and a two-byte link, making 544 bytes per slot. Index storage and block allocation account for further space. The contents figure reports only the six active data fields.

GENVOC.EXEC creates vocabulary entries from a capture screen. It accepts a screen name and optional target filename; omitting the latter uses the screen name. The NOC option suppresses per-datum questions. Its help restricts creation to files with a single access key. For an existing ENTRY screen, the syntax illustration is:

EXEC GENVOC ENTRY

This adds definitions to CONTROL.VOC and requires the screen data and dictionary to exist. It does not create an arbitrary application file. To inspect the procedure's full introductory text, use its AYUDA argument.

VOC.EXEC manages relationships between named attributes and stored data. With no function it starts VOC.BASICCOM for maintenance. LIST or PANT lists the dictionary on the screen, IMPR prints it, and BORRAR removes attribute groups through MANTVOC.BASICCOM. FICHERO optionally restricts those operations to a named file. A non-deleting example is:

EXEC VOC LIST

This invokes CONSULTA with the prepared dictionary-listing query explained in Chapter 35. It requires a usable CONTROL.VOC and the partition's CONSULTA work file. VOC.PANTALLA supports the maintenance interface.

COPIVOC.EXEC enlarges or rebuilds a dictionary. It asks for old and new disk associations and a new record capacity, removes deletion protection, renames the old dictionary to CONTROL.ANTVOC, creates a new CONTROL.VOC, runs COPIVOC.BASICCOM and removes the temporary old file on success. Its error branch attempts restoration. These steps change names, protection and data; they do not make every interrupted migration automatically recoverable. Review the existing dictionary and retain a separate backup before using this interactive procedure.

The following S procedure entries count text bytes; MAESTRO.VOC counts active indexed data fields.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
GENFICH.EXEC S 943 1,024 1982-07-09 15:52
GENVOC.EXEC S 1,327 2,048 1981-11-25 12:04
VOC.EXEC S 2,060 3,072 1982-02-18 09:22
COPIVOC.EXEC S 937 1,024 1983-02-04 08:47
MAESTRO.VOC I 3,072 7,168 1983-01-01 00:02

The following entries count stored binary payload including padding. COPIVOC.BASICCOM is also called by GENFICH; MANTVOC.BASICCOM implements the vocabulary-removal path. They should not be invoked as substitutes for their wrappers without preparing the expected state.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
GENVOC.BASICCOM S 1,524 2,048 1983-03-02 01:05
VOC.BASICCOM S 4,572 5,120 1982-02-18 10:20

COPIVOC.BASICCOM S 254 1,024 1981-12-01 16:26
VOC.PANTALLA S 1,524 2,048 1982-02-18 09:51

Report generation and source insertion

INFORME.EXEC coordinates report-program generation. It obtains the current partition through GETPART.BASICCOM, uses the completion result to select that partition's numbered work file (for example, CONSULTA.TRAB1 for partition 1), empties that work file, queues supplied arguments and starts INFORME1.BASICCOM. INFORME1–INFORME5 are members of the report-support family, not five independent versions of one command. A complete assignment of later generation stages to members 2–5 remains unspecified.

Request the wrapper's help before creating a report definition:

EXEC INFORME AYUDA

This takes the help branch before GETPART and work-file clearing. The documented generation form takes a program name and an optional A or C for an open or closed report. Its syntax is illustrated by:

EXEC INFORME REPORT A

This can replace the partition's report work state and generates BASIC source from operator-supplied definitions. It needs the corresponding numbered CONSULTA work file, BASIC and the report components. It is not a ready-made report over arbitrary files. INFORME1 uses the OCONV2 and KEYSET BASICUSR modules. GETPART is a compiled helper whose result is a partition selector; it is not an instruction to create a partition.

INSER.EXEC queues supplied arguments, runs INSER1.BASICCOM and asks whether to perform another insertion. INSER1.EXEC is a separate command procedure: it drives BASIC and EDITAR to copy a range of source statements, renumber them and save BASICOBJ output on S. The distinction between INSER1.EXEC and INSER1.BASICCOM matters when choosing an entry path.

INSER1.EXEC takes five fields: destination program, insertion point, source program, first source statement and statement count. An illustrative form is:

EXEC INSER1 TARGET 0 SOURCE 100 5

Here zero requests insertion at the beginning of TARGET. SOURCE and TARGET must already be compatible saved BASIC/BASICOBJ programs; 100 identifies the first source statement and 5 the number to insert. This operation rewrites saved source and uses temporary INSER$ files. When source and destination are the same, it removes the original statements after moving them to avoid duplication. Save separate copies before using it; default editing positions, malformed ranges and recovery after interruption are not fully specified.

The three procedure entries below count text bytes.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
INFORME.EXEC S 1,133 2,048 1983-10-01 00:34
INSER.EXEC S 186 1,024 1982-08-02 18:02
INSER1.EXEC S 2,042 3,072 1982-08-02 15:03

The compiled entries below count stored binary payload including padding. Use the report or insertion wrapper for their supplied setup.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
GETPART.BASICCOM S 254 1,024 1983-10-17 16:30
INFORME1.BASICCOM S 4,826 5,120 1983-10-01 00:39
INFORME2.BASICCOM S 6,096 6,144 1983-05-19 13:34
INFORME3.BASICCOM S 6,350 7,168 1983-05-19 13:35
INFORME4.BASICCOM S 7,112 7,168 1983-05-19 13:54
INFORME5.BASICCOM S 5,334 6,144 1983-09-23 01:33
INSER1.BASICCOM S 1,524 2,048 1982-12-24 12:54

System setup, copying and version migration

CIPL.EXEC configures accounts, partitions, associations, printer/spooler state and work files. It is administrative setup software, not a prerequisite command for every login. It invokes a further procedure named CIPL1 whose implementation is unavailable, so its complete execution cannot be prescribed as a normal startup procedure.

The following is a syntax illustration only:

EXEC CIPL ALP4 A IP

ALP4 selects the ALP terminal branch; it does not mean exactly four users. That branch begins with partition 2 and terminal PAN2. NP1 through NP7 reduce the activation limit; the default limit is eight. The A argument requests a public association of logical disk A with physical definition 4, which must already describe appropriate available storage. It does not create or format a volume. IP selects the parallel-printer device in place of the default IMP selection. Use it only with the required hardware and definitions. The procedure allocates or adjusts partitions and initializes spooler/work state, so it is not a harmless status query. Account and partition administration are taught independently in Chapters 10 and 12.

COPSIS.EXEC and COPSIS2.EXEC copy system components. They are alternative procedures, not numbered partitions. COPSIS asks for source and destination and can initialize destination media. COPSIS2 contains an explicit component list that includes optional targets not available in every installation. Neither should replace the planned backup and restoration procedures of Chapter 13 without checking its source, destination and required components.

CAMBVERS.EXEC implements a 6.1/6.1A-to-6.2 migration path. It copies components and invokes O8PRECOM to change disk-precompensation state. It is not required for a system already running 6.2. O8PRECOM performs controller writes; its complete invocation and recovery contract remains unspecified. The command entry therefore provides an inspection example rather than a migration command to try.

These procedure entries count CR-inclusive text bytes.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
CIPL.EXEC S 1,548 2,048 1984-01-01 04:41
COPSIS.EXEC S 6,847 7,168 1985-01-18 12:04
COPSIS2.EXEC S 6,912 7,168 1984-01-02 01:21
CAMBVERS.EXEC S 1,729 2,048 1986-01-01 19:03

BASIC source routines and other support files

RUTINA1.BASIC through RUTINA4.BASIC are source libraries with different purposes. They are neither separate CSI commands nor revision numbers of one routine. They provide material to incorporate into a BASIC program with the required variables, file state and hardware assumptions.

Source file Purpose and prerequisites
RUTINA1.BASIC FNDICO% binary search in a direct file; requires the routine's record/key conventions.
RUTINA2.BASIC FNVFEC$ due-date calculation using a commercial year; do not assume civil-calendar arithmetic.
RUTINA3.BASIC Load and display labels from PANT definitions; uses the DIREC file-information interface.
RUTINA4.BASIC Rectangle drawing described for an 8500 screen; applicability to another terminal is not established.

To inspect one source library before incorporating it, enter:

LISTAR RUTINA1.BASIC:S

This displays source text and its explanatory comments; it does not execute FNDICO%. Read the routine's required data before copying it into another program. The source insertion tools above can modify programs, so their presence does not remove the need to understand the copied routine.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
RUTINA1.BASIC S 18,697 19,456 1984-05-18 13:19
RUTINA2.BASIC S 17,655 18,432 1984-05-16 14:05
RUTINA3.BASIC S 16,949 17,408 1984-11-12 08:34
RUTINA4.BASIC S 4,392 5,120 1984-10-05 12:23

The remaining compiled files require individual interpretation. LONCLA.BASICCOM prompts for a filename and contains a key-length result message, but the complete input/error contract is unspecified. It is distinct from CREAR's LONCLA option. DISCO.BASICCOM contains a VERFORM A command string; this establishes a connection with media checking, not a general disk-management interface. RESUME.BASICCOM has no adequately specified standalone purpose and is distinct from BASIC's RESUME statement.

TRAFIC.BASICCOM presents an input/auxiliary/output-file transformation dialogue and can create an EXEC control file. Its prompts describe selecting keys, fields and output format. They do not establish a complete safe transformation recipe. In particular, output selection can write files, and an optional generated control procedure can replay those choices later. No bare RUN example is prescribed for these incompletely specified tools.

These entries count stored binary payload including padding.

Component Format Contents (bytes) Allocated (bytes) Catalogue date and time
DISCO.BASICCOM S 254 1,024 1985-01-04 21:39
LONCLA.BASICCOM S 508 1,024 1984-10-16 14:42
RESUME.BASICCOM S 254 1,024 1983-06-16 08:42
TRAFIC.BASICCOM S 5,842 6,144 1983-10-17 13:20

MAESTRO.POLIZA is a retained direct-format template whose consumer and full purpose remain unspecified; its name alone does not identify a supported application. Its Contents count is fixed-record capacity rather than the number of occupied records. MAESTRO.TITULO is described separately above and is not another missing member of this group.
