MAESTRO.POLIZA D 612 1,024 1982-11-16 15:33

Appendix D. BASIC commands, statements and functions

Use this reference after the first-program example in Chapter 15. Editor commands are entered at the BASIC dash prompt. Numbered statements become stored program lines. Functions produce values inside expressions, often displayed with PRINT. None of these examples is a CSI command unless explicitly labelled as such.

Every documented form has its own elementary example below. A fragment that names a source line, array or file channel requires that object to exist. It is not a complete program by itself. Chapters 15–17 supply complete workflows and programs.

Interface limits. The core tutorials give defined working behavior. Several additional forms have only an incomplete contract. Their entries distinguish their documented argument shape from a probable role or an unknown effect. A syntax illustration is not a promise of the displayed result, device operation or complete editor dialogue. In particular, unfamiliar string, date and device functions must not be assigned another BASIC dialect's semantics.

Angle brackets in syntax identify placeholders. The example replaces them where the argument domain is known. A remaining device placeholder explicitly means that no runnable device example is specified. Source help has unmatched brackets in some forms; those are normalized only where optional punctuation is unambiguous.

Editor and debugger commands

AUTO

Generate source line numbers automatically.

Syntax: AUTO [<start>[,<incr>]]

Example

AUTO 10,10

10 is the first number and 10 the increment. Enter source at the offered numbers. The complete cancellation dialogue is unspecified.

BOTTOM

Select the bottom of the source-editing area.

Syntax: BOTTOM

Example

BOTTOM

This is an editor positioning command, not program execution. The exact screen redraw and subsequent editing interaction are unspecified.

BREAK AT

Set a breakpoint associated with a source line.

Syntax: BREAK [AT <line> [AFTER <count>]]

Example

BREAK AT 20

This requests a stop at line 20 of the loaded program. AFTER adds a count condition; the complete counting and continuation rules are unspecified.

BREAK ON change

Request a variable-related breakpoint.

Syntax: BREAK ON <var> [CHANGE] [AFTER <count>]

Example

BREAK ON A CHANGE

This form names A and requests change monitoring. Use a disposable program; exact watchpoint timing and initial comparison rules are unspecified.

BREAK ON comparison

Request a breakpoint when a variable meets a comparison.

Syntax: BREAK [ON <var> <relat> <value>]

Example

BREAK ON A = 7

The form compares A with 7. The full breakpoint lifecycle is unspecified; it is not a CSI conditional command.

CHANGE

Replace text in source.

Syntax: CHANGE [/<from>/<to>[/ <range>]]

Example

CHANGE /HELLO/WELCOME/

The slash-delimited fields identify old and new text. Save a copy first; the default range and interactive confirmation rules are unspecified.

CONTINUE

Continue a suspended BASIC execution.

Syntax: CONTINUE

Example

CONTINUE

This belongs after a resumable BASIC stop. It does not resume a command terminated by FIN or Escape-Q. Complete resumability rules are unspecified.

DELETE source

Remove source lines.

Syntax: DELETE [<range>]

Example

DELETE 20

This selects source line 20 for deletion. Save the source first. It is distinct from DELETE #channel,key, which affects file records.

INDENT

Control the source indentation setting.

Syntax: INDENT [<value>]

Example

INDENT 2

This supplies an indentation value of 2. The exact application to existing lines versus later entry is unspecified.

LENGTH

Request the BASIC source-length facility.

Syntax: LENGTH

Example

LENGTH

The command has no argument in the documented form. The output units and accounting rules are unspecified; do not equate them with allocated disk blocks.

LIST

Display source lines.

Syntax: LIST [<range>]

Example

LIST

This displays the program in memory. It does not execute it or write it to disk. LIST 10 selects an individual line in the documented range form.

LOAD

Read saved BASIC source into the editor.

Syntax: LOAD <name>[.<type>][:<drive>]

Example

LOAD HELLO:S

This selects HELLO.BASICOBJ on S. Save current work before loading another program.

LOCATE

Search source text.

Syntax: LOCATE [/string[/<range>]]

Example

LOCATE /HELLO/

This supplies HELLO as the search string. Default range, cursor placement and repeat-search behavior are unspecified.

LPnLIST

Direct a source listing to printer n.

Syntax: LPnLIST

Example

LP1LIST

This selects printer 1. The logical printer must be associated with ready hardware; command acceptance does not prove printed output.

LPnXREF

Direct a cross-reference listing to printer n.

Syntax: LPnXREF

Example

LP1XREF

This requests cross-reference output on printer 1. The exact report layout is unspecified.

MODIFY

Enter source modification for a selected range.

Syntax: MODIFY [<range>]

Example

MODIFY 10

This selects line 10. The complete editing keystrokes and completion dialogue are unspecified; re-entering a numbered line is the basic replacement method taught in Chapter 15.

NAME

Set or query the program name used by the editor.

Syntax: NAME [<name>[.<type>][:<drive>]]

Example

NAME HELLO:S

This supplies a name and drive. It is not a substitute for SAVE; persistence and default-name interactions beyond this form are unspecified.

NEW

Begin a new source program.

Syntax: NEW

Example

NEW

Save work before using NEW because it replaces the current editing context. It does not create an on-disk BASICOBJ until you save.

QUIT command

Leave the BASIC environment.

Syntax: QUIT [<code>|<CSI command>]

Example

QUIT

This returns to CSI. Optional result-code and CSI-command forms are documented, but their complete interaction with open files is unspecified. Close files explicitly.

RENUMBER

Renumber source lines.

Syntax: RENUMBER [<first> [<incr> [<start> [<end>]]]]

Example

RENUMBER 10 10

This requests numbering starting at 10 in increments of 10. Back up before using it; handling of every embedded target and optional range is unspecified.

RUN command

Execute BASIC source through the interpreter.

Syntax: RUN [<name>][ <line>]

Example

RUN

This runs the current source. It differs from CSI RUN, which loads BASICCOM. Execution does not save your source.

SAVE

Write saved BASIC source.

Syntax: SAVE [<name>[.<type>][:<drive>]]

Example

SAVE HELLO:S

This creates or updates HELLO.BASICOBJ:S. It saves source rather than producing compiled BASICCOM.

STEP

Request a bounded debugging step.

Syntax: STEP [<count>]

Example

STEP 1

This supplies count 1 to the stepping facility. Exact stepping units and the required suspension state are unspecified.

TOP

Select the top of the source-editing area.

Syntax: TOP

Example

TOP

This is an editor positioning request. It is not the first-line execution command RUN.

TRACE

Enable execution tracing.

Syntax: TRACE [VARS]

Example

TRACE

Tracing concerns execution rather than saving source. VARS requests the documented variable-related variant; exact display and trace granularity are unspecified.

UNBREAK

Remove a breakpoint selection.

Syntax: UNBREAK [AT <line>|ON <var>]

Example

UNBREAK AT 20

This removes the breakpoint associated with line 20. The ON variable form targets a variable watch rather than a line.

UNTRACE

Disable tracing.

Syntax: UNTRACE

Example

UNTRACE

Use this after a tracing session to request ordinary execution display again.

VARS

Display variables.

Syntax: VARS [<var-list>]

Example

VARS A

This selects variable A for inspection. Omitting the list requests the general variable display; exact formatting is unspecified.

XREF

Request source cross-references.

Syntax: XREF

Example

XREF

This is a navigation aid for source identifiers and their uses. It does not compile or change the program by itself.

Line-number display

Display a source line by its number.

Syntax: nnnn (display line)

Example

10

At the BASIC prompt this requests line 10. Distinguish a number alone from 10 PRINT ..., which supplies source text.

Ctrl-Z

Move upward in BASIC source editing.

Syntax: ctl-z (up line)

Example

Press Ctrl-Z

This is a key action, not literal text. The same byte is not a CSI history command.

LF key

Move downward in BASIC source editing.

Syntax: lf-key (down line)

Example

Press the line-feed key

Send LF, character 0x0A. Do not assume that a modern keyboard's Return key sends LF; the usual command terminator is CR.

Return key

Display the current source line in the documented editing mode.

Syntax: ret-key (display line)

Example

Press Return

This sends CR, character 0x0D. Its editor meaning depends on being in BASIC's editing context rather than at CSI.

Program statements

CASE

Introduce a case within structured selection.

Syntax: CASE <expr>

Example

20 CASE 1

This supplies the value 1 for comparison inside a SELECT construct. The full SELECT/CASE matching rules are unspecified; the fragment is not a complete program.

CEND

Terminate a structured selection construct.

Syntax: CEND

Example

40 CEND

CEND belongs with SELECT/CASE. It does not terminate the whole BASIC program like END.

CHAIN

Request transfer to another BASIC program.

Syntax: CHAIN <str-expr>

Example

10 CHAIN "NEXT:S"

The string names the next program. Exact source/compiled selection and variable/option inheritance are unspecified; do not assume another BASIC dialect's CHAIN contract.

CLEAR

Clear selected variable state.

Syntax: CLEAR [<var-list>]

Example

10 CLEAR A

This selects A in the documented variable-list form. Exact treatment of arrays, strings and omitted lists is unspecified.

CLOSE

Close an open file channel.

Syntax: CLOSE #<channel>

Example

CLOSE #1

Use this after finishing channel 1. It completes output handling and releases the open association; it does not mean logout.

COMMON

Declare variables for interprogram sharing.

Syntax: COMMON <var-list>

Example

10 COMMON A,S$

The declaration names numeric A and string S$. Exact CHAIN/LINK lifetime and layout rules are unspecified, so this is a declaration example only.

CSI

Request a command-processor operation from BASIC.

Syntax: CSI <str-expr>

Example

10 CSI "ESTADO QUIEN"

The string supplies a CSI command. Full return, variable retention and open-channel behavior are unspecified; use the direct CSI example for routine identity checks.

DATA

Store literal values for later READ statements.

Syntax: DATA <lit-list>

Example

10 DATA 11,2.5,"ABC",-4

READ consumes these values in order. DATA is not a file-writing operation.

DEF FN

Define a user function.

Syntax: DEF FN<name>[(<arguments>)][=<expression>]

Example

10 DEF FNT(X)=X+1

The function takes X and returns X+1. PRINT FNT(2) uses it after the definition is available. FNEND closes the separate multiline form.

DELETE record

Delete a keyed record through an open channel.

Syntax: DELETE #<channel>,<key>

Example

DELETE #1,"MMMM"

This removes the active reference for key MMMM from a compatible opened indexed/keyed file. It does not delete source line MMMM or the entire file.

DIM

Declare an array.

Syntax: DIM <dim-var-list>

Example

DIM ARR(2)

With OPTION BASE 0, ARR has indices 0–2; with BASE 1, indices 1–2. Set the base before declaring the array.

ELSE

Introduce the alternative action of a conditional.

Syntax: ELSE [<statement>|<line-ref>]

Example

20 IF A=7 THEN PRINT "YES" ELSE PRINT "NO"

ELSE selects PRINT "NO" when the condition fails. It belongs to the IF statement; it is not an independent unconditional action.

END

Finish program execution.

Syntax: END

Example

20 END

Control returns from the program to its execution environment. It does not save source or disconnect the account.

FNEND

End a multiline function definition.

Syntax: FNEND

Example

100 FNEND

This is the terminator within that definition form. The complete multiline return-value contract is unspecified; use the elementary DEF FNT(X)=X+1 form first.

FOR

Start a counted or value-list loop.

Syntax: FOR <var>=[<expr-list>|<num-expr> TO <num-expr>[ STEP <num-expr>]]

Example

10 FOR I%=1 TO 3

NEXT closes the loop. The range uses an implicit step of 1; a list form such as FOR I%=7,9,11 supplies successive values directly.

GET

Read through the language's device-access form.

Syntax: GET <device> <num-expr>,<var-list>

Example

GET <device> 1,A

The device placeholder must be replaced by a supported designator. The valid device vocabulary and transfer contract are unspecified, so this is syntax only, not a runnable hardware-access example.

GOSUB

Call a subroutine at a source line.

Syntax: GOSUB <line-ref>

Example

10 GOSUB 100

Line 100 must exist. RETURN in the subroutine resumes after this GOSUB; see the complete program in Chapter 16.

GOTO

Transfer execution to a source line.

Syntax: GOTO <line-ref>

Example

10 GOTO 100

Execution continues at line 100 without a saved subroutine return. The target must exist.

IF

Choose an action based on a condition.

Syntax: IF <num-expr>[ THEN <statement>[ ELSE <statement>]]

Example

20 IF A=7 THEN PRINT "SEVEN"

The PRINT runs only when A equals 7. ELSE can add an alternative.

IFEND

End a block IF construct.

Syntax: IFEND

Example

50 IFEND

Use it after the block's THEN and optional ELSE sections. It is distinct from END, which finishes the program.

INPUT

Read values into variables.

Syntax: INPUT [<str-expr>,|#<channel>[,<key>]:]<var-list>

Example

10 INPUT "VALUE",A

This is the documented prompted-input form: display the prompt and request a value for A. File INPUT instead uses #channel and a colon. Complete conversion/retry rules are unspecified.

LET

Assign a value.

Syntax: [LET] <var>[ [<num-expr>:<num-expr>] ]=<expr>

Example

10 LET A=7

LET is optional: A=7 is also assignment. It stores the value in A rather than comparing A with 7 in a conditional.

LINK

Request a program link using a string specification.

Syntax: LINK <str-expr>

Example

10 LINK "NEXT:S"

The form supplies a program name. Its return and state-sharing contract is unspecified; it must not be confused with a native object-file linker.

LINPUT

Read line-oriented text into a variable.

Syntax: LINPUT [[<str-expr>,][USING <str-expr>]|#<channel>[,<key>]:]<var>

Example

10 LINPUT "NAME",A$

This supplies a prompt and string destination in the documented form. Exact delimiter and editing rules are unspecified; INPUT and LINPUT need not parse text identically.

MAT copy

Assign one array through a matrix statement.

Syntax: MAT <var>=<var>

Example

20 MAT A=B

Declare compatible arrays first. The form identifies B as source and A as destination; dimension matching and conversion rules are unspecified.

MAT fill

Assign a scalar expression through a matrix statement.

Syntax: MAT <var>=(<expr>)

Example

20 MAT A=(0)

The parentheses distinguish this expression form. Array bounds and exact fill semantics are incompletely specified.

MAT INPUT

Request array input.

Syntax: MAT INPUT [#<channel>[,<key>]:] <var>

Example

20 MAT INPUT A

A must be a declared array. The complete element order, prompts and error behavior are unspecified.

MAT PRINT

Request array output.

Syntax: MAT PRINT [#<channel>[,<key>]:] <var-list>

Example

20 MAT PRINT A

The statement selects A for printing. Exact element layout and separators are unspecified.

MAT READ

Request array values from the READ source.

Syntax: MAT READ [#<channel>[,<key>]:] <var>

Example

20 MAT READ A

Declare the array and supply compatible DATA before using a complete program. Element traversal and conversion details are unspecified.

MAT WRITE

Write array values through a file channel.

Syntax: MAT WRITE #<channel>[,<key>]:<var>

Example

20 MAT WRITE #1:A

Channel 1 must already be open for compatible output. Exact matrix record serialization is unspecified; ordinary WRITE is taught in Chapter 17.

MOUNT

Request media handling through a string specification.

Syntax: MOUNT <str-expr>

Example

10 MOUNT "A"

The string supplies a drive-like specification. The full accepted string grammar and relationship to CSI MONTAR are unspecified, so this is syntax only.

NEXT

Advance and repeat a FOR loop.

Syntax: NEXT [<var>]

Example

30 NEXT I%

This closes the FOR I% loop in Chapter 16. It is not a general jump to the next source line.

ON ERROR

Select an error-handler line.

Syntax: ON ERROR GOTO <line-ref> | 0

Example

10 ON ERROR GOTO 100

Line 100 must contain the handler. ON ERROR GOTO 0 disables the handler path. Nested errors and every RESUME interaction are unspecified.

ON GOTO

Choose a branch from a one-based target list.

Syntax: ON <num-expr> GOTO <line-ref-list>

Example

20 ON 2 GOTO 100,200,300

This selects line 200. Selector zero is out of range; supply all targets in the complete program.

ON GOSUB

Choose a subroutine from a one-based list.

Syntax: ON <num-expr> GOSUB <line-ref-list>

Example

20 ON 2 GOSUB 100,200,300

This calls line 200; RETURN resumes after the entire ON statement. Targets must be valid subroutines.

OPEN

Connect a channel to a file with a mode and organization.

Syntax: OPEN #<channel>: <str-expr>,<mode><method>[<options>]

Example

OPEN #1: "DIRECT.DATOS:S", UPDATE DIRECT

Create the direct file first as in Chapter 17. The colon after #1 is required. OPEN can have immediate side effects, especially sequential replacement.

OPTION

Select a language or runtime option.

Syntax: OPTION [BASE 0|1]|[PROMPT <str-expr>]|[USR <str>]|[CASE <str>]

Example

OPTION BASE 1

This selects one-based array indexing for subsequent declarations. Other options have separate examples below.

OTHERWISE

Introduce the fallback arm of structured selection.

Syntax: OTHERWISE

Example

30 OTHERWISE

This belongs inside SELECT/CASE/CEND. The full matching and fallthrough contract is unspecified; the fragment is not an independent program.

PRINT

Display values or write formatted output to a channel.

Syntax: PRINT [#<channel>[,<key>]:][USING <str-expr>,][<expr-list>]

Example

PRINT "HELLO"

Quotation marks identify literal text. PRINT A instead evaluates variable A. The basic console form does not create a disk file.

PUT

Write through the language's device-access form.

Syntax: PUT <device><num-expr>,<expr-list>

Example

PUT <device> 1,65

The device placeholder and selector require an established device contract. That contract is unspecified here; do not type this as a guessed port write.

QUIT statement

Leave BASIC from program execution, optionally supplying a result.

Syntax: QUIT [<expr>]

Example

20 QUIT 0

This requests successful completion to the surrounding environment. Close file channels beforehand.

RANDOMIZE

Request initialization of random-number generation.

Syntax: RANDOMIZE

Example

10 RANDOMIZE

The form has no argument. Seed selection and reproducibility rules are unspecified; do not rely on a particular sequence.

READ

Consume DATA values or read a selected file record.

Syntax: READ [#<channel>,<key>:]<var-list>

Example

20 READ A,B,S$,I%

With DATA 11,2.5,"ABC",-4, these variables receive the four values in order. The #channel,key form instead reads from a compatible file.

READNEXT

Read the next indexed record and return its key.

Syntax: READNEXT #<channel>,<var>:<var-list>

Example

READNEXT #1,K$:A$

With a compatible indexed channel positioned after key AAAA, this retrieves the next key into K$ and its data into A$. The example file in Chapter 17 next has MMMM/MIDDLE.

REM

Include a source comment.

Syntax: REM <anything>

Example

10 REM PRINT A GREETING

BASIC retains the comment as source text. It is not a PRINT operation.

RESTORE

Reset the DATA reading position.

Syntax: RESTORE [<line-ref>]

Example

RESTORE

The next READ begins again at the first DATA. RESTORE with a line reference selects that DATA line; missing/non-DATA targets are unspecified.

RESUME

Continue from an error-handling context.

Syntax: RESUME [<line-ref>|0]

Example

100 RESUME 20

This documents a specified target line for resumption. Complete retry and nested-error semantics are unspecified; it is not a substitute for fixing the error cause.

RETURN

Return from a GOSUB subroutine.

Syntax: RETURN [<line-ref>]

Example

110 RETURN

Execution resumes after the calling GOSUB. The optional line-reference variant has an incompletely specified contract.

RUN statement

Start execution through the program-level RUN form.

Syntax: RUN [<str-expr>|<line>]

Example

10 RUN "NEXT:S"

The string selects another program. Exact retention and loading rules for this form are unspecified; ordinary interactive RUN is the introductory execution method.

SELECT

Begin structured selection on a value.

Syntax: SELECT <expr>

Example

10 SELECT A

CASE entries supply alternatives and CEND closes the construct. Full coercion and matching rules are unspecified; use IF first for elementary decisions.

SLEEP

Request a delay or suspended interval.

Syntax: SLEEP <num-expr>

Example

10 SLEEP 1

The argument is numeric. Its time unit, rounding and wake conditions are unspecified, so do not interpret 1 as one second without a defined contract.

STOP

Suspend program execution through BASIC.

Syntax: STOP [<expr>]

Example

20 STOP

This differs from END and from CSI PARAR. The complete continuation and open-file behavior is unspecified.

THEN

Introduce the true arm of IF.

Syntax: THEN [<statement>|<line-ref>]

Example

20 IF A=7 THEN PRINT "YES"

THEN is reached when the condition succeeds. It is part of the conditional grammar, not a separate unconditional command.

WAIT

Request the no-argument waiting operation.

Syntax: WAIT

Example

10 WAIT

The wake condition is unspecified. Do not use it as an assumed keyboard prompt or timed delay.

WAIT device

Wait using a device and numeric conditions.

Syntax: WAIT <device><num-expr>,<num-expr>[,<num-expr>]

Example

WAIT <device> 1,1

The supported designator and condition-bit interpretation are unspecified. This is syntax only and must not be used as an invented device-control recipe.

WEND

Close a WHILE loop.

Syntax: WEND

Example

40 WEND

Execution returns to the corresponding WHILE condition. Exact nesting and exceptional-exit rules are unspecified.

WHILE

Start a condition-controlled loop.

Syntax: WHILE <num-expr>

Example

10 WHILE A<3

The intended loop continues while its condition is true and closes with WEND. This is a form illustration; FOR/NEXT is the complete introductory loop example.

WRITE

Serialize values to an open file channel.

Syntax: WRITE #<channel>[,<key>]:<expr-list>

Example

WRITE #1: "FIRST"

Channel 1 must be open for output. CLOSE it afterward. BASIC WRITE stores typed records rather than guaranteeing plain text bytes.

Functions

Entries marked Interface incomplete give a documented argument form and a probable functional interpretation. Their following notes identify the missing contract. Do not assume an expected result where none is stated.

ABS — absolute value

Absolute value. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: ABS(X)

Example

PRINT ABS(-3)

The numeric result in this elementary case is 3.

ASC — character-to-number conversion

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: ASC(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT ASC("A")

The string is the argument; exact empty-string and multicharacter handling are unspecified.

AT$ — cursor-position string

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: AT$(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT AT$(1,1)

Two numeric coordinates are supplied. Coordinate order/base and generated terminal bytes are unspecified.

ATN — inverse tangent

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: ATN(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT ATN(1)

A numeric argument is supplied. Angular units, precision and boundary behavior are unspecified.

BIN — binary-string conversion to a number

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: BIN(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT BIN("101")

The argument is text. Accepted alphabet, width and overflow handling are unspecified.

BINOF$ — number conversion to binary text

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: BINOF$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT BINOF$(5)

A numeric argument produces a string form; width and signed representation are unspecified.

CHR$ — character from a numeric byte value

Character from a numeric byte value. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: CHR$(X)

Example

PRINT CHR$(65)

CHR$(222) supplies the actual 0xDE field delimiter in the EXT$ example. Numeric values are not typed as literal character names.

COS — cosine

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: COS(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT COS(0)

The elementary form takes one numeric argument. General units, precision and range rules are unspecified.

CRT$ — terminal-control string conversion

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: CRT$(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT CRT$("A")

The documented argument is a string; the accepted control vocabulary and resulting bytes are unspecified. This is syntax only.

DATE$ — date string facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: DATE$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT DATE$(0)

The numeric selector's meaning and output format are unspecified. No current-date result is promised by this illustration.

DAY — date/day conversion facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: DAY(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT DAY("01/01/90")

The date text is illustrative; accepted format and numeric result meaning are unspecified.

DEL$ — delimited-string deletion family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax of the demonstrated form: DEL$(A$,X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT DEL$("ABC",1,1)

This three-argument example is accepted and prints an empty string, visible as an empty output line. A fourth argument causes FALTAN PARENTESIS and enters source-correction editing. The delimiter, position and boundary rules remain incomplete; this result is not ordinary character deletion producing AC.

DTE$ — date-text conversion facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: DTE$(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT DTE$("01/01/90")

The accepted input date grammar and output representation are unspecified.

EOF — end condition for a file channel

End condition for a file channel. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: EOF(X)

Example

PRINT EOF(1)

Query this after a read on open channel 1. An end condition can be -1; it is separate from a CSI result and from physical chain termination.

ERL — error-location value

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: ERL

Example of the documented form

PRINT ERL

Use in an established error context. The value need not be zero before a deliberate error is raised. Exact value outside a handler and interpreted/compiled correspondence are unspecified.

ERR — error-code value

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: ERR

Example of the documented form

PRINT ERR

The value belongs to BASIC's error state, not necessarily a SISTEMA.MENSERR record number. It need not be zero before a deliberate error is raised. Complete lifetime rules are unspecified.

EXP — exponential function

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: EXP(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT EXP(1)

A numeric argument is supplied. Numeric limits, rounding and overflow behavior are unspecified.

EXT$ — extract a delimited field or subfield

Extract a delimited field or subfield. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: EXT$(A$,X,Y)

Example

PRINT EXT$("UN"&CHR$(222)&"DOS",2,0)

This returns DOS: field 2, whole field selected by subfield 0. Fields use 0xDE and subfields 0xDD.

FIX — numeric conversion/truncation facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: FIX(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT FIX(3.5)

The distinction from INT and FLOAT is not fully specified. This form alone does not promise a rounding result.

FLOAT — conversion to a real numeric value

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: FLOAT(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT FLOAT(3)

The accepted numeric input and exceptional conversion rules are incompletely specified.

FORMAT$ — numeric formatting into a string

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: FORMAT$(X,A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT FORMAT$(7,"###")

The second argument is a format string. Its pattern grammar is unspecified; the hashes illustrate a string argument, not a guaranteed supported mask.

HEX — hexadecimal-text conversion to a number

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: HEX(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT HEX("FF")

Accepted prefixes, case, width and overflow rules are unspecified.

HEXOF$ — number conversion to hexadecimal text

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: HEXOF$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT HEXOF$(255)

The function takes a number; string width and signed representation are unspecified.

INP — input-related numeric function

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: INP

Example of the documented form

PRINT INP

The documented form has no explicit argument. Its source, blocking behavior and result meaning are unknown; this is syntax only.

INS$ — delimited-string insertion family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: INS$(A$,X,Y,B$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT INS$("ABC",1,1,"X")

Four arguments are supplied. Exact field/subfield positioning and insertion behavior are unspecified.

INT — integer-valued numeric conversion

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: INT(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT INT(3.5)

Do not infer negative-number rounding from the name. FIX and INT may differ; their complete contracts are unspecified.

LEFT$ — left-hand string selection

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LEFT$(A$,X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LEFT$("ABC",2)

The string and numeric length form are documented. Bounds and exceptional-length behavior are unspecified.

LEN — string-length query

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LEN(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LEN("ABC")

The argument is a string. Treatment of embedded control bytes and length limits is incompletely specified.

LINE — line-state query

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LINE(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LINE(1)

The selector domain and whether it denotes a channel or output device are unspecified. This is syntax only.

With no channel open, this example reports [27] Fich. Cerrado. It is not a complete positive query example: the required open-channel context and useful returned-value contract remain incompletely specified.

LOG — logarithm

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LOG(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LOG(1)

The base, input domain and error handling are not fully specified for this interface.

LPAD$ — left-side string padding

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LPAD$(A$,X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LPAD$("A",4)

The numeric argument is a size-like value. Padding character, truncation and width conventions are unspecified.

LRL — left rotation family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LRL(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LRL(1,1)

Two numeric arguments are documented. Bit width, direction/count interpretation and signed behavior are unspecified.

LRR — right rotation family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LRR(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LRR(2,1)

Two numeric arguments are documented; exact bit-operation rules are unspecified.

LSL — left shift family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LSL(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LSL(1,1)

The name suggests shifting, but operand width and count limits are unspecified. No numeric result is asserted.

LSR — right shift family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LSR(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LSR(2,1)

Logical versus sign-propagating behavior and operand width are unspecified.

LTRIM$ — left-side string trimming

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: LTRIM$(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT LTRIM$("  A")

The exact removable character set and empty-string handling are unspecified.

MATCH — string matching

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: MATCH(A$,B$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT MATCH("ABC","A")

The pattern language and numeric result convention are unspecified. Do not assume modern regular-expression syntax.

MAX — larger-value selection

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: MAX(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT MAX(2,3)

The elementary form has two numeric arguments. Mixed-type and exceptional-value conversion rules are unspecified.

MID$ — interior string selection

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: MID$(A$,X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT MID$("ABC",2,1)

A string and two numeric arguments are supplied. Exact indexing and out-of-range rules are incompletely specified.

MIN — smaller-value selection

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: MIN(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT MIN(2,3)

Two numeric arguments are supplied; general conversion and boundary rules are unspecified.

MOD — remainder facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: MOD(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT MOD(7,2)

Signed, fractional and zero-divisor behavior is unspecified. Do not infer it from integer division alone.

NBR — numeric query with an unspecified role

Interface incomplete.

Syntax of the demonstrated form: NBR(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT NBR("1")

This string-argument example prints -1. A numeric argument produces NECESITA EXPRESION STRING and enters correction editing. The returned value's meaning remains unknown; this example does not establish numeric conversion or any other general interpretation.

OCT — octal-text conversion to a number

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: OCT(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT OCT("10")

Accepted input alphabet, prefixes and overflow handling are unspecified.

OCTOF$ — number conversion to octal text

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: OCTOF$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT OCTOF$(8)

The output width and representation of negative values are unspecified.

OVR$ — delimited-string overwrite family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: OVR$(A$,X,Y,B$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT OVR$("ABC",1,1,"X")

The four-argument form is documented; exact field/subfield and replacement rules are unspecified.

PAGE — page-state query

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: PAGE(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT PAGE(1)

The selector domain, counter units and output-device relationship are unspecified.

With no channel open, this example reports [27] Fich. Cerrado. It is not a complete positive query example: the required open-channel context and useful returned-value contract remain incompletely specified.

PI — pi numeric constant

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: PI

Example of the documented form

PRINT PI

The function-like constant takes no argument. Exact stored precision is unspecified.

POS — position-state query

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: POS(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT POS(1)

The numeric selector and returned coordinate convention are unspecified.

With no channel open, this example reports [27] Fich. Cerrado. It is not a complete positive query example: the required open-channel context and useful returned-value contract remain incompletely specified.

REP$ — delimited-string replacement family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: REP$(A$,X,Y,B$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT REP$("ABC",1,1,"X")

Exact replacement range and field/subfield behavior are unspecified; do not substitute it for EXT$.

RIGHT$ — right-hand string selection

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: RIGHT$(A$,X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT RIGHT$("ABC",2)

The documented form accepts a string and number; exact bounds and exceptional lengths are unspecified.

RND — random-number generation

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: RND

Example of the documented form

PRINT RND

The documented form takes no argument. Distribution, range and reproducibility rules are unspecified.

ROUND — numeric rounding

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: ROUND(X,Y)

Example of the documented form

PRINT ROUND(1.25,1)

The second argument's scale and midpoint rule are unspecified. No particular rounded result is promised.

RPAD$ — right-side string padding

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: RPAD$(A$,X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT RPAD$("A",4)

Padding character, truncation and width interpretation are unspecified.

RPT$ — repeat a string

Repeat a string. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: RPT$(X,A$)

Example

PRINT RPT$(3,"X")

The count comes first, then the string. This produces three X characters. Reversing the argument types is not accepted.

RTRIM$ — right-side string trimming

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: RTRIM$(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT RTRIM$("A  ")

The exact removable character set and bounds are unspecified.

SCH — string searching family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: SCH(X,A$,B$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT SCH(1,"ABC","B")

The order and interpretation of search operands and the result convention are unspecified; this is a form illustration only.

SEC — time-related string conversion

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: SEC(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT SEC("00:00:01")

The accepted time grammar and unit of the numeric result are unspecified.

SGN — sign classification

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: SGN(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT SGN(-3)

The single-argument form is documented; precise result values and mixed numeric rules are incompletely specified.

SIN — sine

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: SIN(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT SIN(0)

Angular units, precision and numeric bounds are unspecified.

SPACE$ — create a string of spaces

Create a string of spaces. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: SPACE$(X)

Example

PRINT SPACE$(8)

This supplies eight spaces. It is useful as the eight-byte workspace for GETUSER.

SQR — square-root family

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: SQR(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT SQR(4)

This example displays 1.999999999999, an approximation to 2. Do not use the displayed digits as an exact equality guarantee. The full numeric domain, precision and error behavior remain incompletely specified.

STR$ — convert a numeric value to text

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: STR$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT STR$(7)

Sign, spacing and decimal formatting conventions are unspecified.

TAN — tangent

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: TAN(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT TAN(0)

Angular units, precision and singularity handling are unspecified.

TIME$ — time-string facility

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: TIME$(X)

Example of the documented form

PRINT TIME$(0)

The selector and output format are unspecified; no current-time contract is implied by the example.

TRIM$ — string trimming

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: TRIM$(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT TRIM$(" A ")

The exact character set removed and relationship to LTRIM$/RTRIM$ are incompletely specified.

USR — call a numeric basic extension

Call a numeric BASIC extension. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: USR(X,Y)

Example

PRINT USR(0,0)

First select GETPRIV or GETID with OPTION USR. Offset 0 and numeric argument 0 follow those getters' contracts; other modules require their own interfaces.

USR$ — call a string basic extension

Call a string BASIC extension. The elementary use below has a defined contract.

Syntax: USR$(X,A$)

Example

PRINT USR$(0,SPACE$(8))

First select GETUSER with OPTION USR. The string supplies an eight-byte workspace; the result contains the account name.

VAL — convert numeric text to a value

Interface incomplete.

Syntax: VAL(A$)

Example of the documented form

PRINT VAL("7")

Accepted whitespace, signs, decimal notation and invalid-tail behavior are unspecified.

Additional controls and build options

OPTION BASE 0

Select zero-based array indices before DIM. DIM ARR(2) then admits 0, 1 and 2.

Example

OPTION BASE 0

OPTION PROMPT

Supply the prompt string in the documented option form. Exact scope across input statements is unspecified.

Example

OPTION PROMPT "? "

OPTION CASE

The documented option accepts a string. The supported values and whether U is accepted are unspecified; this is argument-shape illustration only, not an established case-setting recipe.

Example

OPTION CASE "U"

OPTION USR

Select the account-number extension before PRINT USR(0,0). Selection and invocation are separate operations.

Example

OPTION USR "GETID"

OPTION PRIV

Require at least privilege 1 when this compiled statement runs. A lower account is rejected; the option does not elevate privilege.

Example

OPTION PRIV 1

OPTION SERIAL

Illustrate a two-part serial binding. The value must match the intended system; 22-0 is an example, not a universal setting. All-zero binding bypasses the BASIC comparison.

Example

OPTION SERIAL 22-0

UNLOCK

Release record spans for channel 1. Exclusive OPEN ownership remains until CLOSE.

Example

UNLOCK #1

OPEN INPUT

Open an existing sequential BASIC data file for reading. It must contain records appropriate to the intended BASIC input operation.

Example

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", INPUT SEQUENTIAL

OPEN OUTPUT

Open sequential output. Choose a new filename or deliberately accept replacement; close the channel after writing.

Example

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL

OPEN UPDATE DIRECT

Open the fixed-record practice file for direct access. This is different from sequential replacement.

Example

OPEN #1: "DIRECT.DATOS:S", UPDATE DIRECT

OPEN UPDATE SEQUENTIAL

This replaces the existing file through deletion and recreation at OPEN. It is not an in-place text-editing mode.

Example

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", UPDATE SEQUENTIAL

OPEN EXTEND

Append to a correctly closed sequential file. EXTEND is a trailing option; do not use it on unfinished metadata.

Example

OPEN #1: "LINES.DATOS:S", OUTPUT SEQUENTIAL, EXTEND

OPEN LOCK

Request exclusive open admission. A waiting opener can proceed after CLOSE; UNLOCK alone is insufficient.

Example

OPEN #1: "DIRECT.DATOS:S", UPDATE DIRECT, LOCK

Compiler LIST

At CSI, compile with a console source listing.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (LIST)

Compiler NOLIST

At CSI, suppress the compilation listing.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (NOLIST)

Compiler IMPRn

At CSI, select printer 1 for the listing. The printer must be associated and ready.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (IMPR1)

Compiler XREF

At CSI, request a compilation cross-reference listing.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (XREF)

Compiler NOOBJ

NOOBJ suppresses BASICCOM generation; it does not itself request a source listing. Compiler statistics can still appear. To list the source without producing compiled output, combine LIST and NOOBJ at CSI. An older BASICCOM can remain and must not be mistaken for newly generated output.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (LIST NOOBJ)

LIST requests the source listing; NOOBJ independently suppresses the compiled file. NOOBJ alone does not imply LIST and does not suppress compilation processing.

Compiler NOPANT

At CSI, suppress console output. Avoid this while learning or diagnosing compilation.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (NOPANT)

Compiler OBJ

At CSI, select A for compiled output. A must be an accessible writable disk association.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (OBJ=A)

Compiler COMMA

The supplied help advertises COMMA for decimal-comma formatting, but this version of the compiler rejects it. The following is a rejected invocation, not a working build procedure.

Example

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (COMMA)

The result is Opcion no valida, CR = 8. The alternative compiler spelling COMA is also rejected. Use dot-decimal source in the programming examples; a working compiler decimal-comma option has not been established.

Runtime TRACE

The supplied help advertises TRACE for runtime-address tracing. The following invocation runs the compiled greeting or numeric example, but produces no visible runtime-address trace for those programs. It must not be treated as a demonstrated runtime-debugging procedure.

Example

RUN HELLO.BASICCOM:S (TRACE)

CR = 0 establishes that the program completed, not that this option was recognized. An arbitrary option also completes with the same numeric output. Other internal effects or runtime conditions remain unspecified.

For a compilation trace, use the separate compiler invocation:

BASIC HELLO.BASICOBJ:S (TRACE)

This displays PASO # 1 through PASO # 8 and source line numbers during compilation. It does not trace execution of the compiled program. The interactive BASIC editor's TRACE command is also a separate interface; see TRACE.

Runtime COMA

The supplied help advertises COMA as a runtime decimal-comma option. Its spelling has one M. The demonstrated numeric program still prints 1.5 with this option, so this is not an established way to change decimal display.

For this example, use HELLO compiled from the following two source lines, following the save-and-compile procedure in Chapter 15:

10 PRINT 1.5
20 END

This numeric version replaces the greeting used earlier. Compile it before checking decimal display; an older greeting executable cannot demonstrate a numeric-format option.

Example

RUN HELLO.BASICCOM:S (COMA)

Ordinary RUN and RUN with COMA both display 1.5 and return CR = 0. This does not establish input-conversion behavior or exclude other internal effects. PRINT 1,5 supplies two items to PRINT; it is not a decimal-comma literal. Compiler COMMA and COMA are separately rejected, as described above.

Additional organization, option and expression keywords

The following seventeen names complete the vocabulary beyond the forms above. Their presence in BASIC's keyword table establishes their spelling; it does not establish another BASIC dialect's calling conventions. KEYED and INDEXED have file-opening context, and NOT, AND and OR have identified compiled-expression operations. Several other names still lack a complete programmer-visible contract. They are retained here so that source text can be recognized without suggesting unsupported examples.

INDEXED

INDEXED selects the indexed-file organization in OPEN. For the existing INDEX.DATOS file created in Chapter 17, enter at BASIC:

OPEN #1: "INDEX.DATOS:S", UPDATE INDEXED

This connects channel 1 to the existing indexed file for update. It does not create its index or define its record and key sizes. Follow Chapter 17 for creation, writing and reading by key, and close the channel before reusing it.

KEYED

KEYED selects keyed access without the I-format ordered index area. A syntax illustration for an existing CLAVE-format file is:

OPEN #1: "KEYS.DATOS:S", UPDATE KEYED

The illustration requires KEYS.DATOS to have been created with the appropriate keyed organization and record/key dimensions. It is not interchangeable with opening an indexed file. Chapter 17 distinguishes the organizations; the detailed creation and record layout must agree with the file actually opened.

NOT

NOT is the unary logical-negation operation in the compiled expression stream, represented by byte 0x0F. It operates on one expression rather than introducing a statement. Numeric coercion, integer-width effects and the full precedence rules remain unspecified; no particular numeric result is promised here.

AND

AND combines two operands in an expression; compiled byte 0x10 selects its operation. At the conceptual level, conjunction requires both conditions to hold. The full truth-value encoding, coercion and evaluation-order contract remains unspecified. Do not assume that the second operand is skipped when the first is false.

OR

OR combines two operands in an expression; compiled byte 0x11 selects its operation. At the conceptual level, disjunction permits either condition to hold. The full numeric and evaluation-order contract remains unspecified. Its expression marker is distinct from any CSI or CONSULTA keyword. Appendix A locates these operations within the compiled format.

QUOTE

QUOTE is an option keyword. TRAFIC's output dialogue describes using OPTION QUOTE when opening PROCETEX-type output. That is a specific application use, not a complete specification of quoting, escaping or record delimiters. Do not assume that adding QUOTE creates a universally interchangeable text format; its complete syntax and scope remain unspecified.

RECORDSIZE

RECORDSIZE is recognized by BASIC's option/function table. Its permitted context, argument shape, units and result behavior are not yet specified. It must not be substituted for CREAR's REGISTRO option merely because the names describe related concepts.

FILESIZE

FILESIZE is a recognized language keyword whose argument and units remain unspecified. It cannot yet be documented as a byte-count function or a record-capacity setting. The size distinctions in Appendix C explain why those alternatives matter.

KEYSIZE

KEYSIZE is a recognized language keyword. Its complete relationship to an open channel or a file-creation request remains unspecified; use the established CREAR LONCLA syntax when defining a key length at CSI.

XOR

XOR occurs in the expression/operator vocabulary. Its operand types, numeric representation and evaluation behavior remain unspecified. The spelling alone is insufficient to import a bitwise or Boolean example from another BASIC.

IMP

IMP occurs beside the expression-operator names XOR and EQV. Its complete expression contract remains unspecified. This BASIC keyword must be kept separate from the physical printer name IMP and from similarly spelled output options in other utilities.

EQV

EQV belongs to the same expression/operator vocabulary. Its truth representation, operand conversion and precedence remain unspecified. It is not a documented replacement for an equality comparison.

DEVICE

DEVICE is a recognized language keyword. Its argument grammar and relationship to OPEN or device information remain unspecified. Device names used in an OPEN file specification do not establish a DEVICE function signature.

MEMORY

MEMORY is a recognized language keyword whose scope and arguments remain unspecified. It is separate from CSI's PONER MEMORIA partition-management setting. Do not treat it as a documented allocator or a direct-memory-access operation.

PORT

PORT is a recognized language keyword. No complete privilege, argument or hardware-access contract is specified here. Its name alone does not establish a safe BASIC interface to the I/O ports listed in Appendix B.

TAB

TAB is a recognized language keyword. Its permitted expression or output context, argument base and positioning behavior remain unspecified. Its presence does not justify importing the PRINT TAB syntax of another dialect.

UPCASE

UPCASE is a recognized language keyword, but its argument/result type and case-conversion scope remain unspecified. It must be distinguished from EDITAR's CASE U input mode and COPIFI's MAYUSC file-conversion option.

Appendix E. Binary structure atlas

This is the canonical layout reference. Offsets in these tables are hexadecimal unless a decimal quantity is explicitly stated. Multi-byte words are little-endian except the three-byte timestamp, BASIC fields and fields marked BE. A field marked unknown must be preserved rather than zeroed during recovery. The filesystem tables use an example geometry of 151 directory sectors starting at sector 9; actual header values determine another volume's bounds. Context and driver layouts describe the system interface.

Volume geometry and header

The raw physical mapping is defined in Chapter 27. For that geometry, sector 1 begins the header/bitmap region, sectors 9–159 hold 1,208 directory records, and sector 160 begins file allocation. The header's label is not a magic signature. A reader that unconditionally includes sector 160 in the directory manufactures false entries from data.

Offset Size Meaning Notes
00 8 Space-padded volume label —
08 3 Unresolved, preserve verbatim Unknown
0B 8 Archive tape label Probable; ESTADO displays matching bytes
13 4 Archive/date-related metadata Tentative; exact packing incomplete
17 2 Byte 17 high nibble = logical heads; low nibble followed by byte 18 =12-bit cylinder count kernel 0AE2–0AF8 and ESTADO09BC–0A37;17 93 =1 head,1939 cylinders
19 1 Sectors per track (20h=32) loader A0D6
1A 1 Directory sectors (97h=151) native 1208 entries / eight per sector
1B 2 Unresolved Unknown
1D 2 Free allocation blocks, little endian —
1F 1 Low six bits plus 2 gives first directory sector loader A0DC–A0E7
20 onward variable Most-significant-bit-first allocation bitmap allocation/deletion behavior

Directory record

Each record is 32 bytes, so a 256-byte sector contains exactly eight. Format 00 is an early lookup terminator; FF is a deleted slot. The active format is distinct from the protection bits.

Offset Size Meaning Notes
00 1 Format/attribute bits; FF marks an unused/deleted slot basic format mapping, other flags incomplete
01 8 Space-padded filename —
09 8 Space-padded file type —
11 2 Record count; sector count for A/R; page count for spooler-created SP$ reports native comparisons and original spool close 3E0C–3E15; see spool subsection
13 2 Allocated 1-KiB blocks —
15 2 First logical sector loader, SECTOR and copy
17 2 Maximum counted-write length for specified S output; D record length; A/R length field; packed record/key lengths for I/K selected native/code cases; S value is not necessarily a complete BASIC record length
19 3 Packed date/time, big-endian bitfield —
1C 1 Owning account number; recipient account for a sharing alias; special FF namespace native ownership changes, lookup and sharing controls; see the alias and mailbox layouts below
1D 1 Nonzero target account for a sharing lookup redirect native COMPART grant/revocation and resolved access; see alias layout below
1E 2 R load-image bytes; S final linked sector; A load address; I/K slot capacity R/S on specified files; A from loader
Format value Native letter Storage interpretation
0x01 R Relocatable executable
0x02 A Absolute executable
0x04 S Linked sequential payload
0x08 D Direct fixed-length records
0x10 I Ordered indexed slots
0x18 K Hashed keyed slots
0x20 Read protection Attribute, not compression
0x40 Deletion protection Attribute
0x80 Write protection Attribute; exactly 0x80 can be a sharing alias

For the three-byte big-endian timestamp D: month=(D>>20)&15, day=(D>>15)&31, year=1977+((D>>11)&15), hour=(D>>6)&31, minute=D&63. There is no seconds field. Its year range is 1977–1992. Do not extend its range by assuming a modern epoch.

Sharing alias

Offset Size Alias-specific meaning
00 1 80h in the specified sharing alias
01 8 Same filename as target
09 8 Same type as target
11–1B 11 Zero in the created alias; no own record count/extent/timestamp
1C 1 Recipient account number
1D 1 Target owner account number
1E 2 Zero in the created alias

Payload structures

Format Byte-level rule
S sector +0x00..+0xFD payload 254 bytes; +0xFE LE16 next LBA; zero link terminates
D record n Starts at byte (n-1)*R from file start; full 256-byte sectors; n is one-based
I prefix N LE16 slot numbers; pad prefix to B=ceil(2*N/1024)*1024
I slot s At B+(s-1)*(K+R+2): key K bytes, data R bytes, next-slot LE16
K slot s At(s-1)*(K+R): key K bytes followed by data R bytes

For I/K, directory word+0x17 packs R=(word&0x1FF)+1 and K=(word>>9)+1; word+0x1E is slot capacity N. I allocation blocks are ceil(2*N/1024)+ceil(N*(K+R+2)/1024). The first C prefix entries, with C from directory+0x11, identify active slots in sorted key order. K uses the hash/probe algorithm in Chapter 26. A zero first key byte ends an unused probe; FF marks a tombstone.

R relocation records and BASIC object layouts appear in Appendix A. Allocation slack and sequential padding are not application data lengths.

Partition context

The context base is the current pointer at 0x0033 or the pointer in a scheduler row. Its 0x4CC-byte fixed area precedes the program. The following table deliberately includes unknown regions.

Saved execution frame

At the saved SP, words are HL′, DE′, BC′, AF′, IY, IX, HL, DE, BC, AF and continuation PC, in that order, at offsets 0x00, 02, 04, 06, 08, 0A, 0C, 0E, 10, 12, 14. F is the low byte of each AF word. This is 22 bytes; further frames can follow. No I/R/IFF or interrupt-mode field is established here. Callback entry leaves the continuation at SP=savedSP+0x14 for RET.

Caller channel request

Offset Size Meaning Notes
00 1 Channel index, accepted range 0..16 kernel 34D9
01 1 Operation/method flags;38h used by BASIC UPDATE INDEXED observed value; complete bits partial
02 2 Little-endian pointer to caller-owned sector/index workspace overlay 1:4692 and I/O callers

1D 2 Next slot for sequential traversal indexed READ/READNEXT

Spooler queue records and collector slots are both 16 bytes but have different layouts. Mail bodies use CR lines and 0x7F boundary padding; their enclosing S sectors still carry 254-byte payloads and two-byte links. Neither framing replaces the filesystem structure.

Physical descriptor unions and driver-private storage

A physical descriptor has 32 bytes and begins at 0x0069+32*index. The following tables describe the identified fields for the stated family; gaps remain unclassified. A disk's geometry/free-space field must not be decoded using a character device's class-length interpretation.
