Glossary

Definitions use the system's own distinctions. Reference numbers identify chapters; letters identify appendices. Chapter 31 defines command names and options, Appendix D defines BASIC commands and statements, and Appendix C explains individual system files. This glossary supplies the shared concepts, abbreviations and device terminology needed to read those references.

A format — Absolute executable format. Its directory entry supplies the load address; it is distinct from drive A. See 18, 30; A.

ABI — Application binary interface: the machine-level agreement on registers, memory layouts, entry points and returns. A driver ABI need not be the same as the system-service ABI. See 19, 24, 27, 28.

ABS / REL / COM — Legacy object or toolchain labels appearing in diagnostic vocabulary. Their presence does not establish a supported object format or a complete assembler/linker procedure. COM must also be distinguished from physical COM1–COM4 device names. See 18, 33; A.

account — Named user identity with account number and privilege. It is distinct from a scheduled partition. See 9, 10, 22.

AF — Z80 register pair combining accumulator A and flags F. A service may return a value in A and a separate condition in a flag. The notation AF′ denotes the alternate pair. See 19, 24, 32; E.

allocation block — Four 256-byte sectors, totaling 1,024 bytes. Allocation is tracked by the volume bitmap. See 23, 26; E.

ALP — Terminal interface represented by SCC channels, DMA controllers and flow-control ports. Four consoles are established in this architecture. See 28; B.

API — Application programming interface: the operations and data conventions exposed to application software. Here, system services, BASIC facilities and device interfaces have their own individual contracts. See 17, 19, 32.

ASCII — American Standard Code for Information Interchange, a character encoding used for letters, digits and control characters. A character value and the terminal action assigned to it are different matters. See 4, 19, 28.

ASI — Label of a firmware diagnostic whose omission is controlled by a configuration bit. Its complete expansion and hardware scope are not established here. See B.

association — Partition-local mapping from a logical device use to a physical descriptor. See 12, 23, 34.

bank — Backing 48-KiB RAM region selected into CPU addresses 4000–FFFF. A scheduler index need not equal a bank number. See 20, 22, 25.

BASIC — The system's programming language and integrated development environment, identified as BASIC S20. It provides source editing, interpretation, compilation and runtime execution. BASICOBJ, BASICCOM and BASICUSR name distinct representations used by that environment. See 15–17; D.

BASICCOM — Compiled BASIC statement/expression stream interpreted by the installed runtime; not an R executable. See 15, 16; A.

BASICOBJ — Tokenized saved BASIC source, despite the object-like type name. See 15; A.

BASICUSR — R-format machine-code extension selected by BASIC OPTION USR. See 19; A.

BC — Z80 register pair composed of B and C. Interfaces use it for counts, selectors and other values; its meaning is specified separately for each call. See 19, 32; E.

BCD — Binary-coded decimal: each nibble represents a decimal digit. Used in clock state and BASIC decimal payloads. See 19; A, B.

BE — Big-endian byte order: the most significant byte of a multi-byte value comes first. Several BASIC fields and the packed directory timestamp use this order, even though ordinary Z80 words are little-endian. See A, E.

bitmap — Allocation map with most-significant-bit-first bits, one per 1-KiB block. See 26; E.

bootstrap — Disk loader read by firmware before the full executive. Physical bootstrap coordinates differ from logical volume addresses. See 3, 21, 27.

BUZON — Mailbox retrieval utility; also the type/name context of the sequential persistent mailbox. Retrieval removes messages. See 9, 29, 31.

callback — Routine entered when an event occurs, sometimes in another partition's saved context. It is not necessarily a new execution thread. See 19, 25, 28.

capture definition — Screen, field and file-access description used by the optional ACTUCAP and EXPAND support tools to prepare a data-entry program. See C.

carry — Z80 flag used by some interfaces for error or emptiness, by others for device readiness. There is no universal OS-wide meaning. See 19, 32.

catalogue — The volume's fixed directory records. Exact-name probing and wildcard enumeration traverse it differently. See 5, 23, 26.

CDC — Physical printer definition served by SISTEMA.DIS12. It is distinct from logical printer names such as IMPR1. See 12, 28, 34; B.

channel — One of seventeen per-partition file/device records, indexed 0–16 at the kernel interface. See 17, 24; E.

CHS — Cylinder, head and sector coordinates of a physical disk. The hard-disk interface uses zero-based sector IDs. See 27.

CLASE — Terminal-class program translating logical console controls. It is separate from the physical DIS driver. See 4, 28.

COM1–COM4 — Physical communication-device definitions. They are different from the partition-local COMU names and from file types. A definition does not establish that the necessary hardware and complete driver behavior are available. See 34; B.

COMANDO — File type used by most CSI utilities; native executable format is independently recorded in the directory. See 7, 30, 31.

command processor — reads command input, resolves commands and starts executable/interpreter paths. See 5, 30 and CSI.

command result — The value reported as CR after command execution. It does not certify media persistence or message delivery. See 13, 33.

common RAM — The low 16-KiB region shared across bank selections after reset alias removal. See 20; B.

COMPART alias — Directory redirect granting a recipient access to another account's private file without transferring ownership. See 10, 22; E.

COMU — Family of logical communication-device names, such as COMU1, resolved through associations. Do not substitute a physical name such as COM1 without establishing its association. See 23, 34.

CONSOLA — Logical console name used in command syntax. Input and output resolve through console associations and need not be treated as a physical terminal model name. See 12, 23, 34.

context — Partition header, channels and workspaces together with pointers to its execution state. See 24, 25; E.

CONTROL.VOC — Indexed attribute dictionary used by query and vocabulary-support software; distinct from EXEC’s CONTROL directive. See 35; C.

counted I/O — Sequential byte transfer through 5E/5F. Counted-write call count is not necessarily an application's record count. See 17, 26, 32.

CPU — Central processing unit: the processor executing instructions. The Z80 has a 64-KiB address window; bank selection changes which memory is visible through part of that window. See 2, 20, 22.

CR — Two different abbreviations occur. In character I/O, CR means carriage return, character 0x0D, sent by Return to submit a command line. In command completion messages, CR means command result, a numeric status. See CR character and command result; 4, 9, 33.

CR character — Carriage return, 0x0D. Distinct from the abbreviation CR for command result. See 4, 33.

CRT — Cathode-ray tube, the display technology of the IS 9502 terminal. CRT also appears as an incompletely specified EXEC word, and CRT$ is a BASIC terminal-control string function whose full control vocabulary is unspecified. See 4, 8; D.

CSI — The command processor: the program that displays the > system prompt, reads command lines, finds commands and starts their execution. “System prompt” is also used informally for this command environment; strictly, the prompt is the visible > character, while CSI is the program behind it. Its system file is SISTEMA.CSI. CSI version 5.5 is separate from operating-system Version 6.2. See 1, 5, 30; C.

D format — Direct fixed-length record storage. Record numbers are one-based. See 17, 26; E.

DE — Z80 register pair composed of D and E. Many services use DE for a pointer or value, but it has no universal argument role. See 19, 32; E.

DEL — Delete character, 0x7F. CSI accepts Delete for removing the preceding input character; other programs can apply different input rules. See 4, 28.

descriptor — A 32-byte physical-device record whose fields depend on device family. See 23, 28; E, B.

DIRAL — ESTADO MEMORIA column showing the current memory ceiling minus one, from context offset 0x04. Loaded drivers or classes can lower this boundary within the allocated region. See 12, 20; E.

DIRBA — ESTADO MEMORIA column showing the partition base address, from context offset 0x02. It is a memory address, not a disk-directory number. See 12; E.

DIRECT — BASIC file organization using fixed-length records selected by record number. It corresponds to native D-format storage. See D format; 17, 26; D, E.

directory entry — A 32-byte record containing filename, type, format, allocation, timestamp and ownership. See 26; E.

DIRMX — ESTADO MEMORIA column showing the final address of the allocated partition region: the word at context offset 0x06 minus one. It can exceed the current usable ceiling shown as DIRAL. See 12; E.

DIS — System driver-file family, for example SISTEMA.DIS10 for tape and SISTEMA.DIS18 for PAN1. The numeric suffix selects a driver definition; it is not a system-call number. See 23, 28, 34; B, C.

DISCO1–DISCO6 — Physical disk definitions. Logical letters S and A–G acquire their physical meaning through associations to definitions such as these. See 11, 23, 34.

DMA — Direct memory access used for established ALP output, with channel-pair bank latches independent of CPU bank selection. See 28; B.

DRQ — Data request: a disk-controller status signal used to coordinate transfers through the data register. Consult the controller map for the relevant status bit and direction. See 27; B.

DTR / REQ — Data-terminal-ready and request signals named in the serial/DMA interface. They concern hardware handshaking, not whether an account is logged in. See 28; B.

EBCDIC — Extended Binary Coded Decimal Interchange Code, the character encoding used by the IBM 3741 interchange utilities. SECIBM converts outgoing sequential text from ASCII; IBMSEC performs the reverse conversion. See 11, 31.

EOF — End-of-file condition. Kernel chain exhaustion, a sequential 1A marker and BASIC's EOF function are different layers. See 17, 26, 33.

EOV — Tape-label prefix used when forming end-of-volume trailer labels; EOF is used in corresponding end-of-file labels. These label prefixes are separate from a program's EOF return condition. See A.

ESC — Escape character, 0x1B, also written Escape in keyboard instructions. It introduces several CSI control actions and terminal-class output sequences. Those uses have different consumers. See 4, 28.

ETX — End-of-text character, 0x03. In the ALP output path it is one of the characters that can trigger a buffered-output flush. Its name does not make it a universal program-termination command. See 28.

exclusive open — Admission policy in the file-open table. It survives UNLOCK and does not reliably prevent native deletion. See 17, 25.

EXEC — Command-file interpreter and sequential file type. Full spelling avoids the established EX/EXE token-transfer defect. See 8, 30.

EXECLANG — Installed executable implementing EXEC; distinct from EXECn work files. See 8, 21.

EXTEND — Sequential append mode. It locates the final sector and trims trailing 1A; a zero endpoint can damage reserved metadata. See 17, 26.

FF — In hexadecimal layouts, FF denotes byte 0xFF: depending on context, a deleted entry, special owner, unassigned association or another sentinel. In terminal-control names, FF means form feed, character 0x0C. Distinguish the character name from the hexadecimal byte value. See 28; E.

FIFO — First in, first out: a queue in which the earliest unread item is consumed first. The term also labels a controller data interface; it does not specify its capacity or error behavior. See 25, 27; B.

file type — The second eight-byte filename field. It does not alone determine storage format. See 5, 23.

FIN — Account disconnection command, not a documented global shutdown. See 3, 9, 14.

FLOPPY1 / FLOPPY2 — Physical floppy-device definitions served by SISTEMA.DIS1 and SISTEMA.DIS2. A logical disk letter such as A must be associated with the intended definition before file access. See 11, 27, 34.

FM-7 — Fujitsu computer also offered under Secoinsa branding during its move into personal and educational computing. It is a separate product from the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer. See Preface.

format byte — Directory field combining native organization with protection attributes. See 23; E.

frame — Saved CPU register/continuation data, or an EXEC level's 512-byte saved interpretation state; specify which. See 8, 25; E, A.

free run — Consecutive unallocated blocks available for contiguous allocation. It can be smaller than total free space. See 11, 26.

Fujitsu — Japanese technology company that was a founding partner of Secoinsa and supplied technology to its product range. Fujitsu España later absorbed Secoinsa. See Preface.

HDD — Hard-disk drive. This abbreviation appears in descriptions of startup diagnostics and hardware configuration. See 21, 27; B.

HL — Z80 register pair composed of H and L, commonly used for pointers, counts and results. HL′ is its alternate pair. Interpret it according to the individual interface. See 19, 32; E.

I format — Indexed storage with a sorted slot-number prefix and next-slot links. See 17, 26; E.

IBM 3741 — The floppy interchange format handled by INICIBM, DIRIBM, SECIBM and IBMSEC. Its headers, records and character conversion require those specialized utilities. See 11, 31.

IFF — Z80 interrupt-enable state, commonly referred to through IFF1 and IFF2. It is separate from flags in AF. The documented scheduler frame does not establish saved IFF fields. See 24, 25; E.

IM — Z80 interrupt mode. See IM 0 for the mode used by the documented interrupt interface; do not infer a different mode from a vector address alone. See 24; B.

IM 0 — Z80 interrupt mode using a supplied opcode; the interrupt interface supplies restart instructions. See 24; B.

IMP — Physical printer definition associated with SISTEMA.DIS22. An application normally selects a logical printer such as IMPR1, which ASOCIAR connects to IMP. See 12, 28, 34.

IMPEXEC — Setting permitting implicit command-file execution. See 8, 31.

IMPR1–IMPR4 — Logical printer names available through partition associations. PRINT1 is an option selecting a printer in commands such as LISTAR; IMP is a physical printer definition. See 12, 23, 34.

INDEXED — BASIC file organization with ordered keys and an index prefix. It corresponds to native I-format storage and differs from KEYED. See I format; 17, 26; D, E.

INI — Instituto Nacional de Industria, Spain's state industrial holding company and a founding partner of Secoinsa. See Preface.

inline service number — Byte immediately after RST08 identifying one of 111 services. See 18, 24, 32.

installation serial — Three-byte software binding checked by selected command/BASIC guards; unrelated to serial-port baud. See 10, 16, 30.

IP — Physical parallel-printer definition associated with SISTEMA.DIS11. Here IP is a device name, not an Internet network address. See 28, 34; B.

IPL — Initial program load/restart path, including Escape-I. Declining the specified request ends the command rather than resuming it. See 14, 21.

IS 9502 — The Secoinsa computer terminal paired with the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer, with a separate keyboard and a green monochrome display of 24 lines by 80 characters. See Preface; 4.

IX — Z80 index register. Its role varies between interfaces; do not assume it always points to the same kind of structure. See 19, 24, 32; E.

IY — Z80 index register. It often points to executive, context or descriptor data in the documented routines, but the required meaning is caller-specific. See 19, 24, 32; E.

K format — Hashed key/data slots without the I-format index prefix or next-link trailer. See 17, 26; E.

kernel — The resident executive and associated kernel overlays in SISTEMA.NUCLEO; native displays separate the spooler extent. See 20, 24.

KEYED — BASIC file organization using hashed keys rather than the ordered index of INDEXED files. It corresponds to native K-format storage. See K format; 17, 26; D, E.

LBA — Logical sector number within a volume. Translate through that volume's direction and replacement table to obtain CHS. See 26, 27.

LE — Little-endian byte order: the least significant byte comes first. LE16 means a two-byte, 16-bit value in that order. Byte order must be read from the specified structure, not assumed from its file type. See A, E.

LE16 — A 16-bit little-endian value occupying two bytes. See LE; A, E.

LF — Line-feed character, 0x0A. Terminal output uses it to advance the line; Return submits CSI input using CR. Device options beginning LF can specify line-feed delay. See 4, 12, 28.

LIFO — Last in, first out: newly queued input is placed ahead of earlier input. Separate from EXEC CONTROL STACK/NOSTACK. See 35.

logical device — A partition-local use such as S, console input or IMPR1, resolved through an association. See 12, 23, 34.

MENSERR — Direct file holding 200 fixed 64-byte message records. See 24, 33.

MM58167 — Real-time-clock device named in the hardware map. Its counters and control registers are separate from the filesystem's compact timestamp representation. See B.

module — Term used in native memory displays and also for loaded code units; it is not automatically a scheduler partition. See 12, 20.

NUCLEO — Kernel file and native memory-region name. The stored boot image includes initialization material beyond the resident body. See 20, 21.

NUL — Null character or zero byte, 0x00. Its role depends on the structure: it may terminate a string or mark another condition. It is not interchangeable with a space, CR or EOF. See 19, 32; A, E.

OASIS — Lineage indicated by original copyrights and tape templates. Exact release/API compatibility remains unknown. See A.

OPEN — Channel setup operation whose mode can replace a sequential file immediately. It is not inherently read-only. See 17, 26, 32.

OS — Operating system: the executive, services and associated command environment described in this document. The identified release is S.O. Multi-usuario Version 6.2. See 1, 20.

overlay — Replaceable code loaded into a shared execution window. Identify its number before interpreting an address. See 20, 24.

owner — Account byte in a file entry, or scheduler-index-derived tag in a lock. The two representations are distinct. See 10, 22, 25.

PAB — Term present in original messages whose complete object/toolchain meaning is not established in this operating system. See 33; A.

PAN — Family of physical terminal definitions, such as PAN1 and PAN2. PAN1–PAN4 use the established ALP console drivers; other numbers can designate optional interfaces. These are device names, not account names. See 12, 28, 34.

partition — The OS's scheduled execution context, with memory, channels, associations and identity. Broadly comparable to a process, but without an assumed modern ABI or protection boundary. See 12, 22, 25.

PC — Program counter, the Z80 register identifying the next instruction. PC in execution-state and memory tables does not mean personal computer. See 12, 24, 25; E.

physical device — Descriptor-selected hardware/driver endpoint; a definition can exist without fitted hardware. See 23, 28, 34.

proceso fantasma — Original help's background-process term. Complete activation syntax and lifecycle remain unspecified. See 7, 31.

prologue — Optional CF41 command entry sequence with preceding privilege/serial data. See 10, 30.

public file — Account-0 file considered by fallback lookup unless inhibited. Special accountFF is different. See 5, 10, 26.

R format — Relocatable machine-code image with directory-defined length and sector-based relocation records. See 18, 30; A.

RAM — Random-access memory used for the executive, partitions, buffers and running programs. The architecture divides it into common memory and bank-selected regions. See 2, 20; B.

record lock — Owned inclusive sector span associated with a channel; not an exclusive file-open entry. See 17, 25; E.

relocation — Adding the selected load base to identified address words in an R image. See 18, 30; A.

resident — Retained in the established memory layout across ordinary command execution; does not imply every byte is common across banks. See 20, 29.

ROM — Read-only memory containing startup firmware. Its diagnostic and bootstrap role is separate from the disk-loaded executive and BASIC environment. See 2, 21; B.

RST — Z80 restart instruction transferring to a fixed vector. RST08 carries OS calls; other vectors have distinct uses. See 18, 24; B.

RTC — Real-time clock, the hardware maintaining date/time information. See MM58167; B.

RTS — Request to send, a serial-interface control signal. The interpretation of the ALP receive-flow-control wiring as RTS remains probable rather than fully specified. See 28; B.

RX / TX — Receive and transmit, respectively, in serial-controller and interrupt maps. Receive input and transmit completion can use different buffers and callbacks. See 28; B.

S format — Sequential linked sectors with 254 payload bytes each. See 17, 23, 26; E.

S.O. Multi-usuario — The operating system's displayed name; this document describes Version 6.2. CSI, EXEC and BASIC carry their own component version numbers. See 1.

S20 — Component designation in the name BASIC S20. It identifies the BASIC environment for the Secoinsa Serie 20 computer; its version number is separate from the operating-system release. See 1, 15.

SCC — Serial communications controller. The ALP serial paths use SCC channels for terminal communication, with separate DMA support for output. See 28; B.

scheduler index — Zero-based execution-record identifier, distinct from displayed partition number and bank selector. See 22, 25.

Secoinsa — Sociedad Española de Comunicaciones e Informática, S.A., the Spanish computer and communications company whose Secoinsa Serie 20 computer is described here. See Preface.

sector — A 256-byte unit in this logical filesystem and hard-disk interface; floppy translation can involve 128-byte physical sectors. See 23, 27.

SEQUENTIAL — BASIC file organization using sequential records over linked S-format sectors. Replacement, append and positioning behavior depend on the selected OPEN mode. See S format and EXTEND; 17, 26; D, E.

sharing — Access through a recipient alias to the owner's file. It differs from ownership transfer and shared OPEN admission. See 10, 17, 25.

SIO / PIO / PSIO / CPSIO — Driver/class labels in physical device definitions. They identify interface behavior and defaults; use the definition and associated driver rather than inferring a complete device contract from the letters alone. See 12, 23, 28, 34; C.

SISTEMA — The administrative account used in the procedures, and separately a common filename stem for operating-system components. SISTEMA as an account, SISTEMA.CSI as a file and S as a logical disk are different identifiers. See 3, 5, 10; C.

slack — Allocated bytes beyond current payload. They are allocated but do not belong to the file's current useful contents. See 13, 26.

SP — Stack pointer, the Z80 register locating the current stack. A saved partition SP points to register words and a continuation address used when execution resumes. See 19, 24, 25; E.

SPOOLCOL — Direct queue file with sixteen-byte records used by the resident spooler. See 29; E.

spooler — Resident deferred-output collector and printer manager. Native collection is established; complete physical printing/restart remains incomplete. See 29, 31.

SS2 — Interface grouping including timer/interrupt and optional serial paths in this architecture; complete physical routing remains uncertain. See 28; B.

ST0 / ST1 / ST2 — Floppy-controller result/status bytes. Interpret each bit through the driver's documented error translation; these are not CSI command-result values. See 27; B.

STARTUP — Executable system-initialization component, SISTEMA.STARTUP. It relocates and initializes system code; it is not an editable EXEC command file. See 2, 21; C.

synonym — Configured alternate command name, optionally with a minimum prefix length. Custom synonyms and actual command files can take precedence over standard abbreviation. See 5, 30, 35.

system volume — Drive S, whose associated filesystem supplies kernel, CSI, messages and other required components. See 11, 21, 27.

TAP — Physical tape-driver definition, associated with SISTEMA.DIS10. It is normally connected to the logical name TAPE1. See 13, 28, 34.

TAPE1 — Logical tape-device name used by the tape utilities and driver initialization. It resolves through an association to TAP; it is not a disk letter or a filesystem directory. See 13, 28, 34.

TC — Terminal count, a DMA completion indication. In the hardware maps, it concerns transfer completion and must not be read as a terminal-user or account count. See 28; B.

TE — Elapsed-time field printed in command completion reports, expressed in minutes. It is separate from CR, the command result, and is not another error code.

Telesincro — Spanish electronics and business-computer company whose Factor product range and engineering experience became part of the Secoinsa group. See Preface.

terminal class — See CLASE. See 28.

tombstone — MarkerFF for a deleted keyed slot or catalogue entry; interpretation depends on the surrounding structure. See 26; E.

UPDATE — BASIC access mode. Sequential UPDATE without EXTEND replaces the file; direct UPDATE uses numbered records. See 17, 26.

VOL1 / HDR1 / HDR2 — Identifiers in the 80-byte tape-label structures. VOL1 is a volume label; HDR1 and HDR2 are file-label records. Familiar label names do not by themselves establish compatibility with another tape format. See 13; A.

volume — Logical filesystem with label, geometry, directory and bitmap; its bounds and mapping are held in volume metadata. See 5, 23, 27.

WD / WD1001 — Names used for the hard-disk controller and its register interface. Driver retries, data-register transfers and status bits must be interpreted according to that interface. See 27; B.

workspace — Caller- or context-owned temporary storage used by a service; ownership and lifetime must be stated. See 17, 24; E.

yield — Voluntary entry to scheduling through 4F. Timer preemption also exists independently. See 22, 25.

Z flag — Z80 zero flag. It can report a zero result or an interface-specific condition; some services return A and flags with different meanings. See 19, 32.

Z80 — The processor family executing this operating system. Native programming examples use its registers, restart instructions and 16-bit address space. See 18–20; B, E.

Index

Links lead directly to the relevant section. Chapter and appendix references remain useful in print.

Emulation

Term Reference
MAME: startup, menus and shutdown Emulation in MAME
Hard-disk difference files and backups Emulation in MAME
Floppy insertion, replacement and formatting Emulation in MAME
Serial printer capture Emulation in MAME

Emulation in MAME

Operating the SECOINSA Serie 20 and S.O. Multi-Usuario 6.2 in MAME.

Use a MAME version containing the secoinsa20 driver and the somu62 software-list entry (S.O. Multi-Usuario 6.2). Working emulation support begins with MAME 0.290.

Commands beginning with mame are entered on the host computer. S.O. Multi-Usuario commands are entered at the emulated system’s > prompt.

Installing the images and starting MAME

Place the machine ROM set and system disk in MAME’s ROM search path. One suitable arrangement is:

roms/
    secoinsa20.zip
    secoinsa20/
        somu62/
            somu62.chd

Start the machine with:

mame secoinsa20 somu62 -window

This selects the pre-installed S.O. Multi-Usuario 6.2 hard disk and the default compatible serial terminal. Supplying only secoinsa20 does not automatically select the system disk.

Allow the original startup sequence to finish, then log in as:

SISTEMA

This account has no password. The image contains the system volume, accessible as :S.

For example:

LISDIR SISTEMA.*:S
AYUDA

Switching between the terminal and MAME menus

During normal operation, the keyboard controls the emulated terminal. To operate MAME’s menus:

  1. Press Scroll Lock to enable MAME’s UI controls.
  2. Press Tab to open the menu.
  3. After making changes, close the menu and disable UI controls to resume typing into the terminal.

The UI toggle defaults to Forward Delete on macOS. It can also be reassigned through MAME’s input settings.

The displayed console is an emulated serial terminal. The default terminal understands the SECOINSA control sequences used by the system; no separate terminal application or manual serial configuration is needed.

Saving work and returning to a clean system

When somu62 is selected through the software list, MAME keeps hard-disk changes in a separate difference file, normally:

diff/somu62.dif

The original somu62.chd remains unchanged. Starting MAME again with the same difference directory continues using the modified disk.

To keep a separate working installation:

mame secoinsa20 somu62 -window -diff_directory "work/main/diff"

To start from the clean system image for an experiment:

mame secoinsa20 somu62 -window -diff_directory "work/experiment1/diff" -noautosave

Choose a directory that does not already contain a difference file. Reusing that directory resumes the experiment’s disk contents.

Back up the difference file while MAME is closed, together with its matching parent CHD. The parent CHD alone does not contain your subsequent work. MAME saved states are not substitutes for disk backups.

Starting with a floppy inserted

To start with an existing floppy image:

mame secoinsa20 somu62 -window -flop1 "media/work.mfi"

In the default machine configuration, MAME’s -flop1 device corresponds to FLOPPY2 inside S.O. Multi-Usuario.

After logging in, associate an unused logical disk letter and notify the operating system of the inserted medium:

ASOCIAR A FLOPPY2
MONTAR A
LISDIR *.*:A

These instructions assume that logical disk letter A is available. Enter ASOCIAR without arguments to inspect the current associations.

MFI is the tested format for writable floppy images. It preserves the mixed recording densities and sector sizes used by the original formatting utility. An ordinary PC-formatted disk image is not equivalent.

Inserting, removing and replacing floppies while running

To insert a floppy without restarting:

  1. Enable MAME’s UI controls and open Tab → File Manager.
  2. Select the floppy drive.
  3. Browse to the image and select it.
  4. Choose the access mode when prompted.
  5. Return to the terminal and use ASOCIAR and MONTAR as shown above.

The available access modes include:

Mode Effect
Read-only Protects the selected image from writes.
Read-write Saves writes directly into the selected image.
Read this image, write to another image Uses a separate output image, preserving the selected source.

Floppy writes do not use the hard-disk difference directory. Use read-only access or a separate output image when preserving a floppy master.

Before ejecting, finish applications using the floppy and release its association:

ASOCIAR A

Then open File Manager, select the floppy drive and choose [empty slot]. In a multiuser session, first ensure that other users have also finished accessing the disk.

To insert a replacement, select its image through the same menu, then enter:

ASOCIAR A FLOPPY2
MONTAR A

MONTAR tells S.O. Multi-Usuario that the medium has changed. It does not insert an image into MAME or format a blank disk.

Creating and formatting a new floppy

In File Manager, select the floppy drive and choose [create]. Specify a new filename ending in .mfi, select MAME’s MFI format, and choose the unformatted 5¼-inch double-sided high-density medium if offered.

The new image represents a blank disk. Return to S.O. Multi-Usuario and enter:

ASOCIAR A FLOPPY2
INICIAR A (FORMAT CARA 2)

FORMAT requests physical formatting; CARA 2 selects two sides.

Formatting erases the floppy associated with A. Confirm that the intended new image is inserted before answering the utility’s confirmation prompts.

Supply a disk label when requested and let formatting finish. Then check the result:

MONTAR A
VERFORM A
LISDIR *.*:A

This uses the tested native format. The emulated drive selection does not establish the manufacturer or exact model of the original physical drive.

Trying another hard-disk image

Close MAME before changing the system hard disk. To select a compatible CHD directly:

mame secoinsa20 -harddisk "images/alternative.chd" -window -diff_directory "work/alternative/diff" -noautosave

This replaces the somu62 selection for that run.

The alternative image must contain a disk layout and boot software compatible with the emulated machine. A .chd extension alone does not establish compatibility.

Use a working copy of an unfamiliar image. Directly attached writable images may be modified in place; selecting a difference directory does not force every image to use an overlay. The software-list somu62 procedure described above provides the defined master-and-difference arrangement.

Capturing serial printer output

Attach the serial printer and select a new output filename:

mame secoinsa20 somu62 -window -printerport printer -printout "output.prn"

Inside S.O. Multi-Usuario:

ASOCIAR IMPR1 IMP
LISTAR SISTEMA.NOMBDISP:S (PRINT1 NOHEAD)

The output file contains the printer’s received bytes, including control characters. It is not a rendered PDF. Use a new filename to preserve earlier output.

Ending a session

Finish running applications and release removable media before exiting. Enter:

FIN

FIN logs out of S.O. Multi-Usuario; it does not close MAME. After any other users have finished, enable MAME’s UI controls and exit normally. This allows pending image writes to be saved.

At the time of writing of this document the following emulation characteristics are pending: Tape operation is not implemented. Floppy booting is not established. The emulated terminal does not yet fully reproduce the original Secoinsa IS 9502 terminal firmware functionality.
